**1,000 questions in 20 graded sets**

Very Easy  ·  Easy  ·  Moderate  ·  Tough  ·  Critical  ·  Advanced

**Prepared by Prof. Pankaj Sharma**

Free practice material for students  ·  October 2026

# **How to use this booklet**

Dear student, this booklet gives you 1,000 questions on Unit I of Paper 1, arranged in 20 sets of 50. The sets become harder as you go: Sets 1 to 3 are Very Easy, 4 to 6 Easy, 7 to 10 Moderate, 11 to 14 Tough, 15 to 17 Critical and 18 to 20 Advanced. Work through them in order. The early sets build the facts that the later sets expect you to use.

## **How to take a set**

**1.** Sit down with one set, a sheet of paper and a watch. Give yourself 60 minutes and do not consult notes, books or a phone.

**2.** Write your answers (A, B, C or D) on the sheet. Attempt every question; there is no negative marking in the examination.

**3.** When the time is over, turn to the Answer Key at the end of the set and mark yourself. Enter your score in the record on the next page.

**4.** Read the explanation for every question you got wrong and for every question you guessed. From Set 11 onward each explanation also has a line beginning 'Why not', which tells you why the most tempting wrong option is wrong. Read that line even when your answer was right.

**5.** If you score below 30 in a set, go back to the chapter, revise the weak topics and repeat the same set after a week before moving on. If you score 40 or more, go on to the next set.

## **What the question formats mean**

You will meet direct questions, statement-based questions ('1 and 3 only'), two-statement questions, Assertion and Reason questions, match-the-list questions and sequencing questions. These are the formats used in the examination. In an Assertion and Reason question, first decide whether each statement is true, and only then ask whether the Reason answers the question 'why?' for the Assertion.

## **A note on facts and dates**

Every question in this booklet has been written afresh for practice; none is reproduced from an examination paper. Facts that change with time are stated as they stood in October 2026. Where an older figure is still found in books, the explanation mentions it, so that you recognise both. Two questions quote the Ministry of Education's note of March 2026 on PM e-Vidya for the number of SWAYAM PRABHA channels and of SWAYAM National Coordinators. Check such figures again close to your examination.

(D)	both school and college education

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 1**

**Q1 — (A),** because in CCE, 'continuous' refers to regular assessment throughout the year and 'comprehensive' to coverage of both scholastic areas (academic subjects) and co-scholastic areas such as life skills, attitudes, values, arts and sports. The scheme sought to reduce dependence on a single year-end examination.

**Q2 — (C),** because William Heard Kilpatrick set out the project method in 1918, building on Dewey's idea of learning by doing. Its steps are purposing, planning, executing and evaluating. Montessori and Froebel are known for early childhood methods, and Parkhurst for the Dalton Plan.

**Q3 — (A),** because the understanding level goes beyond memorising to seeing meaning: how facts relate to one another and to general principles, so that the learner can explain and apply them in familiar situations. Rote recall belongs to the memory level and independent problem-solving to the reflective level.

**Q4 — (C),** because a tutorial brings together a small group of students with a tutor who clears doubts, checks understanding and gives personal feedback. It supplements lectures. A lecture addresses a large group, while a symposium and a conference involve formal presentations by several speakers.

**Q5 — (C),** because micro-teaching was developed at Stanford University in 1963 by Dwight W. Allen and his colleagues as a way of giving trainee teachers controlled practice in single teaching skills with immediate feedback. It was later adopted in Indian teacher education in a modified form.

**Q6 — (D),** because the revised taxonomy uses verbs and runs Remember, Understand, Apply, Analyse, Evaluate, Create. Create, which means putting elements together to form something new, sits at the top. In the original 1956 version the highest level was Evaluation, with Synthesis just below it.

**Q7 — (B),** because a norm-referenced test reports where a student stands relative to a group, using ranks or percentiles; competitive selection tests work this way. A criterion-referenced test compares performance with a fixed standard, regardless of how other students perform.

**Q8 — (A),** because Vygotsky defined the Zone of Proximal Development as the gap between what a learner can do alone and what the learner can do with help from a teacher or a more capable peer. Teaching is most effective when it is aimed at this zone and gives suitable support.

**Q9 — (D),** because reliability is the consistency or stability of test scores. If the same students get similar scores on repeated administration, the test is reliable. Validity is a different quality: it asks whether the test measures what it claims to measure. A good test needs both.

**Q10 — (D),** because Malcolm Knowles popularised andragogy as the art and science of helping adults learn. He described adult learners as self-directed, rich in experience, ready to learn what their life roles require, problem-centred and internally motivated. Pedagogy, by contrast, refers to the teaching of children.

**Q11 — (B),** because in a lecture the teacher does most of the talking and controls content and pace, while learners mainly listen and take notes. It is therefore teacher-centred. It is economical for large classes and useful for introducing topics, but it gives little room for learner activity.

**Q12 — (B),** because Krathwohl, with Bloom and Masia, published the affective domain taxonomy in 1964, with levels running from Receiving to Characterisation. The affective domain concerns attitudes, values and feelings. Simpson and Dave are associated with the psychomotor domain, and Gagné with the conditions of learning.

**Q13 — (A),** because reflective practice means looking back on one's own teaching, asking what worked and what did not, and changing future lessons accordingly. Subject mastery and verbal fluency are also desirable qualities, but they describe what a teacher knows or how she speaks, not self-examination.

**Q14 — (C),** because a diagnostic test identifies exactly where and why a learner is going wrong, so that remedial teaching can be planned. A prognostic test predicts future success, an aptitude test measures potential for a particular field, and an intelligence test measures general mental ability.

**Q15 — (B),** because as class size grows, the teacher has less time for each student, so individual guidance, interaction and feedback suffer. A lecture, an announcement or a common written test can still be given to a large group, which is why the lecture dominates large classes.

**Q16 — (A),** because VARK, developed by Neil Fleming, stands for Visual, Auditory (or Aural), Read/Write and Kinaesthetic. Visual learners prefer diagrams, charts and maps. The practical message for teachers is to present material in varied ways, since a class contains learners with different preferences.

**Q17 — (D),** because brainstorming, introduced by Alex Osborn, asks a group to produce as many ideas as possible on a problem without criticism at first; evaluation comes later. It encourages creative thinking and participation. It is not a technique for memorising, testing or revising content.

**Q18 — (B),** because Howard Gardner, in Frames of Mind (1983), argued that intelligence is not a single ability but several relatively independent ones, such as linguistic, logical-mathematical, spatial, musical, bodily-kinaesthetic, interpersonal and intrapersonal. Spearman proposed a general factor, Binet built the first intelligence test, and Cattell distinguished fluid from crystallised intelligence.

**Q19 — (C),** because indoctrination implants beliefs as unquestionable and discourages critical examination, which is why it is regarded as anti-educational. Instruction delivers structured content, training builds specific skills through practice, and tutoring gives individual help; all three leave room for understanding and questioning.

**Q20 — (B),** because learning improves when students feel safe to ask, answer and make mistakes. A positive climate shows mutual respect, warmth, open communication and shared purpose. Fear, humiliating comparison and lack of interaction raise anxiety and reduce participation, so they harm learning.

**Q21 — (A),** because Edgar Dale (1946) arranged learning experiences in a cone, with direct purposeful experience at the broad base and verbal symbols at the narrow top. The cone reminds teachers that concrete experiences support abstract ideas, and it guides the choice of teaching aids.

**Q22 — (B),** because handling apparatus is a physical skill that depends on coordination between mind and muscle, so it belongs to the psychomotor domain. The cognitive domain concerns thinking, the affective domain concerns attitudes and values, and conation refers to will or striving, not to manual skill.

**Q23 — (A),** because prior knowledge belongs to the learner and strongly affects how new material is received. Subject mastery is a teacher-related factor, the library is an institutional facility, and seating layout is part of the learning environment. All four affect teaching, but only prior knowledge is located in the learner.

**Q24 — (D),** because Bloom's cognitive domain covers knowing and thinking, from simple recall to complex judgement. Attitudes, feelings and values belong to the affective domain, and physical or motor skills belong to the psychomotor domain. Together the three domains are used to classify educational objectives.

**Q25 — (B),** because no two learners are alike. They vary in intelligence, aptitude, interests, motivation, background and the speed at which they learn. Recognising individual differences is the reason teachers vary their methods, examples and pace instead of treating the class as a uniform group.

**Q26 — (D),** because micro-teaching is a scaled-down teaching encounter used in teacher training. The trainee teaches a short lesson of about five to ten minutes to a small group, practises one skill at a time, receives feedback and then re-teaches. It has nothing to do with the age of the pupils.

**Q27 — (A),** because Erikson placed identity versus role confusion in adolescence, when young people ask who they are and what they will become. Trust versus mistrust belongs to infancy, industry versus inferiority to the school years, and integrity versus despair to old age. Many undergraduates are still working through this stage.

**Q28 — (B),** because the flipped classroom reverses the usual order. Students study new material on their own before class, usually through recorded videos or readings, and class time is then used for discussion, problem-solving and application with the teacher's help.

**Q29 — (B),** because formative evaluation takes place while teaching is in progress, through quizzes, questions, assignments and observation. Its purpose is to improve learning and teaching by giving feedback, not to award final grades. Summative evaluation comes at the end, and placement evaluation before instruction begins.

**Q30 — (C),** because intrinsic motivation comes from within: the learner studies because the task itself is interesting or satisfying. Extrinsic motivation depends on outside factors such as marks, prizes, praise or fear of punishment. Intrinsic motivation generally leads to deeper and longer-lasting learning.

**Q31 — (B),** because teaching is a purposeful, planned system of actions whose aim is to produce learning in another person. It is not accidental, and it is not defined by finishing the syllabus or keeping order. Those may accompany teaching, but the intention to promote learning is what makes an activity teaching.

**Q32 — (D),** because teaching is described at three levels in rising order: memory, understanding and reflective. The reflective level is the highest because learners examine a problem critically and reach their own conclusions. It builds on, and therefore includes, the memory and understanding levels.

**Q33 — (D),** because in a panel discussion a small group of experts converse with one another on a topic in front of an audience, and a moderator steers the exchange. A tutorial is small-group guidance, a workshop is hands-on practice, and a demonstration shows how something is done.

**Q34 — (B),** because SWAYAM PRABHA is a group of direct-to-home television channels that telecast educational programmes round the clock, so learners without internet access can still benefit. It should not be confused with SWAYAM, which is the web platform for interactive online courses.

**Q35 — (D),** because measurement only quantifies, for example 72 marks out of 100, and testing and scoring are ways of obtaining that number. Evaluation goes further and judges the worth of the performance against objectives or standards, so it is the broadest of these terms.

**Q36 — (C),** because Jackson divided teaching into three phases. The pre-active phase is the planning stage: fixing objectives, selecting content and choosing methods before entering the classroom. The interactive phase is the actual classroom encounter, and the post-active phase covers evaluation and reflection after the lesson.

**Q37 — (C),** because a rubric lists the criteria on which work will be judged and describes what performance looks like at each level, for example excellent, good, fair and poor. It makes marking transparent and consistent and tells students in advance what is expected.

**Q38 — (D),** because the Choice Based Credit System, set out in UGC guidelines of 2015, lets students choose from core, elective and ability enhancement courses. Each course carries credits, performance is reported in letter grades and grade points, and overall achievement is expressed as a CGPA.

**Q39 — (C),** because the chalkboard is the oldest and most widely used classroom aid and needs no electricity or digital device. Smart boards, webinar tools and learning apps are ICT-based supports. Teaching support systems are usually grouped as traditional, modern (audio-visual) and ICT-based.

**Q40 — (A),** because Piaget described four stages of cognitive development: sensorimotor, pre-operational, concrete operational and formal operational. Abstract reasoning appears in the formal operational stage, from about eleven or twelve years. The other three options name theories associated with different psychologists.

**Q41 — (D),** because SWAYAM (Study Webs of Active-Learning for Young Aspiring Minds) is the Ministry of Education's platform offering free online courses from school to postgraduate level. Doctoral theses are stored on Shodhganga, plagiarism detection is supported through ShodhShuddhi, and rankings are produced under NIRF.

**Q42 — (C),** because Skinner showed that behaviour is shaped by its consequences: responses followed by reinforcement tend to be repeated. This is operant conditioning. Pavlov is known for classical conditioning, Köhler for insight learning and Bandura for observational learning. Programmed instruction is a classroom application of Skinner's ideas.

**Q43 — (B),** because Rosenthal and Jacobson (1968) showed that pupils whom teachers expected to bloom actually gained more, because the teachers unknowingly treated them differently. The Pygmalion effect is thus a self-fulfilling prophecy: high expectations tend to raise performance and low expectations tend to depress it.

**Q44 — (A),** because Johann Friedrich Herbart is the chief exponent of memory level teaching, which presents facts systematically for retention and recall. Morrison is linked with the understanding level and Hunt with the reflective level. Bloom is known for the taxonomy of objectives, not for a level of teaching.

**Q45 — (A),** because John Dewey, the leading voice of progressive education, held that learners learn best through active experience and reflection on it, not by passively receiving information. His ideas underlie activity-based approaches such as the project method and the problem-solving method.

**Q46 — (D),** because in learner-centred teaching the students are active: they discuss, inquire and solve problems, while the teacher arranges the conditions, guides and supports. The teacher as transmitter of information is typical of the teacher-centred approach, where learners are comparatively passive.

**Q47 — (C),** because the original cognitive hierarchy runs Knowledge, Comprehension, Application, Analysis, Synthesis, Evaluation. Knowledge, meaning recall of facts, terms and methods, is the base on which the higher levels are built. In the 2001 revision the same lowest level is named Remember.

**Q48 — (A),** because memory level teaching concentrates on committing facts to memory and reproducing them, with the teacher as active presenter and the learner as a largely passive receiver. It is the lowest of the three levels, though it supplies the factual base needed for understanding and reflection.

**Q49 — (C),** because a portfolio gathers samples of a student's work, such as drafts, projects and reflections, over a period, so that growth and process can be judged along with final products. It is one of the innovations in evaluation that supplement conventional written examinations.

**Q50 — (A),** because in the standard analysis of teaching, the teacher is the independent variable who plans and directs, and the learner is the dependent variable whose behaviour is expected to change. Content, methods and strategies act as intervening variables that link the two.

# **Set 2**

**Level:** Very Easy     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

**1.**	The lowest level of the affective domain is:

(A) Responding	(B) Valuing

(C) Organisation	(D) Receiving

**2.**	In teaching, the term 'scaffolding' means:

(A)	temporary support given to a learner

(B)	extra homework given to a learner

(C)	a fixed syllabus given to a learner

(D)	a final grade given to a learner

**3.**	A test whose scores are unaffected by the personal judgement of the examiner is said to have:

(A) objectivity	(B) validity

(C) difficulty	(D) variability

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 2**

**Q1 — (D),** because Krathwohl's affective levels are Receiving, Responding, Valuing, Organisation and Characterisation. Receiving means being willing to attend to a stimulus, such as listening to a talk. The learner then responds, comes to value, organises values into a system and finally lives by them.

**Q2 — (A),** because scaffolding is the help a teacher or capable peer provides so that a learner can do a task just beyond present ability: hints, prompts, models and questions. Like a builder's scaffold, it is removed gradually as the learner becomes able to work alone.

**Q3 — (A),** because objectivity means that different examiners, or the same examiner at different times, give the same score to the same answer. Multiple-choice tests are highly objective; essay tests are less so. Objectivity supports reliability, because scorer bias is one source of inconsistent marks.

**Q4 — (B),** because the National Programme on Technology Enhanced Learning was started in 2003 by seven Indian Institutes of Technology and the Indian Institute of Science, with government funding. It offers free online courses, chiefly in engineering, science, management and humanities, with optional proctored examinations for certificates.

**Q5 — (B),** because gifted learners perform, or can perform, far above their age group in intellectual, creative or other fields. They need enrichment and more challenging work; otherwise they may become bored. Slow learners, by contrast, need more time, repetition and concrete examples.

**Q6 — (D),** because measurement assigns numbers to performance according to rules, such as marks or scores, and so answers the question 'how much?'. It does not say whether the performance is good or bad. That judgement, which may also use qualitative information, belongs to evaluation.

**Q7 — (B),** because factors affecting teaching are grouped under teacher, learner, support material, instructional facilities, learning environment and institution. Communication skill, like subject mastery and personality, belongs to the teacher. Home background is an example of a learner factor, and the timetable of an institutional one.

**Q8 — (C),** because Henry C. Morrison developed the model of teaching at the understanding level, with five steps: exploration, presentation, assimilation, organisation and recitation. His aim was mastery of each unit of content. Thorndike and Skinner are learning theorists, and Dale proposed the Cone of Experience.

**Q9 — (A),** because blended learning is a planned mix of classroom teaching and online activities, so that each mode does what it does best. Learners get flexibility of time and pace online and interaction in class. It is also called hybrid learning.

**Q10 — (C),** because in a seminar a student or scholar presents a prepared paper on a topic, and the group then discusses and questions it under a chairperson. It develops skills of study, presentation and argument. Supervised practice of skills describes a workshop.

**Q11 — (D),** because an instructional objective describes what the learner will be able to do after instruction, in terms that can be observed and assessed, such as define, explain or solve. Statements about what the teacher will do, or lists of topics, do not say what learning is expected.

**Q12 — (A),** because micro-teaching breaks the complex act of teaching into component skills, such as questioning, explaining or reinforcement. The trainee concentrates on one skill in a short lesson, gets feedback and practises again. Mastered skills are later combined in normal classroom teaching.

**Q13 — (D),** because learning is defined as a relatively permanent change in behaviour, and teaching exists to produce such change in knowledge, skills and attitudes. Covering the syllabus, securing marks and keeping order are at best means or by-products; none of them is the purpose of teaching.

**Q14 — (C),** because good teaching is deliberate: it has clear objectives and an organised plan for reaching them. It also encourages interaction, respects differences among learners and welcomes questions. The three wrong options describe features of poor, one-way teaching.

**Q15 — (A),** because the three levels form a hierarchy. Memory level teaching supplies facts, understanding level teaching shows their meaning and relationships, and reflective level teaching uses both to investigate problems. Each higher level presupposes the lower ones, so the order cannot be changed.

**Q16 — (C),** because Piaget's stages run sensorimotor (birth to about two years), pre-operational (two to seven), concrete operational (seven to eleven) and formal operational (eleven onwards). In the sensorimotor stage the infant knows the world through senses and actions.

**Q17 — (B),** because summative evaluation sums up achievement after a unit, term or course is over, usually through a final examination. Its results are used for grading, promotion and certification. Evaluation during lessons is formative, and evaluation before instruction is placement or diagnostic.

**Q18 — (A),** because Maslow arranged human needs in a hierarchy: physiological, safety, love and belonging, esteem and self-actualisation. Lower needs must be reasonably satisfied before higher ones motivate behaviour, which is why a hungry or frightened student finds it hard to concentrate on studies.

**Q19 — (C),** because SWAYAM was launched by the Ministry of Education (then the Ministry of Human Resource Development), Government of India, to take quality courses to all learners free of cost. Courses are prepared by national coordinators such as NPTEL, UGC, CEC, IGNOU, NCERT and NIOS.

**Q20 — (B),** because objective-type items have one predetermined correct answer, so every examiner awards the same mark. They can sample a wide syllabus in a short time and are quick to score. Essay-type items require extended written answers, and their marking depends on the examiner's judgement.

**Q21 — (C),** because learners differ because of the interaction of what they inherit and what they experience: family, culture, schooling, health and opportunity. Neither heredity nor environment alone explains the differences in ability, interest and personality that a teacher meets in every class.

**Q22 — (D),** because in an open-book examination candidates may refer to textbooks or notes while answering. Since facts can be looked up, good open-book questions test understanding, application and analysis instead of memory. It is counted among the innovations in evaluation.

**Q23 — (D),** because placement evaluation is done before instruction to find out what learners already know and where they should begin, for example beginner, intermediate or advanced groups in a language course. Summative and terminal tests come at the end of instruction.

**Q24 — (A),** because in a computer-based test, questions appear on a computer screen and candidates mark answers with a mouse or keyboard at a test centre. Responses are stored and scored electronically, which speeds up results. Many national examinations conducted by the National Testing Agency use this mode.

**Q25 — (B),** because in the project method students carry out a purposeful, real-life task from planning to completion, so they learn through their own activity. It reflects Dewey's pragmatism. Rote learning and passive listening are precisely what the method was designed to replace.

**Q26 — (B),** because Bruner held that learners understand and remember best what they find out for themselves, so teaching should arrange situations in which they discover principles. Ausubel defended meaningful reception learning, Gagné described conditions of learning, and Bandura studied observational learning.

**Q27 — (A),** because the revised taxonomy turned the nouns of the original into verbs. Knowledge became Remember and Comprehension became Understand, while Application, Analysis and Evaluation became Apply, Analyse and Evaluate. Recalling and recognising are processes included under Remember, not names of the level itself.

**Q28 — (A),** because Pavlov showed that a dog would salivate at the sound of a bell after the bell had been repeatedly paired with food. A neutral stimulus thus comes to produce a response. Skinner is associated with operant conditioning and Thorndike with trial-and-error learning.

**Q29 — (C),** because validity is the most important quality of a test: it concerns whether the test truly measures the ability or content it claims to measure. A test of reasoning that mainly rewards fast reading is not valid, however consistent or easy to score it may be.

**Q30 — (A),** because Spearman's two-factor theory holds that every intellectual task draws on a general factor (g) common to all tasks and a specific factor (s) peculiar to that task. Gardner, Sternberg and Guilford each rejected the idea of intelligence as one general ability.

**Q31 — (D),** because maxims of teaching are working rules drawn from experience. The accepted ones include proceeding from simple to complex, from known to unknown, from concrete to abstract and from particular to general. The other three options reverse accepted maxims and would make learning harder.

**Q32 — (D),** because the Semester Grade Point Average measures a student's performance in one semester. It is obtained by dividing the total credit points earned in the semester by the total credits of the courses taken. The CGPA does the same for all semesters together.

**Q33 — (C),** because training aims at proficiency in a particular skill and relies on practice and drill, as in typing or laboratory technique. Instruction imparts knowledge in a structured way, indoctrination implants beliefs without questioning, and counselling helps a person deal with personal or academic problems.

**Q34 — (B),** because in a demonstration the teacher shows how something is done, step by step, while explaining it, as in a laboratory experiment or the use of an instrument. Learners see the process before trying it themselves, so the method suits practical skills and procedures.

**Q35 — (D),** because the word pedagogy comes from Greek roots meaning 'leading the child', and it refers to the teaching of children, in which the teacher directs what and how pupils learn. The corresponding term for helping adults learn is andragogy.

**Q36 — (C),** because reflective level teaching is problem-centred. The teacher creates a genuinely problematic situation, and learners examine it critically, form and test hypotheses and reach their own conclusions. Memorising lists and reciting passages belong to the memory level of teaching.

**Q37 — (B),** because cooperative learning places students in small mixed groups where each member is responsible for the group's success as well as for his or her own learning. It develops both academic and social skills. Individual competition and silent solitary work are its opposites.

**Q38 — (C),** because institution-related factors include the academic calendar, timetable, workload, class size, rules and administrative support. They set the limits within which a teacher plans. A teacher can choose methods and examples, but cannot usually change the time and structure fixed by the institution.

**Q39 — (D),** because the original order is Knowledge, Comprehension, Application, Analysis, Synthesis and Evaluation. Comprehension, the second level, means grasping the meaning of material, for example by explaining or summarising it in one's own words. Each level is assumed to build on the ones below it.

**Q40 — (B),** because ICT covers the digital tools used to create, store, send and receive information: computers, the internet, projectors, mobile devices and software. ICT-based teaching support includes presentations, learning management systems, online courses and digital libraries.

**Q41 — (C),** because a criterion-referenced test asks whether the learner has reached a pre-set standard, for example 'can solve eight of ten problems'. How other students perform is irrelevant. A driving test is a common example. Norm-referenced tests, by contrast, compare students with one another.

**Q42 — (A),** because in role play learners take the parts of people in a real or imagined situation and act it out without a script, after which the class discusses what happened. It builds empathy, communication and social skills by letting learners see a situation from another person's position.

**Q43 — (B),** because under the CBCS, core courses are compulsory for all students of a programme. Elective courses are chosen from a pool, either within the discipline or from another discipline, and ability enhancement courses build general skills. Each course carries a stated number of credits.

**Q44 — (C),** because G. Stanley Hall described adolescence as a period of 'storm and stress' because of rapid physical growth, emotional ups and downs, the search for identity and conflict with authority. Teachers of undergraduates need patience and a supportive classroom for this reason.

**Q45 — (A),** because memory level teaching is teacher-dominated. The teacher presents facts in an organised way and the learner receives, retains and reproduces them. Active inquiry and independent criticism are features of the reflective level, where the learner takes the leading part in the lesson.

**Q46 — (D),** because among the functions of teaching are transmitting knowledge, developing skills, inculcating values and socialising the learner. Socialisation means preparing learners to live and work with others according to the norms of their society and culture. The other options name outcomes of the system, not this function.

**Q47 — (B),** because teaching is a communication process, so the teacher's message must reach learners clearly. Subject knowledge, planning and understanding of learners are the other basic requirements. Costly equipment is helpful but not essential, and unbroken silence or lecturing shows absence of interaction.

**Q48 — (D),** because the learning environment has a physical side (space, light, air, furniture, noise) and a psychological side (relationships, warmth, freedom from fear). Both affect attention and comfort. Poor lighting or overcrowding can defeat even a well-planned lesson.

**Q49 — (C),** because the overhead projector throws an enlarged image of a transparent sheet on to a screen behind the teacher, who can face the class while writing or pointing. It is a projected visual aid, now largely replaced by the LCD projector connected to a computer.

**Q50 — (D),** because programmed instruction applies operant conditioning: content is broken into small steps called frames, the learner responds actively at each step and gets immediate confirmation, which acts as reinforcement. The learner moves at his or her own pace through the programme.

(B)	covers much content for many learners quickly

(C)	ensures dialogue with each learner

(D)	adjusts itself to each learner's pace

**38.**	The maxim 'from concrete to abstract' means that a teacher should begin with:

(A) real objects and examples	(B) definitions and formulae

(C) rules and principles	(D) theories and generalisations

**39.**	In the revised Bloom's taxonomy, the names of the cognitive levels are expressed as:

(A) nouns	(B) adjectives

(C) verbs	(D) numbers

**40.**	To cater to individual differences in a class, a teacher should:

(A)	vary the methods and pace of teaching

(B)	use one fixed method for all lessons

(C)	attend only to the brightest students

(D)	set one common task for every student

**41.**	'Teaching is a form of interpersonal influence aimed at changing the behaviour potential of another person.' This definition was given by:

(A) B. F. Skinner	(B) Jean Piaget

(C) Edgar Dale	(D) N. L. Gage

**42.**	The idea of 'advance organisers' was given by:

(A) Jerome Bruner	(B) Robert Gagné

(C) David Ausubel	(D) Albert Bandura

**43.**	A test that covers the whole syllabus in proper proportion is said to have good:

(A) predictive validity	(B) scorer objectivity

(C) retest reliability	(D) content validity

**44.**	During the idea-generation stage of brainstorming, criticism of ideas is:

(A) encouraged from the start	(B) made compulsory for all

(C) postponed until later	(D) left to the leader alone

**45.**	In a university, the syllabus of a subject is usually framed by the:

(A) Finance Committee	(B) Building Committee

(C) Selection Committee	(D) Board of Studies

**46.**	Which of the following is a learner-centred method?

(A) Formal lecture	(B) Group discussion

(C) Dictation of notes	(D) Teacher demonstration

**47.**	A micro-teaching lesson usually lasts about:

(A) 5 to 10 minutes	(B) 20 to 30 minutes

(C) 40 to 45 minutes	(D) 60 to 90 minutes

**48.**	According to Piaget, abstract and hypothetical thinking develops in the:

(A) formal operational stage	(B) sensorimotor stage

(C) pre-operational stage	(D) concrete operational stage

**49.**	Which of the following learner-related factors is affective (emotional) in nature?

(C) Previous knowledge	(D) Speed of reading

**50.**	The first step in the problem-solving method is:

(A) testing the hypothesis	(B) drawing the conclusion

| 31  **D** | 32  **A** | 33  **A** | 34  **D** | 35  **A** | 36  **D** | 37  **B** | 38  **A** | 39  **C** | 40  **A** |

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 3**

**Q1 — (D),** because a percentile rank tells what percentage of the group scored below a given student, so it expresses position relative to others. That is the logic of norm-referenced testing. Criterion-referenced and mastery tests report whether a fixed standard has been reached.

**Q2 — (C),** because at the memory level the teacher decides and presents everything and the learner only receives. Teacher control lessens at the understanding level, where learners take part more actively, and is least at the reflective level, where the teacher guides while learners investigate.

**Q3 — (D),** because a MOOC is designed for unlimited participation: thousands of learners anywhere can enrol in the same course through the internet. 'Open' means that anyone may join, usually without fee or entry qualification. Most MOOCs run for a few weeks.

**Q4 — (B),** because the Intelligence Quotient was originally calculated as mental age divided by chronological age, multiplied by 100, an idea suggested by William Stern and used by Terman. An IQ of 100 is average. Modern tests report a deviation IQ based on comparison with one's age group.

**Q5 — (C),** because a Learning Management System is software for delivering courses online and managing them: uploading material, collecting assignments, running quizzes and forums, and recording grades. Moodle, Google Classroom, Canvas and Blackboard are familiar examples used by colleges and universities.

**Q6 — (A),** because memory level teaching relies on telling, drill, repetition, recitation and review so that facts are fixed and can be reproduced. Debate, projects and seminars demand understanding and independent thinking, so they belong to the higher levels of teaching.

**Q7 — (B),** because measurement only assigns numbers. Assessment gathers and interprets information, numerical and descriptive, about learning. Evaluation goes furthest by passing a judgement of worth on the basis of that information. Each term therefore includes the one before it.

**Q8 — (B),** because understanding shows itself when a learner can restate an idea, give examples, see relationships and apply a principle in a familiar situation. Exact repetition is a memory level outcome, and independent discovery of new knowledge is the aim of the reflective level.

**Q9 — (C),** because good teaching is interactive. The teacher explains and asks, learners respond and question, and the teacher adjusts the lesson in the light of this feedback. One-way talk gives the teacher no means of knowing whether the class has understood.

**Q10 — (B),** because in Jackson's analysis, the interactive phase covers everything that happens from the teacher's entry into the class until the lesson ends: presenting, questioning, responding and reinforcing. Planning before the class is the pre-active phase, and evaluation afterwards is the post-active phase.

**Q11 — (C),** because verbs such as define, list, name, state and recall ask the learner only to retrieve information from memory, so they belong to Remember, the lowest level. Apply uses verbs like solve and use, Analyse uses compare and differentiate, and Create uses design and compose.

**Q12 — (D),** because new learning has to be linked with what learners already know. Their previous knowledge, also called entry behaviour, tells the teacher where to begin and what examples to use. A lesson pitched above or below this level will not succeed.

**Q13 — (A),** because a workshop is a working session in which participants learn by doing: they practise a skill or produce something, such as test items or a research proposal, with guidance from resource persons. It emphasises the psychomotor and applied side of learning.

**Q14 — (C),** because Robert F. Mager's book (1962) showed teachers how to write objectives in behavioural terms. He asked that an objective state the performance expected, the conditions under which it will occur and the criterion of acceptable performance. Bloom is known for the taxonomy, Tyler for curriculum principles.

**Q15 — (C),** because conditioning builds a fixed connection between a stimulus and a response through repeated pairing or reinforcement, as shown by Pavlov and Skinner. It does not call for understanding or reasoning, which is why it is placed at the lowest end of the continuum that leads up to teaching.

**Q16 — (B),** because the case method was developed at Harvard, first in law and then, from the 1920s, at Harvard Business School, where students analyse real business situations and propose decisions. It is now widely used in management, law, medicine and teacher education.

**Q17 — (B),** because a class quiz is given while a topic is being taught, and its purpose is to show teacher and students what has been learnt and what needs more work. Year-end examinations and the degree viva are summative, and an entrance examination is used for selection.

**Q18 — (A),** because the model of reflective level teaching is associated with Hunt, and reflective teaching is also discussed by Bigge. It is problem-centred and aims at critical, independent thinking. Herbart is the name linked with the memory level; Pavlov and Thorndike are learning theorists.

**Q19 — (A),** because for Vygotsky, learning is first social and then individual: children learn through interaction with adults and more capable peers, using language as the chief tool. His view is therefore called social constructivism. Reinforcement and trial and error belong to behaviourist theories.

**Q20 — (D),** because the University Grants Commission issued guidelines for the Choice Based Credit System in 2015, asking universities to adopt a common credit and grading pattern. NCERT deals with school education, NCTE with teacher education, and NAAC with assessment and accreditation of institutions.

**Q21 — (D),** because self-actualisation, the need to realise one's full potential, stands at the top of Maslow's hierarchy, above physiological, safety, belonging and esteem needs. In education it corresponds to learning for the sake of growth and creativity, not for reward.

**Q22 — (B),** because enthusiasm is infectious. A teacher who is warm, approachable and visibly interested in the subject arouses interest and effort in learners and builds trust. Personality is thus a teacher-related factor that affects learning, along with subject mastery and communication skill.

**Q23 — (B),** because the affective levels rise from Receiving through Responding, Valuing and Organisation to Characterisation by a value or value complex. At this highest level the person's values have become a consistent philosophy of life that governs behaviour in all situations.

**Q24 — (C),** because slow learners can learn, but they need a slower pace, simple language, concrete examples, more repetition and frequent encouragement. Peer tutoring also helps. Speeding up, or withholding feedback, would widen the gap between them and the rest of the class.

**Q25 — (B),** because teaching aids are classified by the sense they address. Audio aids, such as radio, tape recorder and podcast, are heard. Charts, models and globes are visual aids. Television and films, which are both seen and heard, are audio-visual aids.

**Q26 — (A),** because attention is the gateway to learning, and noise competes for it. Noise is treated as a physical barrier in the learning environment. It does not alter intelligence or aptitude, which are relatively stable characteristics of the learner.

**Q27 — (A),** because in peer assessment students give feedback or marks to their classmates' work, usually with a rubric. It makes them think about what good work looks like. In self-assessment a student judges his or her own work; both are regarded as innovations in evaluation.

**Q28 — (D),** because Thorndike's law of effect states that a response followed by a satisfying result is strengthened, and one followed by an annoying result is weakened. With the laws of readiness and exercise, it forms his theory of trial-and-error learning, also called connectionism.

**Q29 — (C),** because according to Knowles, adults want to know why they are learning something and wish to apply it at once to their work or life. They therefore prefer learning organised around real problems. Learning organised around subjects for later use is typical of school pedagogy.

**Q30 — (C),** because because responses are recorded electronically, a computer-based test can be scored almost at once and without human error in marking. It still needs test centres, identity checks and reliable equipment; technical failure and unequal familiarity with computers are its known limitations.

**Q31 — (D),** because essay-type items let students select, organise and express ideas in their own words, so they can test higher abilities such as analysis and evaluation. Their weaknesses are limited sampling of content and subjective scoring. The other three are objective-type items.

**Q32 — (A),** because an LCD (or LED) projector connected to a computer displays slides, videos and web pages on a screen for the whole class. A printer produces paper copies, a scanner converts paper documents to digital form, and a web camera captures video.

**Q33 — (A),** because adolescents seek acceptance from friends of their own age, and peer norms strongly shape their dress, speech, interests and attitude to study. Teachers can use this constructively through group work and peer learning in the undergraduate classroom.

**Q34 — (D),** because Synthesis is the fifth level of the original taxonomy. It involves combining elements into a new pattern or structure, as in writing an essay or designing a plan. Analysis is the opposite process, breaking a whole into its parts to see how they are related.

**Q35 — (A),** because because students have already gone through the content at home, class time is free for activities that need the teacher and peers: clearing doubts, discussion, solving problems and applying ideas. The lecture, in recorded form, has moved out of the classroom.

**Q36 — (D),** because a good test has validity, reliability, objectivity and usability. Usability, or practicability, means that the test can be given, scored and interpreted without undue time, cost or difficulty. A highly valid test is of little use if it is too costly to administer.

**Q37 — (B),** because one teacher can present a large amount of organised material to a large class in a short time, which makes the lecture economical. Its weaknesses are the opposite of the other options: little activity, little interaction and no adjustment to individual pace.

**Q38 — (A),** because learners grasp ideas more easily when they first meet things they can see, handle or picture, and only then move to definitions and general principles. Definitions, rules and theories are all abstract, so they come later in the lesson, built on concrete experience.

**Q39 — (C),** because the original taxonomy used nouns such as Knowledge, Comprehension and Application. The revision changed them to verbs (Remember, Understand, Apply, Analyse, Evaluate, Create) because objectives describe what learners do, and thinking is an active process.

**Q40 — (A),** because since learners differ in ability, interest and speed, a single method at a single pace suits only a few. Varying methods, giving graded tasks and offering extra help or enrichment allows more learners to succeed. This is the basis of differentiated instruction.

**Q41 — (D),** because N. L. Gage (1963) defined teaching as interpersonal influence aimed at changing the ways in which other persons can or will behave. The definition stresses that teaching involves at least two persons and an intention to change behaviour. It is among the most quoted definitions of teaching.

**Q42 — (C),** because Ausubel proposed that a teacher should present a brief, general introduction before the detailed lesson, so that learners have a framework to which new material can be attached. He called these introductions advance organisers. They are central to his theory of meaningful verbal learning.

**Q43 — (D),** because content validity is the extent to which the items of a test represent the content and objectives of the course. It is ensured by preparing a blueprint, a table that distributes marks over topics and objectives, before the items are written.

**Q44 — (C),** because Osborn's rules for brainstorming are: defer judgement, welcome unusual ideas, aim at quantity and build on the ideas of others. Criticism during idea generation makes participants cautious, so evaluation is kept for a separate, later stage.

**Q45 — (D),** because the Board of Studies of each subject, made up of teachers and external experts, frames and revises the syllabus, which then goes to the Academic Council for approval. The curriculum is thus an institution-related factor within which the teacher has to plan.

**Q46 — (B),** because in a group discussion the learners do most of the talking and thinking, while the teacher guides. Lecture, dictation and teacher demonstration keep the teacher at the centre and the learners mainly listening or watching, so they are classed as teacher-centred.

**Q47 — (A),** because micro-teaching is scaled down in every respect: the class is reduced to about five to ten pupils or peers, the lesson to about five to ten minutes, and the task to a single teaching skill. A normal classroom period lasts about forty to forty-five minutes.

**Q48 — (A),** because from about eleven or twelve years, in the formal operational stage, young people can reason about possibilities, form hypotheses and think about abstract ideas. In the concrete operational stage, logical thinking is still tied to real objects and events.

**Q49 — (B),** because learner characteristics are cognitive (intelligence, aptitude, previous knowledge), affective (interest, attitude, motivation, values) and psychomotor. Interest is a feeling of liking that draws the learner towards a subject, so it is affective. The other three are cognitive abilities or attainments.

**Q50 — (C),** because problem-solving follows the steps of scientific inquiry: recognising and defining the problem, collecting relevant data, forming hypotheses, testing them and drawing a conclusion. Nothing can be collected or tested until the problem itself has been clearly identified.

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 4**

**Q1 — (C),** because Analysis, the fourth level of the original taxonomy, involves separating a whole into elements and finding the relationships among them, for example distinguishing facts from opinions in an article. Synthesis moves in the opposite direction, and Evaluation judges the worth of material against criteria.

**Q2 — (C),** because to teach well a teacher must know the subject and know how to teach it: planning, explaining, questioning, managing the class and assessing. These make up professional competence. Seniority, appearance and administrative position do not by themselves improve what students learn.

**Q3 — (D),** because at the Receiving level the learner merely attends. At the Responding level the learner reacts actively and willingly: answers, participates, volunteers. Valuing requires that the learner attach worth to the matter and show commitment, which mere participation in a discussion does not prove.

**Q4 — (D),** because a multiple-choice item has a stem, which poses the problem, a key, which is the correct option, and distractors, which are plausible wrong options meant to attract those who do not know the answer. Premises and responses are the two columns of a matching item.

**Q5 — (A),** because Bruner held that knowledge can be represented through action (enactive), through images (iconic) and through language or other symbols (symbolic), and that teaching should usually move in that order. The other options list learning styles, affective levels and Herbartian steps.

**Q6 — (D),** because three-dimensional aids have length, breadth and depth, and can be viewed from all sides: models, specimens, objects, globes and mock-ups. Charts, maps and photographs are flat, two-dimensional aids. Models are useful when the real thing is too big, small or costly to bring to class.

**Q7 — (C),** because e-PG Pathshala is a UGC initiative under the National Mission on Education through ICT that offers curriculum-based, interactive e-content in a large number of postgraduate subjects. School textbooks in digital form are available on NCERT's ePathshala, and simulated experiments on Virtual Labs.

**Q8 — (B),** because set induction, or the skill of introducing a lesson, prepares learners mentally by arousing interest and connecting the topic with what they know. Closure is used at the end to consolidate, stimulus variation holds attention during the lesson, and reinforcement encourages desired responses.

**Q9 — (B),** because when questions and options are shuffled, neighbouring candidates do not see the same item in the same position, which makes copying difficult. Randomisation from a question bank is one of the security advantages of computer-based testing over a single printed paper.

**Q10 — (A),** because the outcome of memory level teaching is retention and reproduction of facts, which recall and recognition items measure well. Understanding level outcomes need items that call for explanation and application, and reflective level outcomes are best judged through essay-type and open-ended tasks.

**Q11 — (A),** because the statement only reports a quantity and passes no judgement, so it is measurement. If we add 'which is a good performance for his class', a value judgement is made and the statement becomes evaluation. Appraisal is another word for such judgement.

**Q12 — (B),** because Jonathan Bergmann and Aaron Sams, two chemistry teachers in the United States, began recording their lectures for students in 2007 and described the approach in Flip Your Classroom. Hase and Kenyon are linked with heutagogy, Siemens and Downes with connectivism, and Joyce and Weil with models of teaching.

**Q13 — (B),** because the Herbartian steps, used in memory level teaching, are preparation, presentation, comparison (or association), generalisation and application. Exploration, assimilation and recitation, along with presentation and organisation, are steps of Morrison's model for the understanding level.

**Q14 — (A),** because authentic assessment asks learners to use knowledge and skills in tasks that resemble those of real life or work, such as case analyses, projects and presentations. It shows what students can do with what they know, which conventional recall tests often fail to reveal.

**Q15 — (D),** because the term was introduced by Salovey and Mayer and made popular by Daniel Goleman's book Emotional Intelligence (1995). It covers self-awareness, self-regulation, motivation, empathy and social skills. Gardner's interpersonal and intrapersonal intelligences are related ideas but belong to a different theory.

**Q16 — (B),** because assimilation means interpreting a new experience in terms of a schema one already has. When the experience does not fit and the schema itself has to change, the process is accommodation. Equilibration is the balancing of the two, which drives cognitive development.

**Q17 — (A),** because in the inductive method learners examine several particular examples and are led to frame the general rule themselves. The deductive method does the reverse: the rule or definition is stated first and then applied to examples. Induction suits discovery; deduction saves time.

**Q18 — (B),** because the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016 replaced the Persons with Disabilities Act of 1995 and raised the number of recognised disabilities from seven to twenty-one. It requires educational institutions to provide inclusive education and reasonable accommodation. The Right to Education Act dates from 2009.

**Q19 — (C),** because Kilpatrick gave four steps. In purposing, students choose a project that has a real purpose for them. In planning, they decide how to carry it out. In executing, they do the work. In evaluating (judging), they review the result against the purpose with which they started.

**Q20 — (A),** because OER are teaching and learning materials in any medium that are in the public domain or released under an open licence, so that anyone may use, adapt and redistribute them at no cost. Open courseware, open textbooks and many SWAYAM materials are examples.

**Q21 — (B),** because a symposium is a series of short prepared talks by different speakers, each dealing with one aspect of a common theme, after which the audience may ask questions. It gives listeners a many-sided view of a topic in a short time.

**Q22 — (C),** because any learner may enrol in and study SWAYAM courses without charge. A fee is payable only by those who want a certificate, for which they must register for and pass a proctored examination. Credits so earned can be transferred to the learner's university record.

**Q23 — (A),** because under the UGC's CBCS guidelines, one credit generally corresponds to one hour of lecture or tutorial, or two hours of practical work, per week through a semester. Credits therefore show the relative weight of each course and are used in calculating the grade point average.

**Q24 — (D),** because Morrison's five steps are exploration, presentation, assimilation, organisation and recitation. In exploration the teacher tests and questions to find out what learners already know, so that the unit can be planned from that point. Presentation of the new content comes second.

**Q25 — (D),** because the student is working for a reward that lies outside the activity of studying, so the motivation is extrinsic. If the student studied because the subject itself was interesting, it would be intrinsic. External rewards work in the short run but can weaken interest if overused.

**Q26 — (B),** because in simulation learners practise a task in conditions made to resemble the real ones, such as a mock court, a flight simulator or simulated teaching among peers. Mistakes carry no real cost, so learners can experiment and get feedback before facing the actual situation.

**Q27 — (B),** because admissions, sections, class size, the timetable and the teaching load are fixed by institutional policy. Within these limits the teacher chooses examples, questions and style. Institutional factors can therefore support or restrict the methods a teacher is able to use.

**Q28 — (C),** because in a lecture communication is mostly one-way, so learners listen without much chance to question, discuss or apply. Attention falls after fifteen to twenty minutes. Syllabus coverage, large classes and introducing topics are, on the contrary, strengths of the method.

**Q29 — (C),** because a good teacher reads the feedback coming from the class and changes pace, example or method accordingly. Sticking to the plan regardless of the learners' response would put the plan above the learning. Flexibility is therefore listed among the characteristics of effective teaching.

**Q30 — (B),** because aptitude is future-oriented: it indicates how well a person is likely to learn in a field if given training. Achievement is what has already been learnt, interest is liking for an activity, and attitude is a settled tendency to respond favourably or unfavourably.

**Q31 — (B),** because marks suggest a precision that examinations do not possess: a difference of one or two marks is usually within the error of measurement. Grades place students in broad bands, which reduces misclassification, unhealthy competition and stress. Examinations are still needed to award them.

**Q32 — (A),** because the class election is something the students already know, and the teacher uses it as a bridge to the unfamiliar concept of democracy. Linking new material with existing knowledge is the maxim 'from known to unknown'. The lesson moves from a particular case, so 'general to particular' does not fit.

**Q33 — (B),** because Knowles assumed that as people mature they move from dependence to self-direction, accumulate experience that becomes a resource for learning, become ready to learn what their roles demand, prefer problem-centred learning and respond mainly to internal motivation.

**Q34 — (C),** because predictive validity is the degree to which test scores foretell performance on a later criterion, such as grades in a course. It is a form of criterion-related validity and is essential for entrance and aptitude tests. Content validity concerns coverage of a syllabus.

**Q35 — (C),** because the revised cognitive levels are Remember, Understand, Apply, Analyse, Evaluate and Create. Among the four listed, Remember is the lowest, followed by Understand, Apply and Analyse. A learner must recall and understand an idea before applying it, and must be able to apply it before analysing complex cases.

**Q36 — (D),** because the more knowledgeable other may be a teacher, parent or capable peer, or even a digital tool. Learning occurs when this person supports the learner on tasks within the Zone of Proximal Development. 'Generalised other' is a sociological term from G. H. Mead.

**Q37 — (D),** because at the reflective level learners face a problem, question assumptions, weigh evidence and produce their own solutions. This exercises critical and creative thinking and builds independence. Memorisation, imitation and listening are sufficient for lower levels but do not require reflection.

**Q38 — (C),** because teaching is said to have three functions. The diagnostic function finds out learners' entry behaviour and difficulties, the prescriptive function selects the content, methods and strategies needed, and the evaluative function checks how far the objectives were achieved.

**Q39 — (A),** because micro-teaching scales down the size of the class to about five to ten pupils, who may be real pupils or fellow trainees acting as pupils. The small group, the short lesson and the single skill together make the trainee's task manageable and feedback precise.

**Q40 — (C),** because in Thorndike's puzzle-box experiments a cat escaped by trying many responses and gradually dropping the unsuccessful ones. He concluded that learning is the stamping in of correct stimulus–response connections through trial and error, hence the name connectionism.

**Q41 — (D),** because training shapes specific skills and instruction imparts knowledge, while teaching covers both and also aims at understanding, attitudes and values. The usual continuum runs conditioning, training, instruction and teaching, with each later term wider and more concerned with the learner's reasoning.

**Q42 — (D),** because the result depends only on whether the candidate reaches a fixed standard of performance, not on how others perform, so the test is criterion-referenced. In a norm-referenced test the number who succeed is limited by rank, as in a competitive examination for a fixed number of seats.

**Q43 — (A),** because in Think–Pair–Share, devised by Frank Lyman, each student first thinks alone about a question, then discusses it with a partner, and finally the pairs share their ideas with the class. It gives every student a chance to take part, even in a large class.

**Q44 — (D),** because in the split-half method a test is given once, divided into two halves (usually odd and even items) and the two sets of scores are correlated; the Spearman–Brown formula then gives the reliability of the whole test. Other methods are test–retest and parallel forms.

**Q45 — (D),** because adolescents look for models to identify with and often idealise a hero, such as a sports star, leader or teacher. Day-dreaming is also common, as they imagine future roles. Both are linked with the adolescent's search for identity and emotional intensity.

**Q46 — (C),** because teaching is an intentional activity whose goal is learning, so the two are closely related, yet they are distinct processes carried out by different persons. Much learning happens through experience and self-study with no teacher present, so learning does not depend on teaching.

**Q47 — (B),** because summative evaluation judges how far the objectives of a course have been achieved, and its results are used to assign grades, decide promotion and award certificates. Daily feedback is the work of formative evaluation, and placement and diagnosis have their own types of evaluation.

**Q48 — (A),** because the Cumulative Grade Point Average is the credit-weighted average of grade points earned in all courses across all semesters of a programme. The SGPA is the corresponding figure for a single semester. Both are reported on the transcript under the CBCS.

**Q49 — (A),** because a complete behavioural objective names the performance (what the learner will do), the conditions (the circumstances or materials given) and the criterion (the standard of acceptable performance, such as 'eight out of ten correct' or 'within five minutes'). The criterion makes the objective measurable.

**Q50 — (D),** because when students can see one another's faces, they talk to one another and not only to the teacher. A circle or U-shape therefore encourages discussion. Rows and tiered halls direct all attention to the front and suit lectures.

# **Set 5**

**Level:** Easy     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

**1.**	A webinar is:

(A)	a seminar recorded on video tape for later use

(B)	a seminar conducted over the internet

(C)	a website that lists seminars

(D)	a printed report of a seminar

**2.**	The terms 'formative' and 'summative' evaluation were first introduced by:

(A) Ralph Tyler	(B) Robert Glaser

(C) Michael Scriven	(D) Robert Mager

**3.**	Using a formula learnt in class to solve a new numerical problem represents the level of:

(A) Application	(B) Knowledge

(C) Comprehension	(D) Evaluation

**4.**	'Grading on a curve' is a practice of:

(A)	criterion-referenced evaluation

(B)	norm-referenced evaluation

(C)	mastery-based evaluation

(D)	diagnostic evaluation

**5.**	A geography teacher first shows the map of India as a whole and then takes up the individual states. The maxim followed is:

(A) from part to whole	(B) from particular to general

(C) from empirical to rational	(D) from whole to part

**6.**	A limitation of computer-based testing in developing countries is:

(A)	impossibility of scoring answers objectively

(B)	long delay in the processing of results

(C)	unequal access to and familiarity with computers

(D)	difficulty in shuffling the order of items

**7.**	Differences among the various abilities of the same person are called:

(A) inter-individual differences	(B) inter-group differences

(C) intra-individual differences	(D) cross-cultural differences

**8.**	Self-assessment helps a learner mainly to:

(A)	reflect on personal strengths and weaknesses

(B)	compare personal rank with classmates

(C)	avoid the feedback given by the teacher

(D)	obtain higher marks without further study

**9.**	Most students entering undergraduate courses belong to the stage of:

(A) late childhood	(B) early childhood

(C) middle adulthood	(D) late adolescence

**10.**	A lecture can be made more effective by:

(A)	speaking much faster in order to cover more content

(B)	reading directly from the textbook

(C)	avoiding eye contact with the class

(D)	using questions and visual aids at intervals

**11.**	Which of the following is NOT scaled down in micro-teaching?

(A) The size of the class	(B) The role of feedback

(C) The length of the lesson	(D) The number of skills

**12.**	Under the CBCS, the 'credit points' earned in a course are obtained by:

(A)	adding the grade point to the credits

(B)	multiplying the grade point by the credits

(C)	dividing the grade point by the credits

(D)	subtracting the credits from the grade point

**13.**	Maturation, as a learner-related factor, refers to:

(A)	natural growth that makes learning possible

(B)	extra coaching that makes learning faster

(C)	strict discipline that makes learning orderly

(D)	repeated testing that makes learning permanent

**14.**	In Maslow's hierarchy, the need for food and water belongs to:

(A) physiological needs	(B) safety needs

(C) esteem needs	(D) belonging needs

**15.**	The level 'Understand' in the revised taxonomy corresponds to which level of Bloom's original taxonomy?

(A) Knowledge	(B) Application

(C) Comprehension	(D) Analysis

**16.**	Which of the following is an instructional facility?

(A) A teacher's sense of humour	(B) A learner's high aptitude

(C) A syllabus revision policy	(D) A well-equipped laboratory

**17.**	Giving the same test twice to the same group and correlating the two sets of scores is the:

(A) split-half method	(B) test–retest method

(C) parallel-forms method	(D) rational equivalence method

**18.**	Which level of teaching is a prerequisite for the other two?

(A) Understanding level	(B) Reflective level

(C) Memory level	(D) Insight level

**19.**	Compared with the general aims of education, instructional objectives are:

(A) specific and short-term	(B) broad and long-term

(C) vague and philosophical	(D) national and policy-based

**20.**	A classroom in which students are afraid to ask questions has a poor:

(A) psychological climate	(B) physical layout

(C) curriculum design	(D) evaluation scheme

**21.**	Diagnostic testing is normally followed by:

(A) final grading	(B) public ranking

(C) award of certificates	(D) remedial teaching

**22.**	'Hybrid learning' is another name for:

(A) distance learning	(B) programmed learning

(C) mastery learning	(D) blended learning

**23.**	A limitation of memory level teaching is that:

(A)	learners become too critical of what they are taught

(B)	facts learnt without understanding are soon forgotten

(C)	teachers lose control over the pace of the lesson

(D)	problems take too long for learners to investigate

**24.**	For adult learners, the richest resource for learning is their own:

(A) record of school marks	(B) collection of textbooks

(C) experience of life and work	(D) memory of class notes

**25.**	The word 'Open' in MOOC means that:

(A)	the course carries no form of assessment

(B)	the content is prepared without instructors

(C)	the course runs without any fixed duration

(D)	anyone may enrol without entry requirements

**26.**	A student keeps supporting a cleanliness campaign on her own because she believes it is worthwhile. The affective level shown is:

(A) Valuing	(B) Receiving

(C) Responding	(D) Imitation

**27.**	Which of the following comes closest to teaching and consists of imparting knowledge in an organised way?

(A) Conditioning	(B) Indoctrination

(C) Instruction	(D) Propaganda

**28.**	The three focal points of the teaching process are:

(A)	teacher, principal and parent

(B)	teacher, learner and subject matter

(C)	prescribed textbook, timetable and test

(D)	school, home and society

**29.**	At the reflective level of teaching, the teacher acts as a:

(A) strict authority	(B) silent spectator

(C) mere examiner	(D) democratic guide

**30.**	Teaching is regarded as a profession because it requires:

(A)	a fixed monthly salary, long vacations and a pension scheme

(B)	a government order, a post and a pay scale

(C)	specialised knowledge, training and a code of ethics

(D)	a classroom, a timetable and a textbook

**31.**	According to Vygotsky, the most important tool of thought and learning is:

(A) reward	(B) maturation

(C) imitation	(D) language

**32.**	The chance of scoring by guessing is greatest in:

(A) true–false items	(B) essay items

(C) short-answer items	(D) completion items

**33.**	In the case study method, students:

(A)	analyse a realistic situation and propose solutions

(B)	memorise a set of rules and reproduce them in a written test

(D)	repeat a skill until it becomes a habit

**34.**	Evaluating learners' achievement after a lesson and reflecting on how the lesson went belong to the:

(A) pre-active phase	(B) interactive phase

(C) inactive phase	(D) post-active phase

**35.**	Good teaching is called democratic when the teacher:

(A)	lets students fix the examination dates

(B)	takes a vote before starting each lesson

(C)	respects students' views and invites questions

(D)	avoids correcting any of the errors made by students

**36.**	Understanding that the quantity of water stays the same when it is poured into a glass of a different shape is called:

(A) conservation	(B) egocentrism

(C) animism	(D) object permanence

**37.**	Braille is used in teaching learners with:

(A) hearing impairment	(B) visual impairment

(C) speech impairment	(D) locomotor disability

**38.**	When a test merely appears, on the surface, to measure what it is supposed to measure, it is said to have:

(A) content validity	(B) predictive validity

(C) construct validity	(D) face validity

**39.**	The first level in Dave's taxonomy of the psychomotor domain is:

(A) Imitation	(B) Manipulation

(C) Precision	(D) Naturalisation

**40.**	The heuristic method of teaching was advocated by:

(A) W. H. Kilpatrick	(B) H. E. Armstrong

**41.**	Arrange the steps of Morrison's model of understanding level teaching in the correct order:

1\.	Assimilation

2\.	Exploration

**42.**	Brainstorming as a group technique was introduced by:

(C) J. P. Guilford	(D) Frank Lyman

**43.**	In operant conditioning, a reward given after a desired response is called:

**44.**	The first practical test of intelligence was developed by:

**45.**	Flash cards are especially useful for:

**46.**	Online learning in which teacher and students are present together in real time is called:

**47.**	The clarity of a teacher's explanation depends most directly on the teacher's:

**48.**	In programmed instruction, each small unit of content presented to the learner is called a:
