## **Answers and Explanations: Set 5**

**Q1 — (B),** because the word joins 'web' and 'seminar'. A webinar is a live online session in which a speaker presents to participants at different places, who can put questions through chat or audio. It is a synchronous, ICT-based mode of teaching and training.

**Q2 — (C),** because Michael Scriven introduced the two terms in 1967 in the context of curriculum evaluation. Bloom and his colleagues later applied them to the evaluation of student learning. Tyler is known for the objectives-based model of evaluation and Glaser for criterion-referenced measurement.

**Q3 — (A),** because Application is the use of a learnt rule, method or principle in a new concrete situation. Merely stating the formula would be Knowledge, and explaining what it means would be Comprehension. Evaluation would require a judgement of worth against criteria.

**Q4 — (B),** because grading on a curve fixes in advance the proportion of students who will receive each grade, so a student's grade depends on standing relative to the group. That is norm-referenced evaluation. In criterion-referenced evaluation every student who reaches the standard can get the top grade.

**Q5 — (D),** because Gestalt psychology holds that the whole is grasped before its parts, so a lesson should first give the complete picture and then examine details. Presenting the country before its states is 'whole to part'. Starting with single states and building up would be the reverse.

**Q6 — (C),** because candidates from rural or poorer backgrounds may have had little practice with computers, which can affect their performance for reasons unrelated to the subject tested. This digital divide, along with technical failures, is the chief limitation. Scoring, speed and shuffling are advantages.

**Q7 — (C),** because one person may be strong in language and weak in mathematics; such variation within an individual is intra-individual difference. Differences between one person and another are inter-individual differences. Both kinds matter in guiding students towards suitable courses.

**Q8 — (A),** because when learners judge their own work against clear criteria, they become aware of what they have mastered and what they have not, and can plan their next steps. This awareness of one's own learning is called metacognition. It supplements teacher feedback.

**Q9 — (D),** because students join college at about seventeen or eighteen, which falls in late adolescence. They are capable of abstract thought, are forming an identity and are strongly influenced by peers. Postgraduate and research students are mostly young adults.

**Q10 — (D),** because attention in a lecture drops after about fifteen minutes. Breaking the talk with questions, examples, short activities and visual aids restores attention and makes communication two-way. Speaking faster or reading from a book makes a lecture harder to follow.

**Q11 — (B),** because micro-teaching reduces class size, lesson time and the number of skills practised. Feedback is not reduced; it is the heart of the technique. The trainee gets immediate and detailed feedback from supervisor, peers or a video recording, and uses it to re-plan and re-teach.

**Q12 — (B),** because credit points for a course equal its credits multiplied by the grade point earned in it. For example, grade point 8 in a 4-credit course gives 32 credit points. The SGPA is the sum of credit points divided by the total credits of the semester.

**Q13 — (A),** because maturation is the unfolding of physical and mental capacities with age, independent of training. A learner must be mature enough for a task before teaching can succeed; this is the idea of readiness. Coaching, discipline and testing are external influences, not maturation.

**Q14 — (A),** because physiological needs (food, water, sleep, shelter) form the base of Maslow's hierarchy and must be met first. Next come safety needs, then love and belonging, esteem and self-actualisation. A mid-day meal scheme recognises that a hungry child cannot attend to lessons.

**Q15 — (C),** because the second level of the original taxonomy, Comprehension, became Understand in the 2001 revision. It covers interpreting, explaining, summarising, classifying and comparing. Knowledge corresponds to Remember, Application to Apply and Analysis to Analyse; only the top two levels changed places.

**Q16 — (D),** because instructional facilities are the physical resources that an institution provides for teaching: classrooms, laboratories, libraries, projectors and internet access. A sense of humour is a teacher characteristic, aptitude a learner characteristic, and syllabus policy an institutional or administrative matter.

**Q17 — (B),** because the test–retest method estimates reliability as stability over time. The split-half and rational equivalence (Kuder–Richardson) methods need only one administration and measure internal consistency, while the parallel-forms method uses two equivalent versions of the test.

**Q18 — (C),** because learners cannot understand relationships among facts they do not possess, and cannot reflect on problems without both facts and understanding. Memory level teaching therefore supplies the base. It is the lowest level, but the higher levels cannot do without it.

**Q19 — (A),** because aims are broad statements of purpose, such as developing responsible citizens, and are realised over years. Instructional objectives are narrow, precise statements of what learners will be able to do at the end of a lesson or unit, and can be achieved and tested in a short time.

**Q20 — (A),** because the psychological or socio-emotional climate of a class is made by relationships: how the teacher treats students and how they treat one another. Fear of ridicule or punishment silences students and blocks learning, even when the room and the curriculum are satisfactory.

**Q21 — (D),** because diagnosis is not an end in itself. Once the specific weaknesses of a learner and their causes are located, the teacher plans remedial instruction aimed at exactly those weaknesses. Grading and certification follow summative evaluation.

**Q22 — (D),** because hybrid or blended learning deliberately combines classroom teaching with online learning in one course. Distance learning separates teacher and learner for most of the course, programmed learning uses self-instructional frames, and mastery learning requires each unit to be mastered before the next.

**Q23 — (B),** because material memorised by rote has few links with other knowledge, so it fades quickly and cannot be used in new situations. Memory level teaching also leaves the learner passive. Criticism and lengthy investigation are associated with the reflective level, not with the memory level.

**Q24 — (C),** because Knowles pointed out that adults bring a large and varied store of experience to any learning situation. Methods that draw on it, such as discussion, case studies and problem-solving, work better with adults than one-way transmission.

**Q25 — (D),** because MOOCs are open in the sense that enrolment is not restricted by previous qualification, institution or place, and study is usually free. They still have instructors, a schedule of several weeks and assessments; a fee is normally charged only for a certificate.

**Q26 — (A),** because Valuing means attaching worth to an object, idea or behaviour and acting on that belief consistently enough for others to recognise it. Mere attention is Receiving and mere participation is Responding. Imitation is not an affective level at all; it belongs to the psychomotor domain.

**Q27 — (C),** because instruction is the systematic imparting of knowledge and can even be delivered without a teacher, through a programmed text or a computer. It is a part of teaching, though teaching is wider. Conditioning, indoctrination and propaganda do not aim at understanding or free judgement.

**Q28 — (B),** because teaching brings together a teacher who guides, a learner who learns and the subject matter through which the two interact. Effective teaching requires that all three be attended to: a competent teacher, a ready learner and content suited to the learner.

**Q29 — (D),** because the teacher at the reflective level raises a problem, helps learners clarify it and supports their inquiry without imposing conclusions. The classroom is open and democratic. The teacher is neither an authority handing down answers nor a spectator who leaves learners unaided.

**Q30 — (C),** because a profession is marked by a body of specialised knowledge, a long period of training, a service motive and a code of conduct accepted by its members. Teaching has all of these. Salary, post and physical facilities are conditions of employment, not marks of a profession.

**Q31 — (D),** because Vygotsky regarded language as the chief cultural tool through which thinking develops. Children first use speech to communicate with others, then talk to themselves to guide action (private speech), and finally this becomes silent inner speech, or thought.

**Q32 — (A),** because a true–false item has only two choices, so a blind guess has a fifty per cent chance of being right. A four-option multiple-choice item reduces this to twenty-five per cent. Supply-type items, where the student must write the answer, leave little room for guessing.

**Q33 — (A),** because a case is a written account of a real or realistic problem situation. Students study the facts, identify the issues, discuss alternatives and defend a decision. The method links theory with practice and develops analysis, judgement and decision-making.

**Q34 — (D),** because the post-active phase follows classroom teaching. The teacher tests how far the objectives were achieved, selects suitable testing devices and uses the results to change strategies for the next lesson. Its findings feed back into the planning, or pre-active, phase.

**Q35 — (C),** because democratic teaching means treating learners with respect, giving them freedom to ask, differ and take part, and reasoning with them instead of commanding. It does not mean handing over the teacher's responsibilities or leaving errors uncorrected.

**Q36 — (A),** because conservation is the understanding that quantity, number or mass does not change when appearance changes. Piaget found that children achieve it in the concrete operational stage. Egocentrism and animism are features of pre-operational thinking, and object permanence develops in the sensorimotor stage.

**Q37 — (B),** because Braille is a system of raised dots read by touch, which allows learners who are blind or have low vision to read and write. Learners with hearing impairment are helped by sign language and captions. Inclusive education requires such supports.

**Q38 — (D),** because face validity is the impression a test gives to candidates and lay observers that it is relevant. It is not validity in the technical sense, but it affects acceptance and motivation. Content, predictive and construct validity all require systematic evidence.

**Q39 — (A),** because Dave's five levels are Imitation, Manipulation, Precision, Articulation and Naturalisation. At the first level the learner watches a model and copies the action, often crudely. With practice the action becomes accurate, coordinated and finally automatic.

**Q40 — (B),** because Professor H. E. Armstrong advocated the heuristic method for teaching science. The word comes from the Greek for 'I find out': pupils are placed in the position of discoverers and find facts and principles for themselves, with the teacher guiding but telling as little as possible.

**Q41 — (A),** because Morrison's order is exploration, presentation, assimilation, organisation and recitation. The teacher explores previous knowledge and presents the unit; learners then assimilate it through supervised study, organise it in writing without help, and finally present it orally before the class.

**Q42 — (B),** because Alex F. Osborn, an advertising executive, described brainstorming in his book Applied Imagination. De Bono is known for lateral thinking and the six thinking hats, Guilford for the structure of intellect and divergent thinking, and Lyman for Think–Pair–Share.

**Q43 — (B),** because positive reinforcement strengthens a response by adding something pleasant after it, such as praise for a correct answer. Negative reinforcement strengthens a response by removing something unpleasant. Punishment weakens a response, and extinction is the fading of a response that is no longer reinforced.

**Q44 — (D),** because Alfred Binet, with Théodore Simon, produced the first workable intelligence scale in France in 1905 to identify children who needed special help in school. It introduced the idea of mental age. Wechsler later developed widely used scales for adults and children.

**Q45 — (C),** because a flash card is a small card carrying a word, picture, formula or question that is shown briefly to the class. Flash cards suit drill, revision and recall of vocabulary, definitions and number facts. They are simple, non-projected visual aids.

**Q46 — (C),** because synchronous learning happens at the same time for all participants, as in a live video class or webinar, and allows immediate interaction. Asynchronous learning uses recorded lectures, forums and assignments that each learner takes up at a convenient time.

**Q47 — (A),** because a teacher can explain clearly only what he or she understands well, and only in language that reaches the learners. Subject mastery and communication skill are therefore the teacher-related factors behind clear explanation. Age and status have no direct bearing on it.

**Q48 — (B),** because a programme is a sequence of frames. Each frame gives a small piece of information, requires a response and lets the learner check it at once. Small steps, active responding, immediate confirmation and self-pacing are the principles of programmed instruction.

**Q49 — (D),** because Ausubel argued that most classroom learning is reception of verbal material, and that it is meaningful when the learner relates it to what he or she already knows. He contrasted meaningful learning with rote learning. Discovery learning is linked with Bruner.

**Q50 — (C),** because the moderator introduces the topic and the panel, puts questions, gives each member a fair chance, prevents digression and sums up. A rapporteur records the proceedings and prepares the report. An invigilator supervises an examination.

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 6**

**Q1 — (D),** because by acting as another person, a learner experiences that person's situation and feelings and practises ways of communicating. Role play is therefore valued for attitudes, empathy and interpersonal skills, which belong to the affective domain, more than for factual knowledge.

**Q2 — (D),** because assimilation is the longest step of Morrison's model. Pupils work through the material individually in a supervised study period until they have mastered it, and the teacher tests and re-teaches as needed. Mastery is checked before the class moves on to organisation and recitation.

**Q3 — (A),** because an anecdotal record is a teacher's brief written description of a significant incident in a student's behaviour. It gives descriptive, not numerical, information. Test scores, percentile ranks and standard scores are quantitative. Evaluation makes use of both kinds of evidence.

**Q4 — (C),** because McClelland studied the need for achievement (n-Ach), the desire to do things well and to succeed against a standard of excellence. People high in this need prefer tasks of moderate difficulty and want feedback. Maslow proposed the hierarchy of needs.

**Q5 — (C),** because both Piaget and Vygotsky are constructivists, holding that learners build knowledge. Piaget stressed the individual child acting on the world (cognitive constructivism), whereas Vygotsky stressed interaction with others and with culture, so his view is called social or socio-cultural constructivism.

**Q6 — (B),** because the base of the cone represents first-hand, purposeful experience, the most concrete form of learning. As one moves upward through contrived experiences, demonstrations, exhibits and pictures to visual and verbal symbols, experience becomes more and more abstract.

**Q7 — (D),** because the ABCD model asks four questions. Who is to perform (Audience)? What will they do (Behaviour)? Under what circumstances (Condition)? How well (Degree)? It is an extension of Mager's three components of performance, condition and criterion.

**Q8 — (D),** because formative evaluation is assessment for learning because its results are used to improve learning while it is still going on. Summative evaluation is assessment of learning, since it records what has been achieved at the end. Self-assessment is sometimes called assessment as learning.

**Q9 — (A),** because problem-solving, in which learners investigate and reach their own conclusions, is the method of the reflective level. Independent inquiry does not belong to the memory level, rote drill does not belong to the understanding level, and recitation of facts is a memory level activity.

**Q10 — (A),** because an e-portfolio stores a student's selected work in electronic form, such as documents, presentations, audio and video, along with reflections and the teacher's comments. It can be shared online and updated over years, and shows growth better than a single examination.

**Q11 — (B),** because the full form is Study Webs of Active-Learning for Young Aspiring Minds. The platform is built around the three principles of access, equity and quality, and its courses have four parts: video lectures, reading material, self-assessment tests and an online discussion forum.

**Q12 — (B),** because verbs such as design, construct, compose, formulate and plan ask the learner to produce something new by combining elements, which is the level of Create. Remember uses verbs like list and define, Understand uses explain and summarise, and Apply uses solve and demonstrate.

**Q13 — (A),** because Robert Sternberg's triarchic theory describes three aspects of intelligence: analytical (componential), creative (experiential) and practical (contextual). The two-factor theory is Spearman's, the group-factor theory is Thurstone's, and the sampling theory was proposed by Godfrey Thomson.

**Q14 — (D),** because the 1956 hierarchy ends with Evaluation, the ability to judge the value of material for a given purpose using definite criteria. Synthesis is the fifth level. The revision of 2001 reversed the order of these two and renamed Synthesis as Create.

**Q15 — (A),** because Knowles held that while adults respond to some external motivators such as promotion, the strongest are internal: self-esteem, job satisfaction, quality of life and the wish to grow. Pressure, fear and small rewards are typical of the motivation of school children.

**Q16 — (B),** because in Bloom's mastery learning, each unit has a fixed standard, say eighty per cent, that every learner is expected to reach before moving on. Checking this needs tests interpreted against the standard, that is, criterion-referenced tests, followed by correctives for those who fall short.

**Q17 — (D),** because SGPA equals the sum of (credits multiplied by grade point) for all courses of the semester, divided by the sum of the credits of those courses. Dividing by the number of courses would ignore the fact that courses carry different credits.

**Q18 — (B),** because inclusion means that learners of all backgrounds and abilities, including those with disabilities, learn together in the same classroom, with the supports each needs. It is a feature of the learning environment and is required by the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016.

**Q19 — (A),** because reinforcement is the skill of encouraging desired responses through praise, acceptance, nods and smiles, so that students take part more. Probing means asking follow-up questions to deepen an answer, illustrating means using examples, and closure consolidates the lesson at the end.

**Q20 — (C),** because item analysis is carried out after a test is tried out. For each item it finds the difficulty value (the proportion answering correctly) and the discriminating power (how well it separates high from low scorers), and examines whether the distractors are working.

**Q21 — (A),** because Bruce Joyce and Marsha Weil described more than twenty models of teaching and grouped them into four families: information-processing, social, personal and behavioural. Flanders and Amidon are linked with interaction analysis, and Allen and Ryan with micro-teaching.

**Q22 — (B),** because in problem-solving, learners define a problem, gather data, form hypotheses, test them and draw conclusions, which are the steps of the scientific method. The method therefore trains reasoning and a scientific attitude. It makes little use of rote memory or mechanical drill.

**Q23 — (B),** because the Herbartian sequence is preparation, presentation, comparison, generalisation and application. After a rule or generalisation has been drawn, the learner applies it to fresh examples, which fixes it in the mind. Recitation is the final step of Morrison's model, not of Herbart's.

**Q24 — (C),** because the teacher explains a principle and at the same time demonstrates it with apparatus, specimens or models, so learners both hear and see. The method is widely used in science teaching. It is more concrete than a pure lecture, though learners still mostly watch.

**Q25 — (C),** because a test must first of all measure what it is meant to measure. A reliable, objective and convenient test that measures the wrong thing is useless for its purpose. Reliability is necessary for validity, but it does not guarantee validity.

**Q26 — (B),** because the UGC's CBCS guidelines describe core courses, elective courses and ability enhancement courses. Electives offered by the main discipline are Discipline Specific Electives; those taken from another discipline are Generic Electives. Skill Enhancement Courses are a type of ability enhancement course.

**Q27 — (C),** because the leadership and climate of an institution are institution-related factors. An administration that trusts teachers, provides resources and encourages professional development makes good teaching easier. It cannot remove the need for planning, judgement or evaluation, which remain the teacher's work.

**Q28 — (C),** because children who watched an adult behave aggressively towards an inflated doll later imitated that behaviour. Bandura concluded that much learning is observational: people learn by watching models, without direct reinforcement. This is the core of his social learning theory.

**Q29 — (D),** because mock tests let candidates practise navigating between questions, marking and changing answers and watching the on-screen timer. Familiarity reduces anxiety and prevents loss of marks through handling errors. The questions in a mock test are different from those of the real examination.

**Q30 — (D),** because a smart classroom has a computer, projector or interactive board, audio system and internet connection, so that the teacher can use multimedia and online resources in face-to-face teaching. The teacher remains central; the technology supports the lesson.

**Q31 — (B),** because DIKSHA (Digital Infrastructure for Knowledge Sharing), launched in 2017 and led by NCERT under the Ministry of Education, provides curriculum-linked e-content for school students and training material for teachers. QR codes in textbooks link to its resources.

**Q32 — (A),** because adolescents are self-conscious and care greatly about how they appear to others, especially peers, so public criticism hurts them deeply. They are capable of abstract thought and are emotionally changeable. Teachers should correct them privately and respectfully.

**Q33 — (C),** because a matching item has two columns: the premises, which act as questions, and the responses, from which answers are chosen. Giving more responses than premises reduces guessing. Stems and distractors are parts of a multiple-choice item.

**Q34 — (A),** because food naturally produces salivation without any learning, so it is the unconditioned stimulus and the salivation it produces is the unconditioned response. The bell, after being paired with food, becomes the conditioned stimulus, and salivation to the bell alone is the conditioned response.

**Q35 — (D),** because teaching transmits knowledge, develops skills, inculcates values, socialises and motivates the learner, always leaving room for reasoning. Imposing an ideology that may not be questioned is indoctrination, which works against the open, critical outlook that teaching is meant to develop.

**Q36 — (C),** because teacher efficacy is the teacher's confidence that he or she can help even difficult students learn. Teachers with high efficacy persist longer and try new methods. Autonomy is freedom in professional decisions, accountability is answerability for results, and burnout is exhaustion from prolonged stress.

**Q37 — (C),** because the revised order is Remember, Understand, Apply, Analyse, Evaluate, Create. Evaluate, which means making judgements on the basis of criteria and standards, is the fifth level and stands just below Create. In the original taxonomy the corresponding level, Evaluation, was at the top.

**Q38 — (B),** because teacher-centred methods such as the lecture place the teacher in control of content, pace and activity; learners listen, watch and take notes. Discovering, planning and peer tutoring are roles that learners take in learner-centred approaches.

**Q39 — (A),** because dyslexia is a difficulty with accurate and fluent word reading and spelling, despite normal intelligence and teaching. Difficulty with calculation is dyscalculia, with handwriting dysgraphia, and with coordination of movement dyspraxia. Such learners need extra time and alternative formats.

**Q40 — (B),** because in the analytic method, used especially in mathematics, the teacher starts from what is to be proved or found (the unknown) and breaks it down until it connects with what is given (the known). The synthetic method moves the opposite way and is shorter.

**Q41 — (A),** because reliability is estimated by correlating two sets of scores, for example from two administrations or two halves of a test. The resulting reliability coefficient ranges from 0 to 1; values of about 0.80 or more are expected of standardised achievement tests.

**Q42 — (B),** because reflective teaching demands a store of knowledge, mature reasoning and independence, so it suits higher classes, especially postgraduate and research work. Young children and beginners first need facts and understanding, which the memory and understanding levels provide.

**Q43 — (C),** because differentiated instruction, associated with Carol Ann Tomlinson, means planning varied routes to the same goals: different materials, activities and ways of showing learning according to readiness, interest and learning profile. It is the practical response to individual differences in a mixed class.

**Q44 — (C),** because Moodle (Modular Object-Oriented Dynamic Learning Environment) is free, open-source software that any institution may install and modify. Blackboard is a commercial product, and Google Classroom and Microsoft Teams are proprietary services, though they may be offered to institutions without charge.

**Q45 — (D),** because Ned Flanders devised a ten-category system in which an observer codes classroom talk every three seconds as teacher talk, student talk or silence. The record shows how far the teacher's influence is direct or indirect. It deals only with verbal behaviour.

**Q46 — (D),** because the psychological order starts from the learner's interests, needs and level of development; the logical order follows the structure of the subject as an expert sees it. The maxim asks the teacher to begin with the former and move gradually to the latter.

**Q47 — (B),** because in a buzz session a large class is divided on the spot into groups of four to six, which discuss a question for a few minutes and report back. The name comes from the hum of many conversations. It brings participation into large classes.

**Q48 — (A),** because teaching exists to produce learning, so its success is measured by the change it brings about in learners' knowledge, skills and attitudes. Lectures delivered and notes prepared are inputs. A silent class may or may not be a learning class.

**Q49 — (D),** because teaching is a science in that it rests on systematic principles of learning, motivation and evaluation which can be observed, tested and taught to new teachers. It is also an art, because applying those principles to a live class calls for personal skill and creativity.

**Q50 — (C),** because the pre-operational child (about two to seven years) finds it hard to see things from another person's point of view; Piaget called this egocentrism. Reversibility appears in the concrete operational stage, and abstract reasoning and hypothesis testing in the formal operational stage.

# **Set 7**

**Level:** Moderate     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

**1.**	A teacher asks students to identify the unstated assumptions in an argument and show how its parts are organised. In the revised taxonomy this task is at the level of:

(A) Understand	(B) Evaluate

(C) Create	(D) Analyse

**2.**	Essay-type tests are superior to objective-type tests in:

(A)	sampling the content of the whole course

(B)	securing objectivity in the scoring

(C)	saving the time required for scoring

(D)	measuring organisation and expression of ideas

**3.**	In terms of Bloom's taxonomy, a flipped classroom assigns:

(A)	higher-order learning to home and lower-order work to class

(B)	lower-order learning to home and higher-order work to class

(C)	all levels of learning to home and only tests to the class

(D)	all levels of learning to class and only revision to home

**4.**	Köhler's chimpanzee Sultan, after a pause, suddenly joined two sticks to pull in a banana lying out of reach. This illustrates:

(A) trial and error learning	(B) insight learning

(C) operant conditioning	(D) latent learning

**5.**	Consider the following statements about the lecture method:

1\.	It is suitable for large classes.

2\.	It is effective for developing psychomotor skills.

3\.	It can present material that is not readily available in textbooks.

4\.	It ensures feedback from every learner.

Which of the statements given above are correct?

(A) 1 and 2 only	(B) 2 and 4 only

(C) 1, 3 and 4 only	(D) 1 and 3 only

**6.**	Simpson's taxonomy of the psychomotor domain begins with the level of:

(A) Perception	(B) Imitation

(C) Reflex movements	(D) Receiving

**7.**	Awarding grade 'A' to every student who scores 80 per cent or more, however many such students there may be, is:

(A) relative grading	(B) absolute grading

(C) grading on the curve	(D) percentile grading

**8.**	Students of sociology survey sanitation in a nearby village, analyse the data and present a report to the panchayat. The method used is the:

(A) case study method	(B) demonstration method

(C) tutorial method	(D) project method

| 11  **D** | 12  **C** | 13  **A** | 14  **C** | 15  **B** | 16  **D** | 17  **C** | 18  **B** | 19  **A** | 20  **B** |
| 21  **B** | 22  **C** | 23  **A** | 24  **D** | 25  **A** | 26  **A** | 27  **B** | 28  **A** | 29  **B** | 30  **B** |
| 31  **C** | 32  **D** | 33  **C** | 34  **B** | 35  **C** | 36  **A** | 37  **A** | 38  **D** | 39  **A** | 40  **A** |
| 41  **B** | 42  **D** | 43  **C** | 44  **B** | 45  **C** | 46  **D** | 47  **C** | 48  **A** | 49  **C** | 50  **A** |

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 7**

**Q1 — (D),** because Analyse involves breaking material into parts and finding how the parts relate to one another and to an overall structure; its processes are differentiating, organising and attributing. Identifying hidden assumptions is attributing. No judgement of worth is asked for, so the task is not Evaluate.

**Q2 — (D),** because only an extended written answer shows whether a student can select, organise, integrate and express ideas. Objective tests are superior on the other three counts: they sample content widely, are scored objectively and are quick to mark.

**Q3 — (B),** because students gain first exposure to content, that is remembering and understanding, on their own through videos or readings. The harder work of applying, analysing, evaluating and creating is done in class, where the teacher and peers are available to help.

**Q4 — (B),** because the Gestalt psychologists explained such sudden solutions as insight: the learner perceives the whole situation and grasps the relationship between its parts. The solution appears all at once, not through gradual elimination of errors, and it is readily repeated and transferred.

**Q5 — (D),** because statements 1 and 3 are correct: a lecture reaches many learners at once and can bring together recent or scattered material. Statement 2 is wrong because skills need practice, not listening. Statement 4 is wrong because communication in a lecture is largely one-way.

**Q6 — (A),** because Simpson's levels are perception, set, guided response, mechanism, complex overt response, adaptation and origination. Perception is the use of sense organs to obtain cues that guide motor activity. Imitation is the first level in Dave's scheme, reflex movements in Harrow's, and Receiving in the affective domain.

**Q7 — (B),** because in absolute grading, grade boundaries are fixed in advance in terms of marks, and anyone who crosses a boundary gets that grade. In relative grading, boundaries depend on the performance of the group, for example the top ten per cent get 'A'.

**Q8 — (D),** because the students carry a purposeful, real-life task through planning, execution and evaluation and end with a tangible product, which are the marks of the project method. In a case study they would analyse a case already written up for them, without collecting data in the field.

**Q9 — (C),** because Weiner classified causes by locus (internal or external) and stability. Effort lies within the person and can change from one occasion to the next, so it is internal and unstable. Ability is internal and stable, task difficulty external and stable, and luck external and unstable.

**Q10 — (D),** because probing means following up a student's response with further questions that prompt, seek clarification, refocus or ask for justification, so that the student reaches a fuller answer. Reinforcement would praise the answer, and explaining would have the teacher supply the missing part.

**Q11 — (D),** because statements 1 and 2 are correct: the need to know and role-related readiness are among Knowles's assumptions, along with self-concept, experience, orientation and motivation. Statement 3 reverses his position. Adults are life-centred or problem-centred; subject-centred learning is characteristic of pedagogy.

**Q12 — (C),** because anxiety is an emotional state. High anxiety uses up attention and working memory, so performance falls even when knowledge is adequate. Her cognitive ability is not in question, no physical cause is mentioned, and institutional factors lie outside the learner.

**Q13 — (A),** because classification, seriation and conservation are achievements of the concrete operational stage, in which logical thinking is applied to real objects and events. Reasoning about hypothetical statements needs formal operations. Chronological age is only a rough guide, and many adolescents still reason concretely in unfamiliar subjects.

**Q14 — (C),** because Comprehension has three forms: translation, interpretation and extrapolation. Putting material into another form, such as words into a graph, is translation. Nothing is applied to a new problem and nothing is broken into parts, so it is neither Application nor Analysis.

**Q15 — (B),** because an adaptive test estimates ability as it proceeds. A correct answer brings a harder item and a wrong answer an easier one, so each candidate gets items suited to his or her level. This gives a precise score with fewer items than a fixed test.

**Q16 — (D),** because the reflective level needs a genuine problem with no ready answer, on which learners must weigh evidence and reach and defend their own conclusion. Explaining in one's own words and using a taught formula are understanding level tasks, and listing sources is a memory level task.

**Q17 — (C),** because the class meets several particular cases first and the general statement is drawn from them afterwards. This is the maxim 'from particular to general', which underlies the inductive method. Stating the law first and then verifying it with rods would be 'general to particular'.

**Q18 — (B),** because Kolb's cycle runs concrete experience, reflective observation, abstract conceptualisation and active experimentation, and then returns to new experience. The learner has an experience, reflects on it, draws general ideas and tests them. His four learning styles are diverging, assimilating, converging and accommodating.

**Q19 — (A),** because facility value is the proportion of examinees who answer an item correctly. A value of 0.90 means that ninety per cent got it right, so the item is very easy. Items near 0.50 discriminate best; very easy and very hard items discriminate poorly.

**Q20 — (B),** because the objective is simple reproduction of facts, a memory level outcome. Drill and repetition fix such facts, and recall or recognition items check them directly and economically. Seminars, projects and debates aim at higher outcomes and would be wasteful for this purpose.

**Q21 — (B),** because the Indian model allots 6 minutes to teaching, 6 to feedback, 12 to re-planning, 6 to re-teaching and 6 to re-feedback. Adding these, 6 + 6 + 12 + 6 + 6 gives 36 minutes. The model uses peers as pupils and low-cost feedback by supervisors.

**Q22 — (C),** because Bruner held that any subject can be taught in some honest form to any child at any stage. The curriculum should therefore return to the basic ideas of a subject repeatedly, each time at a higher level, moving from enactive through iconic to symbolic representation.

**Q23 — (A),** because summarising is a process of Understand. Differentiating relevant from irrelevant parts is a process of Analyse. Justifying a decision requires judgement against criteria, which is Evaluate. Composing an original piece means producing something new, which is Create.

**Q24 — (D),** because statements 1, 2 and 3 are correct. Planning decisions, such as selecting and sequencing content and fixing objectives, belong to the pre-active phase, so statement 4 is wrong. Classroom presentation, feedback and reinforcement are interactive, and selecting testing devices to judge outcomes is post-active.

**Q25 — (A),** because gifted learners need work that stretches them: enrichment (deeper and wider tasks in the same class), acceleration, independent projects and mentoring. More of the same work increases boredom, and exemption leaves their ability unused.

**Q26 — (A),** because a seminar centres on a paper that is presented and then discussed in depth. A symposium is a set of prepared talks on different aspects of a theme. A panel discussion is a moderated conversation among experts. A workshop is practical work leading to a product.

**Q27 — (B),** because the earliest MOOCs, run by Siemens and Downes, were connectivist (cMOOCs): learners created and shared content across networks. xMOOCs are the later, structured, instructor-led courses offered on large platforms. A SPOC is a small private online course for a limited group.

**Q28 — (A),** because a norm-referenced statement tells where a person stands in relation to a group, as a percentile does. The other three statements describe what Rina did or whether she met a fixed standard, with no reference to other students, so they are criterion-referenced.

**Q29 — (B),** because the interruptions had been maintained by the teacher's attention, which acted as a reinforcer. When the reinforcer is withheld, the behaviour weakens and dies out; this is extinction. Nothing unpleasant is applied, so it is not punishment, and nothing aversive is removed, so it is not negative reinforcement.

**Q30 — (B),** because classroom management covers organising space, time, routines and relationships so that students are engaged in learning for as much of the period as possible. Good management prevents most problems before they arise. Punishment and silence are not its aims.

**Q31 — (C),** because Krathwohl's order is Receiving, Responding, Valuing, Organisation and Characterisation. The principle behind it is internalisation: the learner first attends, then reacts, then accepts a value, then organises values into a system, and finally is characterised by that system.

**Q32 — (D),** because video conferencing provides live, two-way audio and video among several places, so students everywhere can see the expert and ask questions. Podcasts and screen recordings are one-way and asynchronous, and e-mail lacks sight, sound and immediacy.

**Q33 — (C),** because an aid is a means, not an end. It should be chosen for the objective, be accurate and visible, and be shown when the point arises and removed afterwards. Too many aids, or attractive ones displayed all along, distract attention from the lesson.

**Q34 — (B),** because in Aronson's jigsaw, the lesson is divided into parts and each member of a home group is given one part. Members studying the same part meet as an expert group, then return and teach their part to the home group, so every member depends on the others.

**Q35 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Tasks such as experiments, presentations and field reports show whether students can actually do something. Their weakness is on the scoring side: they take longer to mark and depend on the judgement of the assessor, which is why rubrics are used.

**Q36 — (A),** because David Elkind described adolescent egocentrism in two forms. The imaginary audience is the feeling of being always on stage, which explains self-consciousness about appearance. The personal fable is the belief that one is unique and invulnerable, which lies behind risk-taking.

**Q37 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Communication in the classroom is carried by voice, face, gesture and movement as well as by words, and learners read these cues for emphasis, interest and attitude. That is why non-verbal behaviour influences learning.

**Q38 — (D),** because Statement I is false and Statement II is true. What makes evaluation formative or summative is its purpose, not the instrument. A test used to guide further teaching is formative, even if it looks like an examination. A test used at the end to grade or promote is summative.

**Q39 — (A),** because the enactment only provides experience. Learning comes when players and observers analyse what happened, how the characters felt and what alternatives existed. This discussion, or debriefing, connects the experience with the objectives of the lesson.

**Q40 — (A),** because at the memory level the teacher is dominant and the learner passive. At the understanding level learners take part more, and at the reflective level they carry the inquiry while the teacher guides. Teacher control and learner activity thus move in opposite directions across the levels.

**Q41 — (B),** because the epidiascope combines an episcope, which projects opaque material such as a page, picture or specimen by reflected light, with a diascope, which projects transparent slides. It is a projected visual aid that needs a darkened room.

**Q42 — (D),** because the pre-test reveals the learners' entry behaviour and weaknesses, which is the diagnostic function of teaching. Choosing content and activities to remedy those weaknesses is the prescriptive function. The evaluative function would come later, when the teacher checks whether the remedy has worked.

**Q43 — (C),** because programmed instruction rests on five principles: small steps, active responding, immediate confirmation, self-pacing and student testing. Knowing at once whether a response is correct reinforces right answers and prevents errors from being practised, which is the principle of immediate confirmation.

**Q44 — (B),** because knowing the learner means finding out what students already know, how they differ and what they need, and letting that shape the lesson. Finishing early, dictating notes and setting hard papers say nothing about whether teaching has been fitted to the learners.

**Q45 — (C),** because the CBCS guidelines divide ability enhancement courses into Ability Enhancement Compulsory Courses, namely Environmental Science and English or Modern Indian Language Communication, and Skill Enhancement Courses, which are value-based or skill-based. Core and elective courses form separate categories.

**Q46 — (D),** because the teacher is looking for the cause of a persistent, specific difficulty, which is the purpose of diagnostic evaluation. Formative evaluation would simply have shown that subtraction was weak. Diagnosis goes deeper into the nature of the error and leads to remedial teaching.

**Q47 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. Morrison's formula was 'pre-test, teach, test the result, adapt the procedure, teach and test again to the point of actual learning', that is, mastery. In recitation the pupil presents the organised unit orally in his or her own words; verbatim reproduction would be memory level work.

**Q48 — (A),** because interpersonal intelligence is the ability to understand other people's feelings, motives and intentions and to work with them. Intrapersonal intelligence is understanding of oneself. The two together are sometimes called the personal intelligences and are related to emotional intelligence.

**Q49 — (C),** because NPTEL offers online courses prepared by the IITs and IISc. Shodhganga, maintained by INFLIBNET, is a repository of Indian doctoral theses. Virtual Labs provides remote, simulation-based experiments. The National Digital Library of India, developed at IIT Kharagpur, gives single-window access to learning resources.

**Q50 — (A),** because conditioning merely links a response to a stimulus. Training builds a skill through practice. Instruction imparts organised knowledge. Teaching goes furthest, aiming at understanding, reasoning and values. The continuum therefore runs conditioning, training, instruction, teaching.

**Level:** Moderate     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

(B)	Both Statement I and Statement II are false

(C)	Statement I is true, but Statement II is false

(D)	Statement I is false, but Statement II is true

**5.**	In the Herbartian steps, 'comparison' or 'association' means:

(D)	applying the derived rule to fresh examples

**6.**	In Flanders' interaction analysis, the observer records a category number once every:

(A) 3 seconds	(B) 10 seconds

**7.**	A test item suited to the understanding level of teaching asks students to:

(A)	reproduce a definition in the exact words taught

(B)	propose and defend an original theory of their own

## **Answer Key: Set 8**

| 1  **A** | 2  **C** | 3  **C** | 4  **A** | 5  **C** | 6  **A** | 7  **C** | 8  **D** | 9  **A** | 10  **B** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 11  **D** | 12  **D** | 13  **C** | 14  **B** | 15  **C** | 16  **C** | 17  **A** | 18  **B** | 19  **D** | 20  **B** |
| 21  **B** | 22  **A** | 23  **D** | 24  **B** | 25  **D** | 26  **C** | 27  **B** | 28  **C** | 29  **B** | 30  **D** |
| 31  **B** | 32  **D** | 33  **B** | 34  **D** | 35  **C** | 36  **C** | 37  **A** | 38  **A** | 39  **D** | 40  **D** |
| 41  **C** | 42  **C** | 43  **A** | 44  **A** | 45  **B** | 46  **A** | 47  **A** | 48  **B** | 49  **D** | 50  **D** |

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 8**

**Q1 — (A),** because in the rotation model students rotate, on a schedule or at the teacher's discretion, between online learning and other modes such as small-group teaching. In the flex model online learning is the backbone, and in the enriched virtual model most work is done remotely.

**Q2 — (C),** because while writing on the board the teacher loses eye contact with the class, and what is written must be rubbed off and cannot be stored. Against this, the chalkboard is cheap, always available, needs no power and lets a diagram grow step by step.

**Q3 — (C),** because the blueprint, or table of specifications, relates content to objectives and often to forms of questions. It ensures that the test samples the course in proper proportion and so supports content validity. A marking scheme and scoring key are prepared for the items once written.

**Q4 — (A),** because both statements are true. New information is always interpreted in the light of what the learner already knows, which is Ausubel's central point. Physical condition matters too: a tired or unwell learner cannot sustain attention, and without attention little is taken in.

**Q5 — (C),** because Herbart's theory of apperception holds that new ideas are learnt by being linked to the mass of ideas already in the mind. In the comparison step the teacher helps pupils connect and compare the facts just presented with what they already know, which prepares for generalisation.

**Q6 — (A),** because the observer notes the category of the ongoing verbal behaviour every three seconds, giving about twenty tallies a minute. The sequence of numbers is entered in a ten-by-ten matrix, from which ratios such as teacher talk, student talk and indirect to direct influence are calculated.

**Q7 — (C),** because understanding level outcomes are seeing relationships, explaining in one's own words and applying a principle in situations like those studied. Exact reproduction and ordered recitation test memory. Producing and defending an original theory goes beyond understanding to the reflective level.

**Q8 — (D),** because CGPA is the credit-weighted mean of the SGPAs. Semester I contributes 20 × 7.0 = 140 credit points and Semester II 30 × 8.0 = 240. Total credit points = 380 and total credits = 50, so CGPA = 380 ÷ 50 = 7.6. The simple average, 7.5, ignores the unequal credits.

**Q9 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Teaching is called purposeful precisely because the teacher plans and acts with definite objectives in view, namely desired changes in the learner. Without such objectives an activity may be talk or entertainment, but it is not teaching.

**Q10 — (B),** because spatial intelligence is the capacity to perceive the visual world accurately and to transform and re-create it in the mind. Architects, sculptors, navigators and chess players rely on it. Bodily-kinaesthetic intelligence concerns skilled use of the body, as in dance or surgery.

**Q11 — (D),** because remote proctoring lets candidates take a test from their own location while software or a human invigilator watches them through the webcam, microphone and screen. It verifies identity and flags suspicious behaviour, serving the purpose that an invigilator serves in an examination hall.

**Q12 — (D),** because andragogy treats the learners' experience as a resource and prefers problem-centred, participatory methods. A case discussion lets adults bring their own experience to a realistic problem. Dictation, drill and lecture-with-recall treat learners as dependent receivers of content.

**Q13 — (C),** because on an OMR sheet a darkened circle cannot be altered, whereas in a computer-based test an answer can be changed, cleared or marked for review until the test is submitted. The on-screen palette also shows which questions remain unanswered.

**Q14 — (B),** because flexible grouping lets students work at times with others of similar level and at times in mixed groups, and graded assignments give each an appropriate challenge. Uniform treatment looks fair but ignores real differences in readiness, so it leaves some bored and others lost.

**Q15 — (C),** because the rule is given first and then applied to particular cases, which is deduction: from general to particular. In the inductive method students would measure several triangles and arrive at the formula themselves. Deduction is quicker and gives practice, but involves less discovery.

**Q16 — (C),** because the first statement interprets a score and passes a judgement of worth ('very good progress'), which is evaluation. The other three only report quantities: marks, number of items and time. Measurement supplies the data on which evaluation is based.

**Q17 — (A),** because the UGC's ten-point scale runs O (Outstanding, 10), A+ (Excellent, 9), A (Very Good, 8), B+ (Good, 7), B (Above Average, 6), C (Average, 5), P (Pass, 4) and F (Fail, 0). A student absent from the examination is given Ab, with zero grade point.

**Q18 — (B),** because facilities are enabling conditions, not causes of good teaching. Their effect depends on the teacher's skill and readiness to integrate them into lessons. Institutions therefore need to provide training and support along with equipment.

**Q19 — (D),** because comments on a draft tell the student what to improve while there is still time to improve it, which is the essence of formative assessment. Final results, scholarship ranking and entrance tests use assessment to make decisions about students, not to guide their ongoing learning.

**Q20 — (B),** because in asynchronous learning participants do not have to be online at the same time; they contribute when convenient, as in forums, e-mail and recorded lectures. It allows time for reflection. Synchronous learning requires simultaneous presence, as in a live class.

**Q21 — (B),** because tasks a learner can do unaided are at the level of actual development. Tasks that cannot be done even with help lie beyond the zone. Tasks that can be done with guidance fall within the zone of proximal development, where instruction is most fruitful.

**Q22 — (A),** because when new experience cannot be assimilated, the child is in a state of disequilibrium. Accommodation restores balance at a higher level. Piaget called this self-regulating process equilibration and regarded it as the chief motor of cognitive development.

**Q23 — (D),** because Fred S. Keller's plan divides a course into units that students study at their own pace from written material. Each unit must be mastered, as shown by a test taken with a student proctor, before the next is attempted. Lectures are used only to motivate.

**Q24 — (B),** because when everything depends on a final examination, teachers and students concentrate on likely questions and neglect skills and topics that are not examined. This 'teaching to the test' is an institution-related constraint on the methods a teacher can use.

**Q25 — (D),** because a longer test samples the ability more fully, so chance errors on single items tend to cancel out and scores become more consistent. The Spearman–Brown formula expresses this relation between test length and reliability. Very short tests are usually unreliable.

**Q26 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. The levels are cumulative: understanding presupposes that the relevant facts are known, and reflection presupposes both facts and understanding. A learner cannot think critically about a problem while lacking the information on which the thinking must work.

**Q27 — (B),** because a demonstration teaches through observation, so visibility and audibility are essential. The teacher should rehearse beforehand, proceed step by step at a moderate pace, use simple apparatus and question the class during the demonstration to keep them thinking.

**Q28 — (C),** because the test is meant to measure mathematical ability, but students weak in English will lose marks for a reason unrelated to mathematics. Because something other than the intended ability affects the scores, the test is less valid, even if scoring remains objective.

**Q29 — (B),** because the Concept Attainment Model, based on Bruner's work, trains learners to form concepts by comparing positive and negative examples. Since it concerns how learners handle and organise information, it is placed in the information-processing family, along with the Inquiry Training and Advance Organiser models.

**Q30 — (D),** because Kohlberg's conventional level has two stages: the 'good boy, good girl' orientation, which seeks approval, and the law-and-order orientation. Most adolescents and adults reason at this level. The pre-conventional level is governed by punishment and reward, and the post-conventional by self-chosen principles.

**Q31 — (B),** because Evaluate means making judgements based on criteria and standards, through checking and critiquing. The student is not producing a new design, which would be Create, nor only separating the designs into parts, which would be Analyse; a reasoned judgement of worth is required.

**Q32 — (D),** because the project method, problem-solving and group discussion all make the learner the chief actor, with the teacher as guide, so they are learner-centred. In dictation the teacher controls everything and learners only write, which makes it teacher-centred.

**Q33 — (B),** because statements 1 and 2 are correct. Feedback and linkage with previous knowledge are standard features of good teaching. Statement 3 is wrong because good teaching is flexible, and statement 4 is wrong because success is judged by what students have learnt, not by coverage.

**Q34 — (D),** because Herzberg distinguished hygiene factors, such as salary, security, supervision and working conditions, whose absence causes dissatisfaction, from motivators, such as achievement, recognition, responsibility and the work itself, which produce satisfaction. Improving hygiene factors removes dissatisfaction but does not by itself motivate.

**Q35 — (C),** because analysis separates a whole into its elements so that each can be understood; synthesis puts the elements together again into a meaningful whole. The maxim holds that analysis should come first but that learning is incomplete until it is followed by synthesis.

**Q36 — (C),** because SWAYAM courses are produced through national coordinators. NPTEL handles engineering, CEC non-technology undergraduate education, UGC non-technology postgraduate education, IGNOU out-of-school and diploma learners, NCERT and NIOS school education, IIM Bangalore management, and NITTTR teacher training.

**Q37 — (A),** because in Fleming's model, visual learners prefer diagrams, charts and maps; aural learners prefer listening and discussion; read/write learners prefer information displayed as words, such as lists and notes; and kinaesthetic learners prefer real experience, practice and examples.

**Q38 — (A),** because Gagné's eight types run: signal learning, stimulus–response learning, chaining, verbal association, discrimination learning, concept learning, rule learning and problem solving. Each type is a prerequisite for the next, so concepts must be learnt before rules, and rules before problems can be solved.

**Q39 — (D),** because statements 1 and 2 are correct. An NRT needs variability, so items of middle difficulty that discriminate well are chosen, and a CRT reports whether the standard was reached. Statement 3 is wrong: in a CRT, an item everyone gets right may simply show that teaching succeeded.

**Q40 — (D),** because at the level of precision the learner performs the skill accurately and independently of the model or written directions. Manipulation still depends on instructions, and imitation on a model. Naturalisation is reached only when performance is automatic and needs little conscious attention.

**Q41 — (C),** because multimedia combines several forms of content, namely text, still images, sound, video and animation, usually under computer control and often with interactivity. Because it addresses more than one sense, it can hold attention and suit learners with different preferences.

**Q42 — (C),** because reflection needs freedom: learners must be able to doubt, question, propose and test ideas without fear. An open, democratic climate is therefore a precondition. Fixed sequences, teacher-imposed conclusions and pre-announced answers leave nothing for learners to reflect upon.

**Q43 — (A),** because statements 1 and 3 are correct. Objectives are written from the learner's side and, in Mager's form, include conditions and a criterion. Statement 2 is wrong: 'know', 'understand' and 'appreciate' are vague and cannot be observed directly; verbs such as list, explain or solve are preferred.

**Q44 — (A),** because decisions taken while facing the class, in response to cues from the learners, are part of the interactive phase. Jackson noted that such decisions are quick and spontaneous, unlike the deliberate planning of the pre-active phase or the reflection of the post-active phase.

**Q45 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. The method is hard to use alone because projects take much time and the knowledge gained is incidental and not in systematic order. Who propounded the method is a separate fact.

**Q46 — (A),** because in direct grading the examiner judges performance qualitatively and assigns a grade straightaway. In indirect grading marks are awarded first and then converted into grades, either by fixed ranges (absolute grading) or by position in the group (relative grading).

**Q47 — (A),** because each domain has its own organising principle. The cognitive domain runs from simple to complex mental operations. The affective domain is ordered by the degree of internalisation of a value, and the psychomotor domain by the degree of coordination between mind and muscle.

**Q48 — (B),** because ratio schedules depend on the number of responses, and interval schedules on the passage of time. Reinforcing after a set number of responses, here every fifth, is a fixed-ratio schedule. If the number varied unpredictably around an average, it would be a variable-ratio schedule.

**Q49 — (D),** because the rapporteur notes the main points of the presentations and discussion and prepares the report of the session. The convener organises the event, the chairperson or moderator conducts the session, and a discussant comments critically on a paper.

**Q50 — (D),** because the code of professional ethics for teachers requires just and impartial treatment of all students, particularly in assessment, and discourages private tuition that creates a conflict of interest. Favouring paying students in marks is a breach of fairness, whatever the teacher's punctuality or scholarship.

**Level:** Moderate     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

(B)	Both Statement I and Statement II are false

(C)	Statement I is true, but Statement II is false

(D)	Statement I is false, but Statement II is true

(A) webinar	(B) podcast

(A) presentation	(B) comparison

**7.**	The Consortium for Educational Communication (CEC), which produces educational video programmes for higher education, is an inter-university centre of the:

(A) AICTE	(B) NCERT

**9.**	Team teaching means that:

**10.**	In the description of a model of teaching, the term 'syntax' refers to:

(A)	the roles and relationships of teacher and students

(B)	the materials and facilities that the model requires

(C)	the sequence of phases through which the model proceeds

**11.**	Before a lesson on Buddhism, a teacher gives a short overview that compares it with Hinduism, which the class already knows. In Ausubel's terms this overview is:

(A)	an expository advance organiser

(B)	a summative review exercise

(C)	a mnemonic memory device

(D)	a comparative advance organiser

**12.**	In simulated teaching used for teacher training, a trainee plays by turns the roles of:

(A) teacher, pupil and observer	(B) teacher, principal and parent

(C) author, editor and critic	(D) planner, examiner and clerk

**13.**	Arrange the four processes of observational learning described by Bandura in the correct order:

1\.	Retention

2\.	Motivation

3\.	Attention

4\.	Reproduction
