| 1  **C** | 2  **C** | 3  **B** | 4  **C** | 5  **D** | 6  **D** | 7  **C** | 8  **A** | 9  **D** | 10  **C** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 11  **D** | 12  **A** | 13  **C** | 14  **C** | 15  **B** | 16  **D** | 17  **D** | 18  **A** | 19  **D** | 20  **B** |
| 21  **D** | 22  **D** | 23  **B** | 24  **B** | 25  **A** | 26  **A** | 27  **B** | 28  **A** | 29  **A** | 30  **B** |
| 31  **C** | 32  **B** | 33  **D** | 34  **C** | 35  **B** | 36  **C** | 37  **B** | 38  **A** | 39  **D** | 40  **D** |
| 41  **A** | 42  **C** | 43  **C** | 44  **A** | 45  **B** | 46  **A** | 47  **B** | 48  **A** | 49  **A** | 50  **C** |

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 9**

**Q1 — (C),** because a language laboratory provides audio or computer stations where each learner listens to model speech, records responses and compares them, with the teacher monitoring. It is an instructional facility meant for the oral and aural skills that ordinary large classes cannot practise.

**Q2 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. Instruction can be delivered through programmed texts, computers or broadcasts, so a teacher need not be present. Indoctrination does the opposite of what Statement II says: it presents beliefs as beyond question and discourages critical examination.

**Q3 — (B),** because a podcast is a set of audio (or video) files distributed over the internet for listening on demand, which makes it an asynchronous tool. A webinar is a live online seminar, a blog is a web journal of dated posts, and a wiki is a site that users edit collaboratively.

**Q4 — (C),** because preparation, also called introduction, readies the pupil's mind for new knowledge by recalling related previous knowledge and arousing curiosity; it ends with the statement of aim. Presentation of the new material follows, and then comparison, generalisation and application.

**Q5 — (D),** because a good handout gives the structure, key terms and diagrams, so students can listen and think instead of copying. It also ensures accuracy. It supports the lecture and further reading; it is not meant to replace either.

**Q6 — (D),** because RCEM stands for Regional College of Education, Mysore, where the approach was developed. It uses four categories of objectives, namely knowledge, understanding, application and creativity, and specifies seventeen mental processes in place of action verbs when stating objectives.

**Q7 — (C),** because CEC, New Delhi, is an inter-university centre set up by the University Grants Commission for electronic media. It coordinates the Educational Multimedia Research Centres in universities, produces programmes for undergraduate subjects and is a national coordinator for SWAYAM and SWAYAM PRABHA.

**Q8 — (A),** because mastery learning divides a course into units. After each unit a short formative test shows each student what has and has not been mastered; those who fall short receive corrective help and a second test. The tests carry no grades; grading is done by a later summative test.

**Q9 — (D),** because in team teaching a group of teachers pool their strengths and share responsibility for planning, teaching and evaluating the same students. It allows each topic to be taught by the person best qualified. Students teaching one another is peer tutoring.

**Q10 — (C),** because Joyce and Weil describe each model by its focus, syntax, social system, principles of reaction and support system. Syntax is the order of phases or steps. Roles and relationships form the social system, required materials the support system, and the teacher's way of responding the principles of reaction.

**Q11 — (D),** because Ausubel described two kinds of advance organiser. A comparative organiser is used when the new material is related to something familiar, and points out similarities and differences. An expository organiser is used when the material is wholly new, and supplies a general framework.

**Q12 — (A),** because in simulated teaching a small group of trainees practise among themselves. One teaches, some act as pupils and others observe and record; the roles are then rotated. Each trainee thus experiences teaching, being taught and giving feedback.

**Q13 — (C),** because the observer must first attend to the model, then retain what was seen in memory, then be physically able to reproduce the behaviour, and finally have a reason, or motivation, to perform it. Reinforcement, direct or vicarious, mainly affects this last process.

**Q14 — (C),** because the reflective model follows the pattern of inquiry: a problem is recognised, learners propose tentative solutions, gather and weigh evidence, and accept or reject each hypothesis. The teacher helps to sharpen the problem and may challenge reasoning, but does not hand over the hypotheses or the conclusion.

**Q15 — (B),** because appreciation and respect are matters of feeling, attitude and value, so objectives 1 and 3 belong to the affective domain. Solving equations is an intellectual task in the cognitive domain, and operating a microscope is a manual skill in the psychomotor domain.

**Q16 — (D),** because every SWAYAM course is built on four quadrants: e-tutorials in the form of video lectures, e-content that can be downloaded or printed, self-assessment through quizzes and tests, and an online discussion forum for clearing doubts.

**Q17 — (D),** because statements 1 and 3 are correct. Expectations work as self-fulfilling prophecies, and well-framed, well-distributed questions draw students into the lesson. Statement 2 is wrong: subject knowledge is necessary, but learning also depends on communication, method, motivation and the learners themselves.

**Q18 — (A),** because MOOCs attract very large enrolments because they are open and free, but only a small proportion of those who enrol finish the course. Lack of personal contact, self-discipline and incentives are the usual reasons. Enrolment is unrestricted, video is central, and enrolment is normally free.

**Q19 — (D),** because objective items are of two kinds. In supply-type (recall) items the student writes a word, number or phrase, as in completion and short-answer items. In selection-type (recognition) items the student chooses among given answers, as in true–false, matching and multiple-choice items.

**Q20 — (B),** because the discrimination index is the difference between the numbers answering correctly in the upper and lower groups, divided by the size of one group: (32 − 12) ÷ 40 = 20 ÷ 40 = 0.50. Dividing by 80 gives 0.25, a common slip, and 0.55 is the item's facility value, 44 ÷ 80.

**Q21 — (D),** because under authoritarian leadership boys worked hard only while watched and showed hostility; under laissez-faire leadership they did little. Under democratic leadership, work continued in the leader's absence and members were friendly and satisfied. The findings support a democratic classroom climate.

**Q22 — (D),** because the enactive mode represents knowledge through action. The iconic mode uses images and diagrams, and the symbolic mode uses words and formulae. Bruner recommended that new ideas be met in that order, whatever the age of the learner.

**Q23 — (B),** because a learner who cannot hear well needs information through the eyes: captions, written outlines, visual aids, a front seat for lip-reading and, where needed, sign language interpretation. Audio recordings and oral questions depend on hearing, and Braille and large print serve visual impairment.

**Q24 — (B),** because peer marks can be distorted by friendship, rivalry and vague standards. A rubric gives all markers the same criteria, anonymity reduces bias, and averaging several markers evens out individual errors. Training students with sample scripts helps further.

**Q25 — (A),** because Guilford classified intellectual abilities along three dimensions: the operation performed (such as memory or divergent production), the content operated on (such as symbolic or semantic) and the product that results (such as units or relations). The combinations give 120 or more distinct abilities.

**Q26 — (A),** because Johnson and Johnson list positive interdependence, individual accountability, face-to-face interaction, social skills and group processing as essentials of cooperative learning. Individual accountability means each member's contribution is assessed, so nobody can hide behind the group, a problem known as free riding.

**Q27 — (B),** because in Knowles's comparison the pedagogical model assumes a dependent learner, subject-centred content and external motivation, while the andragogical model assumes a self-directing learner, problem-centred learning and internal motivation. The other three options reverse one of these contrasts.

**Q28 — (A),** because papers set for different shifts cannot be exactly equal in difficulty, so raw scores are converted, usually into percentile scores, by a statistical procedure called normalisation. Randomisation only shuffles questions, and negative marking and revaluation do nothing to equate different papers.

**Q29 — (A),** because Lee Shulman argued that good teachers possess a special blend of content and pedagogy: the most useful examples, analogies and explanations for a topic, and the difficulties learners usually have with it. It is distinct from subject knowledge and from general teaching skill.

**Q30 — (B),** because Robert Glaser introduced the distinction between norm-referenced and criterion-referenced measurement in 1963, in connection with programmed instruction, where what matters is whether a learner has reached a specified level of performance. Scriven coined the terms formative and summative evaluation.

**Q31 — (C),** because James Marcia described four statuses using two questions: has there been exploration, and has there been commitment? Commitment without exploration is foreclosure. Exploration without commitment is moratorium, neither is diffusion, and commitment after exploration is identity achievement.

**Q32 — (B),** because Knowledge, Comprehension and Application are commonly treated as lower-order thinking, and Analysis, Synthesis and Evaluation as higher-order thinking. Only the first pair lies wholly in the lower group; each of the other pairs contains at least one higher-order level.

**Q33 — (D),** because placement evaluation determines where a learner should begin. Formative evaluation monitors learning during instruction and feeds back to teacher and learner. Diagnostic evaluation looks for the causes of difficulties that persist. Summative evaluation certifies achievement at the end of a course.

**Q34 — (C),** because the scale is O (Outstanding) 10, A+ (Excellent) 9, A (Very Good) 8, B+ (Good) 7, B (Above Average) 6, C (Average) 5, P (Pass) 4 and F (Fail) 0. B+ therefore carries 7 grade points, one below A and one above B.

**Q35 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Memory level teaching is retained because higher levels need a base of facts to work upon. The association with Herbart is a correct but unrelated fact about the origin of the model.

**Q36 — (C),** because the evaluative function concerns judging outcomes against objectives after teaching, and using the result to improve further teaching. Finding entry behaviour is the diagnostic function, and choosing strategy and aids is the prescriptive function.

**Q37 — (B),** because moving up the cone, experience becomes more abstract. Demonstrations, study trips and exhibits lie below motion pictures and are more concrete. Above motion pictures come recordings, radio and still pictures, then visual symbols such as charts and maps, and finally verbal symbols.

**Q38 — (A),** because both statements are true. Assessment is the process of collecting evidence through tests, observation and assignments and making sense of it. Evaluation takes the further step of judging how good the performance or programme is against objectives or standards, and so includes assessment.

**Q39 — (D),** because at the level of Organisation the learner brings different values together, resolves conflicts among them and begins to build an internally consistent value system. Valuing concerns a single value. Characterisation is reached only when the organised system has controlled behaviour consistently over a long time.

**Q40 — (D),** because self-determination theory holds that people are intrinsically motivated when they feel that they act by choice (autonomy), that they are effective (competence) and that they are connected to others (relatedness). The other sets come from Maslow, McClelland and Alderfer respectively.

**Q41 — (A),** because micro-teaching trains skills one at a time, but real teaching needs them together. Link practice, or integration of skills, bridges the gap: the trainee teaches lessons of intermediate length to a group of intermediate size, using several skills in combination.

**Q42 — (C),** because field-independent people can separate an item from its background, so they approach tasks analytically and are comfortable with self-directed work. Field-dependent learners see the situation as a whole, attend to social cues and prefer group learning with clear guidance.

**Q43 — (C),** because the law of exercise states that a connection between stimulus and response is strengthened by use and weakened by disuse. Thorndike later modified it, noting that practice helps only when accompanied by knowledge of results. Drill and revision in classrooms rest on this law.

**Q44 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. The economy of the lecture lies in the ratio of teachers to learners and in the amount of content covered per hour. Because one teacher can reach hundreds at once, cost and time per student are low.

**Q45 — (B),** because a single paper presented and then examined in depth by the group is the pattern of a seminar. A symposium has several speakers on different aspects of a theme, a panel is a conversation among experts, and a workshop is practical work.

**Q46 — (A),** because set induction opens a lesson by linking it with what learners know. Stimulus variation holds attention through changes such as switching between listening and looking. Reinforcement uses verbal and non-verbal approval. Closure consolidates the lesson by summarising and relating the main points.

**Q47 — (B),** because procedural knowledge is knowledge of how to do something: methods, techniques, algorithms and the criteria for using them. Factual knowledge covers terms and details, conceptual knowledge covers classifications, principles and theories, and metacognitive knowledge is awareness of one's own thinking.

**Q48 — (A),** because Kilpatrick named four types. Producer (constructive) projects make something; consumer (aesthetic) projects aim at enjoyment and appreciation; problem projects answer an intellectual question; and drill projects aim at mastering a skill or body of knowledge.

**Q49 — (A),** because the fort is something students can see and walk through; feudalism is an abstract concept. By giving the concrete experience first and building the concept on it, the teacher moves from concrete to abstract. Beginning with a definition of feudalism would reverse the maxim.

**Q50 — (C),** because at the understanding level the teacher explains relationships and the learner grasps them, so the thinking is largely the teacher's. Originality and independent judgement are not demanded. The other options are untrue: this level is built on relationships and previous knowledge, and it can be tested.

# **Set 10**

**Level:** Moderate     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

**1.**	Herbart's view that new knowledge is taken in by relating it to ideas already present in the mind is called:

(A) connectionism	(B) apperception

(C) conditioning	(D) equilibration

**2.**	The shift from a teacher-centred to a learner-centred approach is best described as a shift from:

(B)	facilitating construction of knowledge to transmitting content

(C)	formative assessment to end-of-course assessment only

(D)	cooperative group work to individual competitive work

**3.**	The method in which two teams argue for and against a stated proposition, following agreed rules, is a:

(A) panel discussion	(B) symposium

(C) colloquium	(D) debate

**4.**	Consider the following statements about Piaget's theory:

2\.	Egocentrism is typical of the pre-operational stage.

3\.	Children are passive recipients of knowledge.

4\.	Formal operations begin at about two years of age.

Which of the statements given above are correct?

(A) 1 and 3 only	(B) 1 and 2 only

(C) 2 and 4 only	(D) 1, 2 and 4 only

**5.**	Students who believe that their results depend on their own effort, whatever luck or the teacher's favour may bring, are said to have:

(A)	an internal locus of control

(B)	an external locus of control

(C)	a state of learned helplessness

(D)	a field-dependent cognitive style

**6.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:

**Reason:** Essay-type tests sample the syllabus more widely than objective-type tests.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

**7.**	In Flanders' ten-category system, the number of categories that record student talk is:

(A) 1	(B) 2

(C) 3	(D) 7

**8.**	A teaching aid that appeals to both the ear and the eye at once is the:

(A) tape recorder	(B) silent filmstrip

(C) flannel board	(D) sound film

**9.**	'Teaching is an intimate contact between a more mature personality and a less mature one, which is designed to further the education of the latter.' This definition was given by:

(A) N. L. Gage	(B) B. O. Smith

(C) H. C. Morrison	(D) Edmund Amidon

**10.**	Which tool best supports a group of students in jointly writing and editing a shared set of web pages?

(A) A podcast	(B) A webcast

(C) A screencast	(D) A wiki

**11.**	Calling the teacher the 'independent variable' in teaching means that the teacher:

(A)	is free to plan, organise and manipulate the teaching process

(B)	is answerable neither to the institution nor to the students

(C)	has no influence at all on the learning of the students

(D)	works without any reference to a prescribed curriculum

**12.**	Which of the following distinguishes formative from summative evaluation?

(A)	Its questions are always set and marked by an external agency

(B)	Its results are used to modify instruction still in progress

(C)	Its coverage always extends to the whole course

(D)	Its results are always reported as a final grade

**13.**	Studies of attention suggest that a lecture should be broken by a question or activity about every:

(A) 2 to 3 minutes	(B) 15 to 20 minutes

(C) 40 to 45 minutes	(D) 55 to 60 minutes

**14.**	In Kolb's model, the learner who combines abstract conceptualisation with active experimentation and excels at the practical application of ideas is the:

(A) diverger	(B) assimilator

(C) accommodator	(D) converger

**15.**	Using the classical formula for IQ, a child whose chronological age is 10 years and whose mental age is 12 years has an IQ of:

(A) 83	(B) 100

(C) 110	(D) 120

**16.**	Arrange the following steps of the evaluation process in the correct order:

1\.	Interpreting the results

2\.	Formulating the objectives

3\.	Providing learning experiences

4\.	Applying tools of evaluation

Choose the correct order:

(A) 2, 4, 3, 1	(B) 3, 2, 4, 1

(C) 2, 3, 4, 1	(D) 2, 3, 1, 4

**17.**	'Finish the assignment first; then you may go to the playground.' This use of a preferred activity to reinforce a less preferred one is the:

(A) law of readiness	(B) shaping technique

(C) Premack principle	(D) token economy

**18.**	Anita Harrow's classification of psychomotor behaviour ends, at its top, with:

(A)	non-discursive communication

(B)	origination

(C)	naturalisation

(D)	characterisation by a value complex

**19.**	A holistic rubric is one that:

## **Answer Key: Set 10**

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 11  **A** | 12  **B** | 13  **B** | 14  **D** | 15  **D** | 16  **C** | 17  **C** | 18  **A** | 19  **A** | 20  **C** |
| 21  **B** | 22  **D** | 23  **B** | 24  **C** | 25  **D** | 26  **A** | 27  **D** | 28  **C** | 29  **A** | 30  **A** |
| 31  **C** | 32  **A** | 33  **D** | 34  **B** | 35  **C** | 36  **A** | 37  **D** | 38  **C** | 39  **B** | 40  **C** |
| 41  **D** | 42  **D** | 43  **B** | 44  **C** | 45  **B** | 46  **A** | 47  **A** | 48  **D** | 49  **B** | 50  **B** |

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 10**

**Q1 — (B),** because Herbart held that the mind consists of an 'apperceptive mass' of existing ideas, and that learning is the assimilation of new ideas into it. The formal steps of teaching were designed to secure this linkage. Connectionism is Thorndike's theory and equilibration is Piaget's term.

**Q2 — (A),** because in the learner-centred approach, learning is seen as something the learner constructs through activity, and the teacher's task changes from delivering content to designing and guiding that activity. Continuous assessment and cooperation are also features of the learner-centred approach.

**Q3 — (D),** because a debate has a motion, two opposing sides, a fixed order of speakers and time limits. It trains students to gather evidence and argue logically. Members of a panel exchange views without taking fixed opposing sides, and symposium speakers present different aspects of a theme.

**Q4 — (B),** because statements 1 and 2 are correct. The sequence of stages is invariant, though ages vary, and egocentrism marks pre-operational thought. Statement 3 contradicts Piaget's view of the child as an active constructor of knowledge, and formal operations begin at about eleven years, not two.

**Q5 — (A),** because Julian Rotter's concept of locus of control refers to where people place the causes of what happens to them. Those with an internal locus feel responsible for outcomes and tend to work harder and persist. Learned helplessness is the belief that nothing one does matters.

**Q6 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Marks for essays vary from examiner to examiner because scoring depends on judgement. Essay tests contain few questions and so sample the syllabus narrowly; it is objective tests, with many short items, that sample it widely.

**Q7 — (B),** because categories 1 to 7 record teacher talk (four indirect and three direct), categories 8 and 9 record student talk (response and initiation), and category 10 records silence or confusion. Student talk therefore has two categories, against seven for the teacher.

**Q8 — (D),** because an audio-visual aid appeals to both hearing and sight, as a sound film or television does. A tape recorder is an audio aid, and a silent filmstrip and a flannel board are visual aids. Aids involving more senses generally produce better retention.

**Q9 — (C),** because the definition is Morrison's (1934) and stresses the personal relationship in teaching. Gage defined teaching as interpersonal influence, B. O. Smith as a system of actions intended to induce learning, and Amidon as an interactive process, chiefly classroom talk between teacher and pupils.

**Q10 — (D),** because a wiki is a website whose pages any authorised user can create and edit through a browser, with a history of changes. It is therefore suited to collaborative writing. Podcasts, webcasts and screencasts deliver audio or video from one source to many listeners.

**Q11 — (A),** because an independent variable is the one that is manipulated to bring about change in another. In teaching, the teacher plans, organises, leads and controls the process, and the learner's behaviour changes as a result. The term describes this functional role, not freedom from accountability.

**Q12 — (B),** because the defining feature of formative evaluation is its use: findings flow back at once to improve teaching and learning. External setting, whole-course coverage and final grades are typical of summative evaluation. The same test could serve either purpose, depending on how its results are used.

**Q13 — (B),** because listeners' attention is high at the start of a lecture and declines after about fifteen to twenty minutes. Lecturers are therefore advised to divide the hour into segments and insert a question, short task or change of medium between them, which restores attention.

**Q14 — (D),** because Kolb's four styles arise from two dimensions. Convergers (thinking and doing) apply ideas to practical problems. Divergers (feeling and watching) are imaginative. Assimilators (thinking and watching) build theories. Accommodators (feeling and doing) learn by hands-on trial and rely on intuition.

**Q15 — (D),** because the classical formula is IQ = (mental age ÷ chronological age) × 100. Here 12 ÷ 10 = 1.2, and 1.2 × 100 = 120. Dividing the other way round, 10 ÷ 12, gives about 83, which is the usual slip. An IQ of 100 means mental age equals chronological age.

**Q16 — (C),** because evaluation begins with objectives, which state the changes sought. Learning experiences are then provided to bring those changes about. Suitable tools are applied to gather evidence of change, and the results are interpreted and used. Objectives, learning experiences and evaluation form a triangle.

**Q17 — (C),** because David Premack showed that a behaviour a person performs often can reinforce one performed less often. It is popularly called 'Grandma's rule'. Shaping reinforces successive approximations to a target behaviour, and a token economy uses tokens exchangeable for rewards.

**Q18 — (A),** because Anita Harrow's levels are reflex movements, basic fundamental movements, perceptual abilities, physical abilities, skilled movements and non-discursive communication, which means expressive and interpretive movement as in dance. Origination is the highest level in Simpson's scheme, naturalisation in Dave's, and characterisation in the affective domain.

**Q19 — (A),** because a holistic rubric describes levels of overall quality, and the assessor chooses the level that best fits the work, giving one score. It is quick but gives little diagnostic feedback. Separate scores on several criteria are the mark of an analytic rubric.

**Q20 — (C),** because blended learning keeps the social presence, immediate feedback and practical work of the classroom and adds the flexibility, variety and self-pacing of online study. It still needs a campus, devices and, above all, careful planning of what is done in each mode.

**Q21 — (B),** because Skinner held that learning is most efficient when the learner responds correctly and is reinforced at every step. Small steps make correct responses almost certain, so errors are rare. Branching is a feature of Crowder's programmes, where errors are used for diagnosis.

**Q22 — (D),** because Statement I is false and Statement II is true. Induction is slow because learners must examine many examples before reaching the rule; deduction is quicker because the rule is given. Deduction is therefore well suited to applying, practising and revising rules once they have been established.

**Q23 — (B),** because credit points are 4 × 9 = 36, 4 × 7 = 28 and 2 × 5 = 10, a total of 74. Total credits are 4 + 4 + 2 = 10. SGPA = 74 ÷ 10 = 7.4. The simple average of 9, 7 and 5 is 7.0, which wrongly gives the 2-credit course the same weight as the others.

**Q24 — (C),** because simple sentences before compound ones is simple to complex. Beads before number symbols is concrete to abstract. The whole poem before its stanzas is whole to part. Several measured triangles before the rule is particular to general, the basis of the inductive method.

**Q25 — (D),** because Krathwohl ordered the affective levels by internalisation: the extent to which an attitude or value has become part of the person. At Receiving the value is external; by Characterisation it directs the person's behaviour. Complexity orders the cognitive domain and coordination the psychomotor domain.

**Q26 — (A),** because when people are rewarded for something they already like doing, they may come to see the reward as their reason for doing it, and intrinsic interest declines once the reward is withdrawn. This overjustification effect is a warning against the heavy use of extrinsic rewards.

**Q27 — (D),** because Bigge described a continuum running from 'thoughtless' to 'thoughtful' teaching. Memory level teaching demands least thought, understanding more, and reflective level the most. The levels are defined by this cognitive demand, not by external features such as time or class size.

**Q28 — (C),** because the nine events are: gaining attention, informing learners of the objective, stimulating recall of prior learning, presenting the stimulus, providing learning guidance, eliciting performance, providing feedback, assessing performance and enhancing retention and transfer. They guide the design of a lesson.

**Q29 — (A),** because Universal Design for Learning plans variety into a course from the start: content is presented in several ways, learners are engaged in several ways and they may show learning in several ways. This benefits all learners and reduces the need for later individual accommodation.

**Q30 — (A),** because Anderson and Krathwohl set out a taxonomy table with four types of knowledge (factual, conceptual, procedural, metacognitive) on one axis and six cognitive processes (Remember to Create) on the other. An objective is classified by the cell in which its noun and verb fall.

**Q31 — (C),** because because courses are expressed in credits on a common pattern, a student who moves to another institution can have the credits already earned counted towards the programme there. This mobility is one of the stated aims of the Choice Based Credit System.

**Q32 — (A),** because workload is an institution-related factor. Time spent on clerical and administrative tasks is taken from lesson preparation, feedback on assignments and contact with students, and it adds to fatigue. It has no effect on admissions or on the syllabus.

**Q33 — (D),** because a norm-referenced test exists to rank examinees, and an item answered correctly (or wrongly) by all contributes nothing to differences among scores. Such items are dropped. In a criterion-referenced test the same item might be retained, since discrimination is not its purpose.

**Q34 — (B),** because Havighurst listed tasks that society expects individuals to master at each stage. For adolescence these include accepting one's body, achieving emotional independence from parents, forming mature relations with peers and preparing for a career. Success leads to happiness and to success with later tasks.

**Q35 — (C),** because Gestalt psychologists, namely Wertheimer, Köhler and Koffka, held that we perceive and learn organised wholes, not separate elements. In teaching this supports presenting the overall pattern first and explains learning by insight. Behaviourism and connectionism analyse learning into stimulus–response units.

**Q36 — (A),** because both statements are true. The outcomes of reflective teaching, such as reasoning, originality and judgement, show themselves in extended answers, so essay-type tests are used. Since learners must question, propose and test ideas, the classroom must be open, with the teacher acting democratically.

**Q37 — (D),** because portfolios are of three main kinds. A working portfolio holds work in progress, a showcase (or display) portfolio holds the pieces the student selects as best, and an assessment portfolio holds work chosen to show achievement of stated objectives.

**Q38 — (C),** because objectives are fixed first and strategies chosen to achieve them; both are pre-active operations. Reinforcement is given during the interactive phase. Changing the strategy after examining test results is a post-active operation, which then feeds into the planning of the next lesson.

**Q39 — (B),** because problem-based learning was introduced in the medical school of McMaster University, Canada, in the late 1960s by Howard Barrows and colleagues. Students meet a patient problem first and learn the relevant science in order to deal with it. The case method is linked with Harvard and micro-teaching with Stanford.

**Q40 — (C),** because scholastic areas cover the curricular subjects and the intellectual abilities developed through them. Co-scholastic areas cover life skills, attitudes, values, health and physical education, and literary, artistic and cultural activities. CCE asked schools to assess both regularly.

**Q41 — (D),** because a SPOC uses MOOC-style materials, such as video lectures and automated quizzes, for a limited, selected group, typically the students of one institution. It allows closer interaction and is often used for blended teaching on campus.

**Q42 — (D),** because teachers come to know personal information about students and are expected to keep it private unless disclosure is needed to protect the student or others. Betraying a confidence damages trust, on which guidance and counselling depend. The other options name different professional obligations.

**Q43 — (B),** because prognosis means forecast. A prognostic test estimates how well a person is likely to do in a particular subject or course in future, and is used in guidance and selection. Locating the causes of present errors is the work of a diagnostic test.

**Q44 — (C),** because an aid that cannot be seen or understood by the class fails in its purpose. Support material should be large and clear enough for all, simple, accurate and matched to the learners' level. Novelty, cost and durability are secondary considerations.

**Q45 — (B),** because statements 1 and 3 are correct: the cognitive levels are cumulative and hierarchical, so that evaluation, for instance, presupposes the lower levels. Statement 2 is wrong. Handbook I (1956) dealt with the cognitive domain; the affective domain appeared as Handbook II in 1964.

**Q46 — (A),** because explaining a familiar fact by means of a principle calls for seeing a relationship, which is understanding. Stating the density and naming the states of matter call only for recall. Designing a test requires independent, original thinking and belongs to the reflective level.

**Q47 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Teaching is a two-way process, and the teacher can match pace, examples and method to the class only by knowing how the class is responding. Questions, expressions and test results all supply this feedback.

**Q48 — (D),** because Knowles described self-directed learning as a process in which individuals take the initiative in diagnosing their needs, setting goals, finding resources, choosing strategies and evaluating outcomes. Incidental learning is unplanned, and programmed learning follows a path fixed by the programme writer.

**Q49 — (B),** because synchronous tools need everyone present at the same time: live video classes, webinars and instant chat. Asynchronous tools can be used at different times: discussion boards, e-mail and recorded lectures. Only the first pair assigns both tools correctly.

**Q50 — (B),** because a construct is a theoretical quality, such as creativity, intelligence or anxiety, that cannot be observed directly. Construct validity is the degree to which test scores reflect that quality, shown through accumulated evidence such as correlations with related measures and factor analysis.

**Level:** Tough     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

**16.**	Match the families of models of teaching in List I with the models in List II:

| **List I (Family)** | **List II (Model)** |

| 1. Information-processing | i. Non-directive teaching |

Choose the correct matching:

(A) 1-iii, 2-iv, 3-i, 4-ii	(B) 1-iv, 2-iii, 3-i, 4-ii

## **Answer Key: Set 11**

| 1  **B** | 2  **B** | 3  **A** | 4  **D** | 5  **C** | 6  **C** | 7  **D** | 8  **A** | 9  **A** | 10  **D** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 11  **A** | 12  **B** | 13  **B** | 14  **C** | 15  **B** | 16  **A** | 17  **C** | 18  **B** | 19  **D** | 20  **C** |
| 21  **D** | 22  **C** | 23  **B** | 24  **D** | 25  **C** | 26  **A** | 27  **B** | 28  **D** | 29  **A** | 30  **C** |
| 31  **B** | 32  **A** | 33  **D** | 34  **A** | 35  **B** | 36  **D** | 37  **D** | 38  **C** | 39  **A** | 40  **A** |
| 41  **D** | 42  **B** | 43  **C** | 44  **A** | 45  **D** | 46  **A** | 47  **D** | 48  **C** | 49  **B** | 50  **C** |

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 11**

**Q1 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Participation falls in a threatening climate because students fear ridicule or punishment, which is a psychological matter. The Reason is about the physical environment, a different component of the learning environment.

*Why not (A): both statements concern the classroom environment, but one is about its emotional side and the other about its physical side.*

**Q2 — (B),** because statements 1, 3 and 4 are correct. Gardner proposed seven relatively autonomous intelligences in 1983 and later added the naturalistic, and he criticised schools for valuing only linguistic and logical abilities. Statement 2 is wrong: the theory was a reaction against describing intelligence by one score.

*Why not (A): statement 4 is true as well; the original seven did not include naturalistic intelligence.*

**Q3 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Rote material stands isolated; with nothing to connect it to existing ideas it is soon forgotten and cannot be recognised as relevant in new situations. Meaningful connections are what make retention and transfer possible.

*Why not (B): the Reason is the psychological cause of the weakness described, not a separate observation about memory.*

**Q4 — (D),** because statements 2, 3 and 4 are correct. Formative evaluation includes informal questioning and observation; summative evaluation decides grades and promotion; and the distinction lies in use, not in the instrument. Statement 1 is wrong because certification is the function of summative evaluation.

*Why not (B): statement 4 is also correct; a unit test is formative if used for feedback and summative if used for grading.*

**Q5 — (C),** because outcome-based education 'designs down' from clear statements of what learners should be able to do at the end. Teaching activities and assessments are then aligned with those outcomes. Starting from content, staff or time and adding outcomes afterwards reverses this logic.

*Why not (A): content-first planning is the traditional practice that outcome-based education was introduced to replace.*

**Q6 — (C),** because being able to reproduce a statement without being able to illustrate it shows that the words have been retained but the meaning has not been grasped. Restating, relating and applying are signs of understanding, and explaining the limits of a law goes further still.

*Why not (B): solving a familiar type of problem with the law already shows application, which is an understanding level outcome.*

**Q7 — (D),** because Tomlinson names four things a teacher can differentiate: content (what is learnt), process (how learners make sense of it), product (how they show what they have learnt) and learning environment. Here content is common and only the means of demonstrating learning varies.

*Why not (B): process refers to the activities through which students come to understand; the choice offered here concerns the final evidence of learning.*

**Q8 — (A),** because the scale assigns O = 10, A+ = 9, A = 8, B+ = 7, B = 6, C = 5, P = 4 and F = 0. P is the lowest passing grade and carries 4 grade points. C (Average) stands one step higher with 5, and B (Above Average) carries 6.

*Why not (C): P is the right grade, but its grade point is 4; the value 5 belongs to C (Average).*

**Q9 — (A),** because the teacher at this level is neither an authority who supplies answers nor a bystander. Her task is to keep inquiry rigorous by raising counter-examples and asking for evidence, so that students revise the conclusion themselves. That preserves both independence and quality of thinking.

*Why not (D): democratic guidance is not passivity; leaving a weak conclusion unchallenged abandons the teacher's role in sharpening inquiry.*

**Q10 — (D),** because the distinguishing features are informal conversation among the experts, absence of prepared papers and the presence of a moderator. In a symposium each speaker delivers a prepared talk on one aspect and the speakers do not converse with one another.

*Why not (A): a symposium also has several experts and an audience, but its speakers present set speeches in turn.*

**Q11 — (A),** because statements 1, 2 and 4 are correct. e-PG Pathshala provides postgraduate e-content, Shodhganga is INFLIBNET's repository of theses, and DIKSHA serves school students and teachers. Statement 3 is wrong: NPTEL was started in 2003 by the IITs and IISc.

*Why not (B): DIKSHA's school focus is correctly stated, so statement 4 must be included.*

**Q12 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Entry behaviour must be known so that new learning can start from where learners are. Subject mastery is a separate basic requirement of teaching and gives no reason for studying the learners.

*Why not (A): two true requirements of teaching placed side by side do not explain each other.*

**Q13 — (B),** because both statements are false. Osborn's rules require that judgement be deferred until idea generation is over, and that the group aim at quantity, on the view that many ideas make good ideas more likely. Unusual ideas and building on others' ideas are also encouraged.

*Why not (D): quality is the eventual aim, but during the session the rule is to seek quantity; quality is sorted out later.*

**Q14 — (C),** because an adaptive test must know the difficulty and discrimination of each item on a scale that does not depend on the particular group tested, so that candidates who answer different items can still be compared. Item response theory provides such parameters; classical statistics are sample-dependent.

*Why not (A): classical difficulty values change with the group tested, so they cannot place candidates who took different items on one scale.*

**Q15 — (B),** because the verb gives the cognitive process and the noun phrase gives the kind of knowledge. 'Apply' is the process. 'The procedure for computing the standard deviation' is knowledge of how to do something, which is procedural. The objective is therefore placed where Apply meets procedural knowledge.

*Why not (A): standard deviation is a concept, but the objective is about carrying out the steps of its computation, which is procedural knowledge.*

**Q16 — (A),** because Suchman's inquiry training model belongs to the information-processing family. Thelen's group investigation model belongs to the social family. Rogers's non-directive teaching is a personal model. Contingency management, based on Skinner's operant conditioning, is a behavioural model.

*Why not (B): both names contain a word for inquiry, but inquiry training trains individual thinking, while group investigation rests on democratic group process.*

**Q17 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. Fleming introduced VARK in 1987. Reviews of research have found little sound evidence for the 'meshing' claim that teaching to a diagnosed style improves results. The defensible lesson is to vary methods for all learners.

*Why not (A): learning styles are widely taught, which makes the second statement look settled, but the supporting evidence is weak.*

**Q18 — (B),** because writing on the board is slow, which keeps the teacher in step with students' thinking, and the argument grows before their eyes and can be altered in response to questions. Photographs, recordings and large prepared tables are better handled by projected or digital aids.

*Why not (D): a table prepared in advance is exactly what a slide shows well; the board's strength is material that unfolds during the lesson.*

**Q19 — (D),** because TPACK (Technological Pedagogical Content Knowledge), proposed by Mishra and Koehler, extends Shulman's pedagogical content knowledge by adding technology. SAMR describes levels of technology use (substitution, augmentation, modification, redefinition). ADDIE and ASSURE are models of instructional design.

*Why not (A): SAMR is also about technology in teaching, but it classifies uses of technology; it does not describe teacher knowledge.*

**Q20 — (C),** because Simpson's order is perception (using sensory cues), set (readiness to act), guided response (imitation and trial under guidance), mechanism (habitual performance), complex overt response, adaptation and origination. Readiness must precede the first guided attempts, and habit comes only after them.

*Why not (A): set means mental, physical and emotional readiness, so it comes before any guided attempt at the act.*

**Q21 — (D),** because the in-basket (or in-tray) exercise is a simulation technique for training decision-making. The trainee handles realistic paperwork under time pressure, and the decisions are then discussed. Sociodrama enacts a social problem, and role reversal has two persons exchange roles.

*Why not (A): sociodrama is also a simulation, but it involves acting out a group problem, not individual decisions on documents.*

**Q22 — (C),** because Carol Dweck distinguishes a fixed mindset (the 'entity' theory that ability is a fixed quantity) from a growth mindset (the 'incremental' theory that ability grows with effort). Those with a fixed mindset avoid challenges to protect their image. Praising effort and strategy encourages a growth mindset.

*Why not (D): 'incremental theory' is the technical name for the growth mindset, the opposite of what the student shows.*

**Q23 — (B),** because listing features is retrieval from memory (Remember). Using a learnt procedure on a new case is Apply. Distinguishing two categories by their features is differentiating, a process of Analyse. Drafting a new clause produces something original, which is Create.

*Why not (A): distinguishing is an analytic act; deciding a hypothetical case by a known procedure is application, not analysis.*

**Q24 — (D),** because item writers tend to qualify the correct answer to make it defensible, and experienced test-takers learn to pick the longest option. The item then rewards test-wiseness instead of knowledge, which lowers validity. Options should be similar in length, grammar and detail.

*Why not (A): a clue helps weak but test-wise examinees to succeed, which lowers discrimination instead of raising it.*

**Q25 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Training is confined to building specific skills through practice; teaching includes this and also aims at understanding, attitudes and judgement. The Reason attributes to each the aim of the other, so it cannot stand.

*Why not (A): the Reason has the right two terms but exchanges their aims; read each half separately before accepting it.*

**Q26 — (A),** because explaining means showing how statements are related. Linking words and phrases such as because, hence, therefore and as a result carry these relationships, and are called explaining links. Without them the listener receives separate facts and must guess the connections.

*Why not (C): planned repetition stresses key points for retention; it does not supply the causal connections that links provide.*

**Q27 — (B),** because the lecture addresses a large group and is controlled by the teacher. The tutorial gives remedial and personal help to a small group. Programmed instruction lets each learner proceed alone at his or her own pace. Brainstorming uses a group to generate ideas freely.

*Why not (A): a tutorial gives individual attention, but it is a small-group meeting with a tutor; self-pacing through frames is programmed instruction.*

**Q28 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Being online together is the mark of synchronous learning. Asynchronous learning frees learners from a common timetable, and recorded lectures and forums are its typical tools, as the Reason states.

*Why not (A): the Reason is a correct description of asynchronous tools, which is exactly why the Assertion is wrong.*

**Q29 — (A),** because the Kuder–Richardson formulas (KR-20 and KR-21) estimate internal consistency from a single administration of a test whose items are scored 1 or 0. Test–retest needs two administrations, parallel forms needs two versions, and the inter-rater method compares scorers.

*Why not (B): test–retest uses one test, but it must be given twice with an interval between the two sittings.*

**Q30 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. A project such as running a cooperative store draws on arithmetic, language, civics and science together, so subjects are naturally correlated. This is possible precisely because the method does not follow a timetable of separate subject periods.

*Why not (A): the Reason describes conventional timetabled teaching, which is what the project method departs from.*

**Q31 — (B),** because empirical knowledge rests on observation and experience; rational knowledge rests on reasons and proof. The lesson starts with what students have observed and then moves to the scientific explanation. The teacher is not building a general rule out of several different cases.

*Why not (A): 'particular to general' would need several particular cases leading to a rule; here one observed fact is given its reasoned explanation.*

**Q32 — (A),** because micro-teaching has three phases. In the knowledge acquisition phase the trainee learns about the skill through reading, discussion and observing a model. In the skill acquisition phase the trainee plans, teaches, gets feedback and re-teaches. In the transfer phase the skill is used in a real classroom.

*Why not (B): skill acquisition begins only when the trainee plans and practises the skill; observing a model precedes it.*

**Q33 — (D),** because shaping is the reinforcement of successive approximations to a desired behaviour. Each small step towards the target is reinforced, and the requirement is raised gradually. It is used when the final behaviour is not yet in the learner's repertoire.

*Why not (A): chaining links a series of separate responses into a sequence; here a single response is being refined step by step.*

**Q34 — (A),** because the regulations call the institution where the student is enrolled the parent institution, and the institution to which the course coordinator belongs, and which offers the course, the host institution. The parent institution incorporates the credits in the student's record.

*Why not (B): the labels are easily exchanged; 'host' refers to the institution hosting the course, not the one hosting the student.*

**Q35 — (B),** because Ausubel saw cognitive structure as hierarchical, with broad concepts at the top. Meaningful learning occurs when new, more specific material is subsumed under a relevant inclusive idea, called a subsumer. Advance organisers are designed to supply or activate such subsumers.

*Why not (A): accommodation is Piaget's term for altering a schema; Ausubel's term for anchoring new material under an existing idea is subsumption.*

**Q36 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Piaget gave ages only as approximations, and later accepted that many adolescents and adults use formal reasoning only in fields they know well. The Reason correctly describes formal operational thought.

*Why not (A): the Reason is a true description of the stage, but it does not make the over-general claim about age and uniformity true.*

**Q37 — (D),** because Statement I is false and Statement II is true. A behavioural objective describes the learner's performance, not the teacher's activity. The second statement has all three of Mager's parts: 'label the parts' (performance), 'on a given diagram' (condition) and 'not more than one error' (criterion).

*Why not (A): Statement I sounds like a lesson aim, but it names what the teacher does and gives nothing observable in the learner.*

**Q38 — (C),** because statements 1 and 2 are correct. Admission and sectioning policies fix class size, and what the institution examines and rewards strongly influences classroom practice. Statement 3 is wrong: subject mastery is a characteristic of the teacher, though institutions can support it through faculty development.

*Why not (D): an institution may recruit or train for subject mastery, but the mastery itself resides in the teacher.*

**Q39 — (A),** because an ordinal scale shows order but not the size of the differences: the gap between first and second need not equal that between second and third. A nominal scale only labels categories. Interval scales have equal units, and ratio scales have a true zero as well.

*Why not (C): ranks look like numbers with equal steps, but the differences between successive ranks are not equal, so the scale is not interval.*

**Q40 — (A),** because the task reproduces the conditions of professional work: a real product, a real audience and a real deadline. That makes it authentic, or performance-based, assessment. It shows whether students can use what they know, which a written test on journalism could not show.

*Why not (B): a portfolio is a collection of work gathered over time; here a single performance task is assessed.*

**Q41 — (D),** because joint planning, teaching and evaluation of the same body of students by a group of teachers is team teaching. Large-group lectures, small-group discussions and independent study are its usual components. A tutorial system supplements one teacher's lectures; it does not involve shared responsibility for the course.

*Why not (B): small groups are used here, but the defining feature is that several teachers share planning, teaching and evaluation.*

**Q42 — (B),** because facility value is the proportion of the combined groups answering correctly: (20 + 10) ÷ (25 + 25) = 30 ÷ 50 = 0.60. The discrimination index is a different quantity: (20 − 10) ÷ 25 = 0.40. The item is of moderate difficulty and discriminates satisfactorily.

*Why not (A): 0.40 is the discrimination index, (20 − 10) ÷ 25, not the facility value.*

**Q43 — (C),** because vicarious reinforcement occurs when an observer's behaviour increases after seeing a model rewarded for it. The observer herself has received nothing. Direct reinforcement would mean that she was praised, and self-reinforcement that she rewarded herself for meeting her own standard.

*Why not (A): the praise went to the classmate, not to her, so the reinforcement is experienced at second hand.*

**Q44 — (A),** because the learning material exists; what is missing is the infrastructure needed to reach it. Networks, terminals, laboratories and libraries are instructional facilities provided by the institution. The case shows that material and facilities are distinct factors and that one cannot substitute for the other.

*Why not (B): the e-journals are the support material, and they are available; the bottleneck is the means of access.*

**Q45 — (D),** because selecting a fixed number from the top depends on rank among candidates, which is norm-referenced. Licensing all who cross a fixed standard depends only on the standard, which is criterion-referenced. In the first case the cut-off mark floats; in the second the number who pass floats.

*Why not (B): both use a cut-off, but only the licensing cut-off is fixed in advance; the admission cut-off is whatever the 100th candidate scores.*

**Q46 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Vygotsky defined the zone with reference to help from adults or more capable peers. A peer tutor who is slightly ahead can give the hints and models that let the tutee do what he or she could not do alone.

*Why not (B): the Reason is the theoretical ground for the benefit claimed, so it is an explanation, not a side fact.*

**Q47 — (D),** because the Golem effect is the negative side of the self-fulfilling prophecy: low expectations lead to treatment that lowers performance. The Pygmalion effect names the positive case, in which high expectations raise performance. Both work through the teacher's behaviour towards the student.

*Why not (A): the Pygmalion effect is the same mechanism in the positive direction; a decline produced by low expectations is the Golem effect.*

**Q48 — (C),** because statements 1, 3 and 4 are correct. Teaching exists for the sake of learning and is judged by it, and learning refers both to the activity of acquiring and to what is acquired. Statement 2 is wrong because much learning takes place through experience, without any teaching.

*Why not (A): statement 4 is also true; 'learning' names both the ongoing activity and its outcome, so it cannot be left out.*

**Q49 — (B),** because because adults see themselves as self-directing, they should share in planning. Because experience is their resource, discussion and case methods are used. Because readiness follows the tasks of social roles, learning should be timed to those tasks. Because their orientation is problem-centred, content is organised around problems.

*Why not (A): readiness concerns when adults are ready to learn (role demands); orientation concerns how content should be organised (around problems).*

**Q50 — (C),** because statements 1, 2 and 3 are correct. Morrison organised content into meaningful units, had pupils assimilate them through supervised study, and required a written outline produced unaided in the organisation step. Statement 4 is wrong: recitation is the last step, presentation the second.

*Why not (A): statement 3 is also true; the unaided written outline is exactly what the organisation step requires.*
