| 1  **A** | 2  **C** | 3  **A** | 4  **A** | 5  **B** | 6  **A** | 7  **C** | 8  **B** | 9  **D** | 10  **D** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 11  **C** | 12  **C** | 13  **D** | 14  **C** | 15  **A** | 16  **B** | 17  **B** | 18  **C** | 19  **B** | 20  **B** |
| 21  **A** | 22  **B** | 23  **A** | 24  **D** | 25  **D** | 26  **B** | 27  **C** | 28  **D** | 29  **D** | 30  **A** |
| 31  **D** | 32  **C** | 33  **A** | 34  **A** | 35  **D** | 36  **A** | 37  **C** | 38  **B** | 39  **B** | 40  **D** |
| 41  **C** | 42  **B** | 43  **C** | 44  **D** | 45  **C** | 46  **A** | 47  **C** | 48  **B** | 49  **D** | 50  **B** |

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 12**

**Q1 — (A),** because e-PG Pathshala offers curriculum-based e-content for postgraduate subjects. Shodhganga is the repository of theses submitted to Indian universities. DIKSHA serves school education, Shodhgangotri holds approved research synopses, and Virtual Labs offers simulated experiments for science and engineering.

*Why not (C): Shodhgangotri holds synopses of research in progress, not completed theses, and DIKSHA is a school-level platform.*

**Q2 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Good teaching locates learners' difficulties and provides help to overcome them. It does so precisely because learners differ in pace, style and background; if all learnt alike, diagnosis and remediation would be unnecessary.

*Why not (A): the Reason would remove the need for diagnosis altogether, so it cannot explain a diagnostic approach.*

**Q3 — (A),** because judging another person's work forces the assessor to apply the criteria, which makes the standards explicit in his or her own mind and improves later work. This is why peer assessment is valued as a learning activity, apart from any marks it produces.

*Why not (B): a lighter marking load is a benefit to the teacher, not to the student who does the assessing.*

**Q4 — (A),** because George Polya, in How to Solve It, gave four steps: understand the problem, devise a plan, carry out the plan, and look back to check the result and reflect on the method. The last step is where the solver learns something to use in future problems.

*Why not (B): a plan cannot be carried out before it has been devised; devising comes second and carrying out third.*

**Q5 — (B),** because in Lewin's field theory, behaviour depends on the person and the environment as that person perceives it; together these form the 'life space'. Learning is a change in the cognitive structure of the life space. The theory directs teachers to the learner's perceived situation, goals and barriers.

*Why not (A): practice and effect belong to Thorndike's connectionism, which Lewin's field theory opposed.*

**Q6 — (A),** because statements 2, 3 and 4 are correct. Slow learners have somewhat low general ability; specific learning disabilities such as dyslexia occur despite normal intelligence; and underachievement is a gap between ability and attainment. Statement 1 is wrong: gifted learners need enrichment or acceleration to stay engaged.

*Why not (C): statement 3 is also true; a specific learning disability is defined by difficulty that general intelligence does not explain.*

**Q7 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. The learner's behaviour is what changes as a result of the teacher's planning. That change is brought about through the content, methods and strategies used, which is exactly why they are called intervening variables.

*Why not (A): Statement II denies the very function that defines an intervening variable.*

**Q8 — (B),** because tutorials exist to deal with individual and small-group difficulties that lectures cannot reach. Finding a misconception and correcting it through targeted work is remediation, supported by diagnosis. No grading, placement or prediction of future performance is involved.

*Why not (A): nothing is being graded or certified; the tutor uses what she finds only to correct the students' learning.*

**Q9 — (D),** because punishment aims to reduce a behaviour. When it works by taking away something pleasant, such as recess, it is negative punishment, also called response cost. Positive punishment adds something unpleasant, such as a reprimand. Negative reinforcement strengthens behaviour by removing something unpleasant.

*Why not (B): negative reinforcement increases a behaviour; here the aim and effect is to decrease talking.*

**Q10 — (D),** because the comparison is Gronlund's. Formative evaluation gives quick feedback and correction. When a difficulty persists in spite of this, diagnostic evaluation is used to find its causes with specially designed tests and observation, so that remedial teaching can be planned.

*Why not (C): prognostic evaluation forecasts future success; it does not look for the causes of a present difficulty.*

**Q11 — (C),** because arriving at the rule from examples is induction; applying the rule to fresh cases is deduction. The two are complementary, and good teaching usually combines them in this order: induction secures understanding of the rule, and deduction gives practice and tests it.

*Why not (B): the first half is inductive, but the application exercises that follow are deductive work.*

**Q12 — (C),** because Jack Mezirow described adult learning that begins with a disorienting dilemma, proceeds through critical reflection on one's assumptions and ends in a changed perspective. Kolb describes a cycle of learning from experience in general, without the idea of transforming a frame of reference.

*Why not (A): Kolb's cycle also starts from experience, but it explains how concepts are formed and tested, not how deep-seated assumptions are overturned.*

**Q13 — (D),** because Comprehension has three sub-categories. Translation changes the form of a communication, interpretation explains or summarises it, and extrapolation extends trends beyond the given data to predict consequences. Estimating future enrolment from the trend of a graph is extrapolation.

*Why not (B): no general rule or method is being applied to a new problem; the student is extending a trend already contained in the data.*

**Q14 — (C),** because a good distractor attracts those who do not know the answer, that is, mostly low scorers. If able students prefer a 'wrong' option, it is probably defensible as an answer or the key is wrong. The item must be reviewed and revised.

*Why not (A): a working distractor draws more low scorers than high scorers; the reverse pattern is a warning sign.*

**Q15 — (A),** because at the memory level facts are committed to memory; at the understanding level relationships explained by the teacher are grasped; at the reflective level a problem is explored critically. Bigge also described an autonomous development level, at which the teacher gives little direction and learners follow their own feelings.

*Why not (C): autonomous development is permissive and feeling-centred; critical exploration of a problem is the mark of reflection.*

**Q16 — (B),** because Gagné named five learned capabilities: verbal information (stating facts), intellectual skills (using rules and concepts), cognitive strategies (managing one's own learning and thinking), motor skills and attitudes (choices of personal action). The fifth, motor skills, has no example in this list.

*Why not (A): applying a rule is an intellectual skill; devising one's own way of remembering is a strategy for managing learning.*

**Q17 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Affective outcomes are hard to evaluate because attitudes and values are internal, develop slowly and can be faked in a test. The number of levels in the taxonomy has nothing to do with this difficulty.

*Why not (A): a true fact about the taxonomy's structure does not account for a difficulty of measurement.*

**Q18 — (C),** because the personal fable is the adolescent's conviction of being special, unique and invulnerable, which leads to risk-taking. It is one of the two forms of adolescent egocentrism described by Elkind. The other, the imaginary audience, is the feeling of being constantly watched and judged.

*Why not (A): the imaginary audience concerns being observed by others; the sense of being immune from harm is the personal fable.*

**Q19 — (B),** because in Skinner's linear programming all learners follow one sequence of small steps. In Crowder's branching programming the response decides the next frame. Gilbert's mathetics uses retrogressive chaining, mastering the last step first. Keller's Personalised System of Instruction uses self-paced units checked by student proctors.

*Why not (A): the two programming styles are reversed in that option; the fixed path is linear and the response-dependent path is branching.*

**Q20 — (B),** because criterion-related validity is of two kinds. When the criterion measure is available at the same time as the test, the evidence is concurrent; when the criterion lies in the future, it is predictive. Here both tests are taken in the same week.

*Why not (A): predictive validity needs a criterion measured later, such as grades at the end of the year.*

**Q21 — (A),** because statements 1, 2 and 3 are correct. Create heads the six processes; the knowledge categories are factual, conceptual, procedural and metacognitive; and Understand covers interpreting, exemplifying, classifying, summarising, inferring, comparing and explaining. Statement 4 is wrong: Synthesis was renamed Create.

*Why not (B): statement 3 is true as well; these are three of the seven processes listed under Understand.*

**Q22 — (B),** because reflective teaching follows the pattern of inquiry. A problem is first recognised and defined. Learners then propose hypotheses, test them against evidence and reasoning, and finally draw conclusions. A conclusion that precedes testing would be opinion, not the outcome of reflection.

*Why not (A): evidence cannot be judged relevant until there is a hypothesis for it to test, so hypotheses come before testing.*

**Q23 — (A),** because in on-demand examination the timing is chosen by the learner, and a unique paper is drawn from a large question bank according to a blueprint. It suits open and distance learning. Adaptive testing changes item difficulty during the test, which is a different idea.

*Why not (B): adaptive testing also draws on a bank, but its defining feature is adjustment to responses, not freedom to choose the date.*

**Q24 — (D),** because in assessment as learning, the learner is his or her own assessor: students monitor their work against criteria and regulate their own learning. In assessment for learning the teacher uses evidence to adjust teaching. Assessment of learning records achievement at the end.

*Why not (A): assessment for learning is also formative, but there the teacher gathers and uses the evidence; here the students do so themselves.*

**Q25 — (D),** because following Tyler, evaluation is seen as part of a triangle. Objectives state the intended changes, learning experiences are provided to produce them, and evaluation finds out how far they have occurred. Each element influences the other two.

*Why not (B): input, process and output describe a system in general; the evaluation triangle is specifically objectives, learning experiences and evaluation.*

**Q26 — (B),** because both statements are false, because each assigns to one type the feature of the other. cMOOCs are the connectivist courses in which learners build and share knowledge across networks. xMOOCs are the structured courses of video lectures and quizzes offered on large platforms.

*Why not (A): both features are real, which makes the statements sound right, but they have been exchanged between the two types.*

**Q27 — (C),** because Synthesis includes the production of a unique communication, of a plan and of a set of abstract relations, such as a hypothesis that accounts for given data. The student puts elements together into something not clearly there before. In the revised taxonomy the same objective would be classified under Create.

*Why not (A): analysis would stop at breaking the result into its elements; proposing a new explanation goes beyond it to synthesis.*

**Q28 — (D),** because Jerome Kagan distinguished impulsive learners, who respond fast and inaccurately, from reflective learners, who respond slowly and accurately; he measured the difference with the Matching Familiar Figures Test. Impulsive learners benefit from being taught to pause and check alternatives.

*Why not (B): field independence concerns separating figure from background, not the speed and accuracy of responding.*

**Q29 — (D),** because Green placed the activities related to teaching on two sides. Training and conditioning shape behaviour; instructing and indoctrinating shape knowledge and belief. On each side the first is closer to teaching because it engages intelligence, while the second moves away from it.

*Why not (C): conditioning and indoctrinating are the two activities farthest from teaching, but they lie on different sides: one shapes behaviour, the other belief.*

**Q30 — (A),** because both statements are true. Even at the memory level, well-organised and patterned material is easier to learn and retain than a jumble of facts, which is why the Herbartian steps stress orderly presentation. The outcome, retention and recall, is evaluated through oral questioning and written recall tests.

*Why not (C): evaluation is not absent at the memory level; recall and recognition tests are its standard instruments.*

**Q31 — (D),** because using another person's work as one's own is plagiarism, a breach of intellectual honesty and of the respect teachers owe to colleagues under the code of professional ethics. Academic freedom protects a teacher's right to teach and publish; it gives no right over other people's work.

*Why not (A): academic freedom is the right to pursue and express ideas; it is not infringed here, and it does not license unacknowledged use.*

**Q32 — (C),** because statements 1 and 3 are correct. A final mastery test is both criterion-referenced and summative, and a score of 70 per cent can be read against a pass mark or converted to a percentile. Statement 2 is wrong: many teacher-made tests are interpreted by ranking the class.

*Why not (D): norm-referencing is a way of interpreting scores, not a property of standardised tests alone, so statement 2 fails.*

**Q33 — (A),** because the SGPA is calculated over all courses taken, including failed ones. Credit points are 4 × 0 = 0, 4 × 8 = 32 and 2 × 6 = 12, a total of 44. Total credits are 10, so SGPA = 44 ÷ 10 = 4.4. Leaving out the failed course gives 44 ÷ 6 = 7.3, which is wrong.

*Why not (B): 7.3 comes from dividing by 6 credits, leaving out the failed course; failed courses stay in the denominator.*

**Q34 — (A),** because statements 1 and 3 are correct. Because teaching behaviour is observable, it can be studied and improved, as in interaction analysis and micro-teaching, and teaching occurs both in formal institutions and informally in the family and community. Statement 2 is wrong: teaching is interactive and depends on feedback.

*Why not (D): statement 2 denies the interactive nature of teaching, so no option containing it can be right.*

**Q35 — (D),** because an aid teaches only when it is integrated with the lesson. The teacher should preview the film, tell the class what to look for, show it (or the relevant part) and then discuss it and relate it to the objectives. Mere screening leaves learning to chance.

*Why not (A): uninterrupted screening treats the film as complete in itself; without preparation and follow-up, students watch passively.*

**Q36 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Bandura showed that people who believe they can succeed choose harder tasks, try harder and continue longer in the face of difficulty, whereas those who doubt themselves give up early. Self-efficacy is thus a learner-related factor in achievement.

*Why not (B): the Reason states the mechanism by which self-efficacy produces persistence, so it is the explanation.*

**Q37 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Class time becomes available for application and discussion precisely because first exposure to content has been moved out of class, to videos or readings studied beforehand. The Reason describes the conventional classroom.

*Why not (A): if the lecture stayed in class, as the Reason says, no time would be freed; the Reason contradicts the model.*

**Q38 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Skills are not developed by lectures because skills require practice by the learner, and in a lecture the learner only listens. Suitability for large groups is a separate feature of the method.

*Why not (A): the size of the audience is not what prevents skill learning; lack of learner practice is.*

**Q39 — (B),** because moving up from the base, Dale's cone places demonstrations, then study trips, then exhibits, followed by television, motion pictures and still pictures. A demonstration is the most concrete of the three, and an exhibit the most abstract.

*Why not (A): a study trip feels the most 'real', but in Dale's cone it lies above demonstrations, not below them.*

**Q40 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Bruner rejected the idea of waiting for readiness; he claimed that any subject can be taught in some intellectually honest form to any child at any stage. The three modes of representation show how content can be adapted to make this possible.

*Why not (A): the three modes are Bruner's means of teaching a subject early, which contradicts the idea of waiting for readiness.*

**Q41 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. Mary Budd Rowe's studies of wait-time showed that longer pauses bring longer, more thoughtful answers from more students. Calling only on volunteers concentrates participation in a few confident students and leaves the rest out.

*Why not (A): volunteers are usually the same few students, so relying on them narrows participation.*

**Q42 — (B),** because Maslow proposed the hierarchy of needs. Weiner explained motivation through the causes to which people attribute success and failure. Atkinson treated achievement behaviour as the result of the motive to succeed and the motive to avoid failure. Bandura introduced self-efficacy, the belief in one's capability.

*Why not (A): Weiner and Atkinson both deal with success and failure, but attribution of causes is Weiner's and the two opposing motives are Atkinson's.*

**Q43 — (C),** because the guidelines describe the dissertation or project as an elective course designed to acquire special or advanced knowledge, which the student studies on his or her own with advisory support from a teacher. It carries credits like any other course.

*Why not (A): many programmes make a project compulsory, but in the guidelines' classification it comes under elective courses, not core courses.*

**Q44 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Technology is a tool; its effect depends on how the teacher designs and uses it, and poorly used slides or videos can make learners more passive. That ICT can combine text, sound, image and animation is a fact, but a capability is not a guarantee.

*Why not (A): the Reason states what ICT can do; the Assertion wrongly turns that possibility into a certainty.*

**Q45 — (C),** because the I/D ratio is the number of tallies in categories 1 to 4 divided by the number in categories 5 to 7: 120 ÷ 240 = 0.5. A ratio below 1 shows that the teacher's influence was more direct than indirect, with lecturing, directing and criticising dominating.

*Why not (D): 2.0 is the inverse, direct divided by indirect, 240 ÷ 120.*

**Q46 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Planning before class allows calm, rational choice, whereas in the classroom events follow one another quickly and the teacher must act immediately on cues from the learners. This pressure of time is what makes interactive decisions spontaneous.

*Why not (B): the Reason gives the cause of the spontaneity, namely lack of time to deliberate, so it does explain the Assertion.*

**Q47 — (C),** because statements 1, 3 and 4 are correct. Those are the usual roles in a seminar; a workshop is defined by hands-on work; and a conference is a larger gathering with many sessions on a broad theme. Statement 2 is wrong: a symposium normally ends with questions from the audience.

*Why not (A): statement 4 is also true and must be included; a conference is broader than a seminar or symposium.*

**Q48 — (B),** because Morrison's formula runs: pre-test, teach, test the result, adapt the procedure, teach and test again to the point of actual learning. Failure to master is treated as a signal to change the teaching, not as a verdict on the pupil.

*Why not (C): re-teaching is required, but the formula says the procedure must first be adapted; the unchanged lesson has already failed once.*

**Q49 — (D),** because Sternberg's triarchic theory has three parts. Analytical (componential) intelligence is used in academic problem-solving, creative (experiential) intelligence in dealing with novelty, and practical (contextual) intelligence in adapting to, shaping and selecting everyday environments. 'Street-smartness' is the third.

*Why not (B): creative intelligence deals with novel tasks and new ideas; adapting shrewdly to everyday settings is practical intelligence.*

**Q50 — (B),** because relative grading distributes grades according to position in the group. If all students are able, small differences in marks are stretched over the full range of grades, and good performers are labelled poor. Absolute grading avoids this by using fixed standards.

*Why not (A): that is the risk of lenient absolute grading; relative grading forces a spread and so cannot give everyone the top grade.*

# **Set 13**

**Level:** Tough     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

**1.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:

**Reason:** Students seated in the back rows always participate more than those in the front rows.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

| --- | --- |

(A)	the teacher's signature on each of the papers

(B)	the inclusion of every single test paper

(C)	the student's reflection on purposefully selected work

(D)	the total number of pages it contains

**50.**	Match the Herbartian steps in List I with the classroom activities in List II:

| **List I (Step)** | **List II (Activity)** |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Preparation | i. Using the rule in a new situation |
| 2. Presentation | ii. Framing a rule from the facts studied |
| 3. Generalisation | iii. Recalling related previous knowledge |
| 4. Application | iv. Introducing the new facts to the class |

Choose the correct matching:

(A) 1-iv, 2-iii, 3-ii, 4-i	(B) 1-iii, 2-iv, 3-ii, 4-i

(C) 1-iii, 2-iv, 3-i, 4-ii	(D) 1-iii, 2-ii, 3-iv, 4-i

## **Answer Key: Set 13**

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 13**

**Q1 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Studies of the classroom 'action zone' show that students in the front and centre tend to interact most with the teacher, and circular seating increases student-to-student talk. The Reason states the opposite of the usual finding.

*Why not (A): the Reason is about seating too, but it gets the direction of the effect wrong.*

**Q2 — (A),** because statements 1, 2 and 3 are correct. Learning on SWAYAM is free, certification needs a fee-based proctored examination, and the platform is designed around access, equity and quality. Statement 4 is wrong: any learner, anywhere, may register.

*Why not (B): statement 3 is also true; access, equity and quality are the stated cardinal principles.*

**Q3 — (A),** because induction goes from particular cases to a general rule, and deduction from the rule to particular cases. Analysis starts from the unknown (what is to be proved) and works back to the known; synthesis starts from the known data and builds up to the unknown.

*Why not (C): analysis begins from what is to be found, the unknown; it is synthesis that proceeds from known to unknown.*

**Q4 — (C),** because acceleration moves a gifted learner through the curriculum faster, by early entry, skipping a grade or taking advanced courses. Enrichment keeps the learner with age-mates but adds depth and breadth. Ability grouping places learners of similar ability together.

*Why not (A): enrichment broadens and deepens work at the same grade level; it does not move the student ahead in time.*

**Q5 — (B),** because Ausubel set three conditions for meaningful learning: logically meaningful material, relevant anchoring ideas in the learner's cognitive structure, and a meaningful learning set, that is, the intention to relate new material to what one knows. A learner bent on verbatim memorising defeats the third condition.

*Why not (A): reception through a lecture does not make learning rote; Ausubel insisted that reception learning can be fully meaningful.*

**Q6 — (C),** because a true–false item can be guessed correctly half the time. Matching items work only for homogeneous sets of related facts. Essay answers are scored subjectively. Multiple-choice items are versatile, but writing three or four plausible distractors for each is difficult.

*Why not (A): distractors belong to multiple-choice items; the limitation of matching items is their restriction to homogeneous material.*

**Q7 — (C),** because laboratory work is where students handle apparatus, measure and observe, so manipulative skills cannot be developed without it. Knowledge of facts and principles can still be gained from lectures, though less vividly. The case shows how instructional facilities limit the objectives a course can achieve.

*Why not (A): understanding suffers somewhat, but concepts can still be explained in lectures; practical skills cannot be acquired at all.*

**Q8 — (A),** because in expectancy–value theory, motivation equals expectancy of success multiplied by the value of the goal. If either is near zero, the product is near zero. A highly valued goal that seems unattainable produces little effort, which is why building confidence matters.

*Why not (C): 'moderate' assumes the two factors are added or averaged; the theory multiplies them.*

**Q9 — (D),** because a video recording shows the trainee exactly what happened, can be replayed and stopped at any point, and is free from the observer's memory and bias. Supervisors and peers supply useful judgements, but these are filtered through perception and may be softened or distorted.

*Why not (A): a supervisor is expert but still reports an impression; the recording preserves the behaviour itself.*

**Q10 — (C),** because in the fishbowl technique the inner group is watched like fish in a bowl; observers study both the content and the process of the discussion and may exchange places with the inner group. A buzz group is a brief simultaneous discussion in many small groups.

*Why not (B): a panel also discusses before an audience, but it consists of experts under a moderator, not students in concentric circles.*

**Q11 — (D),** because outcome-based education works at three levels. Course outcomes state what students can do at the end of a course. Programme outcomes state what they can do at the time of graduation. Programme educational objectives describe what graduates are expected to achieve some years into their careers.

*Why not (A): programme-specific outcomes, like programme outcomes, refer to attainments at the point of graduation, not to achievements in later career.*

**Q12 — (B),** because a ratio scale needs an absolute zero that means complete absence of the quality measured. A test samples only some of the subject, so zero on the test is not zero knowledge, and a score of 80 does not show twice the knowledge of a score of 40.

*Why not (A): scores do show order, since 60 is more than 40; what they lack is an absolute zero.*

**Q13 — (D),** because items 1 and 2 are basic requirements: a teacher must know the subject and be able to communicate it, in addition to understanding learners and methods. Item 3 is not a requirement of teaching at all. Fair evaluation calls for transparency and accountability, not unchecked authority.

*Why not (C): evaluation is part of teaching, but freedom from scrutiny is not a professional requirement.*

**Q14 — (B),** because for the students who took it, the examination comes at the end and decides their grades, so it is summative. For the course, the same results serve as feedback for improvement, which is a formative use. Purpose and user decide the label.

*Why not (C): the examination is summative for the students, but using its results to improve the course is a formative use.*

**Q15 — (B),** because Honey and Mumford's four styles correspond to stages of Kolb's cycle. Activists plunge into new experiences, reflectors observe and think before acting, theorists integrate observations into logical theories, and pragmatists want to try ideas out to see whether they work in practice.

*Why not (C): theorists also think carefully, but their concern is to fit facts into a logical system, not to watch and gather data.*

**Q16 — (A),** because J. L. Moreno distinguished psychodrama, which deals with the private problems of an individual and is used in therapy, from sociodrama, which deals with problems shared by a group or community. Classroom role play on social issues is sociodrama.

*Why not (B): psychodrama centres on one person's personal difficulties; a community problem explored by a group is sociodrama.*

**Q17 — (C),** because when reinforcement comes after an unpredictable number of responses, the learner cannot tell that it has stopped, so responding continues for a long time. Continuous reinforcement gives the fastest initial learning but the quickest extinction, because the absence of reinforcement is noticed at once.

*Why not (A): continuous reinforcement is best for establishing a behaviour, but the behaviour dies out fastest once reinforcement stops.*

**Q18 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Armstrong's heuristic method requires that pupils find things out by their own observation and experiment; the teacher sets the problem and guides but tells as little as possible. Supplying facts and conclusions is the method it opposes.

*Why not (A): if the teacher supplied everything, nothing would be left to discover; the two statements are opposed.*

**Q19 — (B),** because the overhead projector gives a bright image in normal light, so students can take notes, and the teacher faces the class while writing or pointing on the transparency. Slide projectors need a darkened room. Opaque objects are projected by an episcope or epidiascope.

*Why not (A): projection of opaque objects is the work of the epidiascope, not of the overhead projector.*

**Q20 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Understanding level teaching is more thoughtful because it requires learners to see relationships and use principles, not just retain facts. Who developed the model has no bearing on how thoughtful it is.

*Why not (A): a correct attribution to Morrison does not explain why the level demands more thinking.*

**Q21 — (A),** because cultural capital is the stock of knowledge, language habits and familiarity with educated culture that families pass on and that institutions reward. Students without it are not less able; they have had less exposure. Bridge courses and mentoring address this home-background factor.

*Why not (B): aptitude is the potential to learn; these students have the potential but lack the background familiarity that others bring from home.*

**Q22 — (D),** because consistency is reliability, and the test has it. Validity requires that the test measure what it claims, and here it does not. The case shows that reliability is necessary for validity but not sufficient: a test can be consistently wrong, like a scale that always reads two kilograms too much.

*Why not (A): 'valid but not reliable' cannot occur; a test that gives inconsistent scores cannot be measuring anything accurately.*

**Q23 — (D),** because Statement I is false and Statement II is true. Reflective teaching presupposes a fund of knowledge and the capacity for abstract reasoning, so it is not suited to young or unprepared learners. Because learners must investigate and discuss, it is slow, which is one of its recognised limitations.

*Why not (A): the first statement ignores the prerequisites of reflection; immature learners lack the knowledge base it needs.*

**Q24 — (B),** because the standard error of measurement estimates how far obtained scores would spread around a person's true score on repeated testing. With an obtained score of 60 and a standard error of 3, the true score probably lies between 57 and 63. The more reliable the test, the smaller this error.

*Why not (A): the standard deviation describes the spread of scores across the group, not the error in one person's score.*

**Q25 — (A),** because a rubric has three parts: criteria (the aspects of performance to be judged), a scale of levels (such as excellent to poor) and descriptors (the wording in each cell that says what that level of that criterion looks like). Descriptors are what make scoring consistent.

*Why not (B): criteria name the aspects to be judged, such as organisation; descriptors say what each level of an aspect looks like.*

**Q26 — (A),** because both can store and share files. An LMS adds the management of learning: enrolment, assignments with deadlines, quizzes with automatic scoring, grade books and reports on what each learner has done. This tracking function is what the word 'management' refers to.

*Why not (B): storing documents is common to both kinds of service, so it cannot be the distinguishing feature.*

**Q27 — (D),** because centration is the pre-operational child's tendency to focus on one striking aspect of a situation and neglect the others. It is the reason such a child fails conservation tasks. Conservation, reversibility and seriation are abilities that appear later, in the concrete operational stage.

*Why not (A): conservation is the understanding she has not yet reached; the tendency that prevents it is centration.*

**Q28 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. The 1956 taxonomy places Evaluation at the top, above Synthesis; it is the 2001 revision that puts Create (the former Synthesis) above Evaluate. Evaluation was defined as judgement of value using internal evidence or external criteria.

*Why not (A): the order asserted is that of the revised taxonomy; a candidate who mixes the two versions will accept it.*

**Q29 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Morale depends on leadership, recognition, participation in decisions and relations among staff, which make up institutional climate. The fact that the institution fixes the calendar is correct but explains nothing about morale.

*Why not (A): both statements mention the institution, but a scheduling function is not the cause of teacher morale.*

**Q30 — (C),** because reflection starts when learners feel a genuine difficulty: something that does not fit their existing beliefs. Confronting them with contradictory data creates this tension and the wish to resolve it. Recall prepares for a lesson, and dictating definitions or conclusions removes the problem.

*Why not (A): recall of the previous lesson links old and new knowledge, but it creates no perplexity for learners to resolve.*

**Q31 — (A),** because Synectics, developed by William Gordon, uses direct analogy, personal analogy and compressed conflict to break habitual ways of seeing and so generate new ideas. Inquiry training (Suchman) teaches the process of investigating puzzling events, and concept attainment (Bruner) teaches concepts through examples and non-examples.

*Why not (B): inquiry training also starts from something puzzling, but it trains questioning and explanation, not creative use of analogy.*

**Q32 — (B),** because at the level of adaptation, skills are so well developed that the person can modify movement patterns to meet special requirements or new situations. Mechanism is habitual performance of the learnt pattern. Origination, the highest level, is creating entirely new movement patterns.

*Why not (D): origination means inventing a new pattern of movement; here an existing skill is adjusted to a new situation.*

**Q33 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. What learners work out, discuss and apply for themselves is processed more deeply and retained longer. This follows from the learner's activity in such methods; the Reason describes teacher-centred methods.

*Why not (A): the Reason attributes to learner-centred methods the passivity that is typical of teacher-centred ones.*

**Q34 — (D),** because Student Teams–Achievement Divisions (STAD), developed by Robert Slavin, has four steps: teaching, team study, individual quizzes and team recognition based on improvement points. Because every member can earn points by improving, weaker students contribute as much as stronger ones.

*Why not (A): Jigsaw divides the content among members who teach one another; it does not rest on improvement scores from quizzes.*

**Q35 — (D),** because statements 2 and 3 are correct. Individuals differ in every aspect of development, and the differences are both quantitative (more or less of an ability) and qualitative (different interests or styles). Statement 1 is wrong: uniform teaching does not remove differences and usually widens the gaps.

*Why not (C): uniform treatment of unequal learners does not make them equal, so statement 1 cannot be accepted.*

**Q36 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. A model is a recognisable representation, enlarged, reduced or simplified, used when the real thing cannot be brought to class. A specimen is a real object, a sample that represents its class, such as a rock, leaf or insect.

*Why not (A): the description in Statement II fits a model; a specimen is the real thing in sample form.*

**Q37 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Nobody can learn on behalf of another person; the learner must attend and make an effort. Since teaching succeeds only through the learner's activity, arousing and maintaining the will to learn becomes one of its necessary functions.

*Why not (B): the Reason says why motivation must be a function of teaching, so it is an explanation and not a separate fact.*

**Q38 — (D),** because if a student is named first, the rest relax because they know they will not be asked. Asking first and pausing makes everyone prepare an answer. It is a standard component of the skill of questioning, along with clarity, relevance and distribution of questions.

*Why not (B): the pause puts mild pressure on all students, including weaker ones; that is its purpose.*

**Q39 — (A),** because the scale runs O (Outstanding, 10), A+ (Excellent, 9), A (Very Good, 8), B+ (Good, 7), B (Above Average, 6), C (Average, 5), P (Pass, 4) and F (Fail, 0). Plain 'A' is therefore Very Good with 8 points; 'Excellent' and 9 points belong to A+.

*Why not (B): 'Excellent' with 9 points is A+, the grade above A.*

**Q40 — (B),** because Robert Glaser's model has four components in sequence: instructional objectives, entering behaviour, instructional procedures and performance assessment, connected by feedback loops. The learner's entering behaviour must be known before procedures are chosen, since it is the starting point from which the objectives are to be reached.

*Why not (A): procedures are chosen only after the gap between entering behaviour and objectives is known, so they come third.*

**Q41 — (C),** because statements 1, 3 and 4 are correct. Linking new material to what is known is Ausubel's principle; induction moves from particular cases to a general rule; and a learner's first vague notions are made definite through teaching. Statement 2 reverses the accepted maxim, 'from simple to complex'.

*Why not (A): statement 4 is a genuine maxim and correctly explained, so it must be included.*

**Q42 — (A),** because both statements are true. Vygotsky held that instruction aimed at the zone of proximal development awakens functions that are still maturing, so learning leads development. Piaget held that what a child can learn depends on the stage of cognitive development already reached.

*Why not (C): Statement II is a fair summary of Piaget; his stages mark what kinds of learning are possible.*

**Q43 — (B),** because statements 2, 3 and 4 are correct. Receiving covers awareness, willingness to receive and selected attention; Characterisation is the top level; and Krathwohl, Bloom and Masia published the handbook in 1964. Statement 1 is wrong: the order is Receiving, Responding, Valuing, so Responding comes first.

*Why not (C): the date in statement 4 is also correct, so an option that omits it is incomplete.*

**Q44 — (A),** because a percentile rank gives the percentage of the reference group whose scores fall below a given score. It says nothing about the percentage of items answered correctly: on a hard test a student with 45 per cent marks may stand at the 80th percentile.

*Why not (B): percentage of marks and percentile rank are different; the first compares with the maximum, the second with other examinees.*

**Q45 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Late adolescents seek the company and approval of peers and learn readily from one another. Methods that let them work together, such as group projects and discussions, make use of this characteristic.

*Why not (B): the peer orientation of adolescents is the reason group methods suit them, so the Reason does explain the Assertion.*

**Q46 — (D),** because the March 2026 note describes SWAYAM PRABHA as a group of 48 DTH channels telecasting educational programmes round the clock, available free through DD Free Dish. The initiative began in 2017 with 32 channels, the figure still found in older books and on some websites.

*Why not (B): 32 was the number of channels when the initiative was launched; it has since been expanded.*

**Q47 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. The 'system of actions' definition is Smith's. The 'intimate contact between a more mature and a less mature personality' is Morrison's definition. Gage defined teaching as a form of interpersonal influence aimed at changing another person's behaviour potential.

*Why not (A): the second definition is genuine, but it belongs to Morrison, not to Gage.*

**Q48 — (B),** because Knowles recommended the learning contract as a tool of self-directed learning. The learner, in consultation with the facilitator, sets out what will be learnt, how, by when and how it will be judged. It gives adults ownership while keeping the learning accountable.

*Why not (A): a portfolio collects evidence of work done; a contract is drawn up beforehand and specifies what will be done.*

**Q49 — (C),** because a portfolio is purposeful: work is selected against stated criteria, and the student explains why each piece was chosen and what it shows about his or her learning. Collection without selection and reflection is only storage.

*Why not (B): including everything is exactly what makes a folder; a portfolio depends on selection.*

**Q50 — (B),** because preparation recalls previous knowledge and readies the mind. Presentation introduces the new material. After comparison, generalisation draws a rule or principle from the facts. Application uses that rule in fresh situations, which both tests and fixes the learning.

*Why not (C): a rule must be framed before it can be used; generalisation precedes application.*

# **Set 14**

**Level:** Tough     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

**1.**	The two topmost bands of Dale's Cone of Experience, visual symbols and verbal symbols, correspond to the mode of representation that Bruner called:

(A) iconic	(B) enactive

(C) formal	(D) symbolic

**2.**	Under the CBCS, a course has three hours of lectures and two hours of practical work every week through the semester. The course carries:

(A) 3 credits	(B) 4 credits

(C) 5 credits	(D) 6 credits

**3.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:

**Assertion:** Memory level teaching is teacher-centred.

**Reason:** At the memory level, learners themselves select the content and its sequence.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

**4.**	Given below are two statements about the two senses of the word 'teaching':

**Statement I:** In the 'task' sense, a person may be said to teach even when nobody learns.

**Statement II:** In the 'achievement' sense, to say that a person taught something implies that it was learnt.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Statement I and Statement II are true

(B)	Both Statement I and Statement II are false

(C)	Statement I is true, but Statement II is false

(D)	Statement I is false, but Statement II is true

**5.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:

**Assertion:** Teachers are advised to use a variety of methods and media.

**Reason:** Every learner has exactly one fixed style and cannot learn through any other channel.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

**6.**	A seminar session on a research paper passes through the stages listed below. Identify the order in which they take place:

1\.	Open discussion by the participants

2\.	Presentation of the paper

3\.	Summing up by the chairperson

4\.	Introduction of the theme and the speaker

Choose the correct order:

(A) 4, 1, 2, 3	(B) 4, 2, 1, 3

(C) 2, 4, 1, 3	(D) 4, 2, 3, 1

**7.**	Students type their essays on a word processor instead of writing them by hand, with no other change in the task. In the SAMR model, this use of technology is at the level of:

(A) Augmentation	(B) Modification

(C) Redefinition	(D) Substitution

**8.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:

**Assertion:** Formative evaluation provides feedback to the teacher as well as to the learner.

**Reason:** Summative evaluation is used for certification.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

**9.**	Students can solve textbook-type problems on supply and demand correctly, but cannot judge an unfamiliar policy proposal reported in a newspaper. Their learning has stopped at the:

(A) memory level	(B) reflective level

(C) autonomous level	(D) understanding level

**10.**	Test results are used to help a student choose the elective courses best suited to her strengths. The function of evaluation served here is:

(A) guidance	(B) instructional

(C) administrative	(D) research

**11.**	A paper-setter is asked by a close relative to reveal the questions she has set for a university examination. The professional obligation that requires her to refuse is:

(A) loyalty to the family	(B) freedom of expression

(C) autonomy of the teacher	(D) integrity in examination work

**12.**	A student who was humiliated in a mathematics class now feels anxious on entering any mathematics classroom. This anxiety is best explained by:

(A) operant conditioning	(B) observational learning

(C) insight learning	(D) classical conditioning

**13.**	A group of adults is learning an entirely unfamiliar technical skill and at first needs step-by-step direction from the instructor. In Knowles's later view, this shows that:

(A)	pedagogy and andragogy are ends of a continuum, used as the situation requires

(B)	andragogy must be applied to every adult in every situation

(C)	pedagogy is never appropriate once learners are over eighteen

(D)	adults are unable to acquire wholly unfamiliar skills

**14.**	In a flipped course, several students arrive without having watched the assigned video. The response most consistent with the model is to:

(A)	deliver the full lecture again to the whole class

(B)	cancel the class activity and show the video in class

(C)	abandon the flipped model for the rest of the term

(D)	use a brief entry quiz and proceed with the planned activity

**15.**	Given below are two statements about good teaching:

**Statement I:** It is measured by coverage of the syllabus alone.

**Statement II:** It is rigid and authoritarian in its methods.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Statement I and Statement II are true

(B)	Both Statement I and Statement II are false

(C)	Statement I is true, but Statement II is false

(D)	Statement I is false, but Statement II is true

**16.**	Teaching is often analysed in terms of independent, dependent and intervening variables. Which statement about this analysis is NOT true?

(B)	The learner functions as the dependent variable in the process

(C)	The intervening variables are free of any influence from the teacher

(D)	Content and method operate as intervening variables in the process

(A)	let them skip the unit or take up advanced work

(C)	re-teach them the prerequisites of the unit

(D)	award them the final grade for the course

## **Answer Key: Set 14**

| 1  **D** | 2  **B** | 3  **C** | 4  **A** | 5  **C** | 6  **B** | 7  **D** | 8  **B** | 9  **D** | 10  **A** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 11  **D** | 12  **D** | 13  **A** | 14  **D** | 15  **B** | 16  **C** | 17  **A** | 18  **C** | 19  **C** | 20  **D** |
| 21  **B** | 22  **B** | 23  **C** | 24  **A** | 25  **A** | 26  **D** | 27  **C** | 28  **C** | 29  **A** | 30  **A** |
| 31  **B** | 32  **B** | 33  **A** | 34  **C** | 35  **D** | 36  **D** | 37  **B** | 38  **B** | 39  **A** | 40  **D** |
| 41  **C** | 42  **B** | 43  **C** | 44  **D** | 45  **C** | 46  **D** | 47  **C** | 48  **A** | 49  **B** | 50  **A** |

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 14**

**Q1 — (D),** because Dale's cone is often read in three parts that match Bruner's modes: the bottom bands of direct, contrived and dramatised experience are 'doing' (enactive); the middle bands from demonstrations to still pictures are 'observing' (iconic); and the top bands of visual and verbal symbols are 'symbolising' (symbolic).

*Why not (A): 'visual' suggests iconic, but charts, graphs and maps are abstract symbols; the iconic band consists of pictures and observed events.*

**Q2 — (B),** because one credit corresponds to one hour of lecture or tutorial, or two hours of practical work, per week. Three lecture hours give 3 credits and two practical hours give 1 credit, so the course carries 3 + 1 = 4 credits.

*Why not (C): 5 comes from counting each practical hour as a full credit; two practical hours make only one credit.*

**Q3 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. At the memory level the teacher chooses the content, fixes its order, presents it and tests it; the learner receives and reproduces. The Reason describes a learner-controlled arrangement, which is the opposite of what happens.

*Why not (A): if learners selected content and sequence, the teaching would not be teacher-centred; the Reason contradicts the Assertion.*

**Q4 — (A),** because both statements are true. Philosophers distinguish 'teaching' as trying (the task sense) from 'teaching' as succeeding (the achievement sense). A teacher may teach all morning in the first sense without success. In the second sense teaching and learning are logically tied, as selling is tied to buying.

*Why not (C): Statement II is the standard account of the achievement sense; in that sense unsuccessful teaching is not teaching.*

**Q5 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Variety is recommended because a class contains diverse learners and because content itself demands different modes. Learners have preferences, not exclusive channels; most can learn through several modes, and fixed 'styles' are not supported by evidence.

*Why not (A): the Reason overstates learning-style theory; variety is justified without assuming fixed, exclusive styles.*

**Q6 — (B),** because the chairperson opens the session by introducing the theme and the speaker. The paper is then presented. Participants discuss it and raise questions, to which the speaker responds. Finally the chairperson sums up the discussion and closes the session.

*Why not (D): summing up comes last, because the chairperson has to take account of the points raised in the discussion.*

**Q7 — (D),** because Puentedura's SAMR model has four levels. In Substitution, technology replaces an older tool with no functional change. In Augmentation it adds functional improvement, such as a spell-check. Modification redesigns the task, and Redefinition makes possible tasks that were previously inconceivable.

*Why not (A): augmentation requires some functional gain from the technology; here the task is unchanged in every respect.*

**Q8 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Formative evaluation informs the teacher about the effectiveness of teaching and the learner about progress, because it takes place while change is still possible. The Reason is a correct statement about a different type of evaluation.

*Why not (A): a true statement about summative evaluation cannot explain a feature of formative evaluation.*

**Q9 — (D),** because applying principles to problems of a familiar type shows understanding. Handling an unfamiliar, open question by weighing evidence and forming a judgement requires reflective level learning, which these students have not reached. They have clearly gone beyond rote memory.

*Why not (A): rote learners could not solve even the textbook problems; correct application shows more than memory.*

**Q10 — (A),** because evaluation serves several functions. Its instructional function gives feedback to teaching and learning; its administrative function supports decisions on promotion, selection and certification; its guidance function helps students with educational and vocational choices; and its research function supplies data for studies.

*Why not (B): the instructional function improves teaching and learning of a course; advising on a choice of courses is guidance.*

**Q11 — (D),** because the code of professional ethics requires teachers to carry out examination duties, namely setting, invigilating and evaluating, with honesty and confidentiality. Leaking questions is a serious breach that damages the fairness of the whole examination. Personal relationships give no exemption.

*Why not (C): teacher autonomy covers professional decisions in teaching; it does not include discretion to disclose confidential papers.*

**Q12 — (D),** because the classroom, at first neutral, was paired with humiliation, which naturally produces distress. The classroom has become a conditioned stimulus that by itself evokes the emotional response. Involuntary emotional reactions of this kind are learnt through classical, not operant, conditioning.

*Why not (A): operant conditioning explains voluntary behaviour shaped by its consequences; an involuntary emotional reaction to a setting is classically conditioned.*

**Q13 — (A),** because Knowles first presented andragogy as opposed to pedagogy, but later treated the two as ends of a continuum. Where adults are complete novices, a teacher-directed approach is suitable at first, moving towards self-direction as competence grows. The choice depends on the learner and the task.

*Why not (B): Knowles himself gave up the strict rule that andragogy fits all adults; novices may need direction at first.*

**Q14 — (D),** because if the teacher re-lectures whenever some students are unprepared, the incentive to prepare disappears and class time is lost. A short quiz or entry task holds students accountable, and the unprepared can catch up quickly while the activity goes on.

*Why not (A): re-delivering the lecture rewards non-preparation and uses up the time the model had freed for higher-order work.*

**Q15 — (B),** because both statements are false. Good teaching is judged by the learning it produces, not by the amount of content covered. It is also flexible and democratic: the teacher adapts methods to learners and encourages participation instead of imposing a fixed procedure.

*Why not (C): coverage is an input; a syllabus can be 'covered' with little learning, so Statement I cannot be accepted.*

**Q16 — (C),** because the teacher is the independent variable, the learner the dependent variable, and content, methods and strategies the intervening variables. The intervening variables are selected and organised by the teacher; it is through them that the teacher's influence reaches the learner. They are therefore not free of that influence.

*Why not (D): this is the standard, true description of intervening variables; the question asks for the statement that is false.*

**Q17 — (A),** because placement evaluation answers two questions: does the learner have the prerequisites, and has the learner already achieved the intended outcomes? Those who have already mastered the objectives should be placed further ahead or given enrichment; teaching them the same unit wastes their time.

*Why not (B): identical instruction ignores what the pre-test has revealed and defeats the purpose of placement evaluation.*

**Q18 — (C),** because formulating a proposal means producing an original plan, which is Create, the highest level. Critiquing against criteria is Evaluate, one level lower. Distinguishing is Analyse, and computing the mean by a known procedure is Apply.

*Why not (A): critiquing is high, at Evaluate, but in the revised taxonomy Create stands above Evaluate.*

**Q19 — (C),** because Bandura rejected one-way explanations. A person's beliefs and expectations affect behaviour; behaviour changes the environment; and the environment in turn shapes beliefs and behaviour. This three-way interaction is central to his social cognitive theory.

*Why not (A): one-way environmental determination is the behaviourist position that Bandura set out to correct.*

**Q20 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Open and free enrolment brings in huge numbers, many of whom register out of curiosity and drop out; completion rates are commonly reported in single digits or low double digits. Openness is, in fact, one cause of low completion.

*Why not (A): large enrolment does not mean large completion; the two move in opposite directions.*

**Q21 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Suitability matters because material that is too hard or too childish fails to communicate. Accuracy and currency are a separate criterion for selecting material. Each is a principle in its own right.

*Why not (A): two sound criteria for choosing material stand side by side; neither is the reason for the other.*

**Q22 — (B),** because statements 1, 3 and 4 are correct. Reflective teaching is built around a problem, trains learners to demand evidence and test ideas, and needs an open, democratic teacher. Statement 2 is wrong: the learner is the most active participant at this level.

*Why not (A): the democratic role of the teacher in statement 4 is an essential feature and must be included.*

**Q23 — (C),** because statements 1, 2 and 4 are correct. Formal operations make abstract thought possible, the wish for autonomy brings conflict with parents, and rapid physical change makes adolescents self-conscious about appearance. Statement 3 is wrong: adolescence is marked by emotional intensity and fluctuation.

*Why not (A): concern about body image is characteristic of adolescence, so statement 4 must be included.*

**Q24 — (A),** because a z-score expresses a raw score as its distance from the mean in standard deviation units: z = (score − mean) ÷ SD = (65 − 50) ÷ 10 = 15 ÷ 10 = 1.5. The student is one and a half standard deviations above the mean. A z-score is a norm-referenced measure.

*Why not (B): 6.5 comes from dividing the raw score by the standard deviation without first subtracting the mean.*

**Q25 — (A),** because James Flynn documented large gains in average IQ scores over decades, attributed to better nutrition, schooling and a more stimulating environment. The finding shows that measured intelligence is not fixed by heredity alone. The Matthew effect refers to early advantages accumulating over time.

*Why not (B): the Matthew effect ('the rich get richer') describes widening gaps between individuals, not a rise in the average.*

**Q26 — (D),** because Statement I is false and Statement II is true. Representation through action is the enactive mode; the iconic mode uses images and diagrams. 'Scaffolding' was introduced in a 1976 paper by Wood, Bruner and Ross and later linked with Vygotsky's zone of proximal development.

*Why not (A): the first statement confuses two modes: action is enactive, images are iconic.*

**Q27 — (C),** because Norman Gronlund proposed first writing a general instructional objective (such as 'understands scientific principles') and then listing under it a sample of specific learning outcomes in behavioural terms (explains, gives examples, predicts). This avoids long lists of trivial objectives.

*Why not (A): the three-part behavioural format with condition and criterion is Mager's approach, not Gronlund's.*

**Q28 — (C),** because Maslow called the four lower needs (physiological, safety, belongingness and esteem) deficiency needs: they arise from lack and cease to motivate once met. Self-actualisation is a growth or 'being' need, which continues to motivate and even increases as it is satisfied.

*Why not (A): esteem is the highest of the deficiency needs, but it is still a deficiency need.*

**Q29 — (A),** because SWAYAM offers online courses whose credits can be transferred. SWAYAM PRABHA telecasts programmes on DTH channels. One Nation One Subscription, operational from 2025, gives institutions nationwide access to scholarly e-journals, taking over the role of earlier consortia such as e-ShodhSindhu. Shodhgangotri holds synopses of research in progress.

*Why not (B): the two similar names are exchanged in that option: SWAYAM is the online course platform and SWAYAM PRABHA the television service.*

**Q30 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. In a linear programme every learner takes the same path and only the pace varies. In a branching programme a learner who errs is routed to remedial frames, and one who answers correctly moves ahead, so both pace and path are individualised.

*Why not (B): the response-dependent route is precisely the mechanism by which branching adapts, so the Reason explains the Assertion.*

**Q31 — (B),** because Tolman argued that the rats had learnt the layout of the maze, a mental representation he called a cognitive map, and not a fixed series of turns. This is why they could choose a new route. His view is known as purposive behaviourism or sign learning.

*Why not (C): a chain of habits would have sent the rats down the old, blocked path; choosing a new route shows knowledge of the layout.*

**Q32 — (B),** because Flanders found that someone is talking for about two-thirds of classroom time, that about two-thirds of that talk is the teacher's, and that about two-thirds of teacher talk is direct influence (lecturing, directing, criticising). The rule shows how teacher-dominated ordinary classrooms are.

*Why not (C): the third part of the rule concerns direct influence, not indirect; indirect talk is the smaller share of teacher talk.*

**Q33 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Since outcomes define what the programme is for, assessment must provide evidence about those outcomes and nothing else. This 'constructive alignment' of outcomes, teaching activities and assessment is the organising idea of the approach.

*Why not (B): the Reason gives the purpose that makes alignment necessary, so it is the explanation of the Assertion.*

**Q34 — (C),** because when books are allowed, questions of recall lose all value because the answers can be looked up. The examination is worthwhile only if it asks candidates to use, interpret and judge information, so that the books serve as tools and not as answer keys.

*Why not (A): questions answerable by copying turn the examination into a test of speed in locating passages.*

**Q35 — (D),** because the index is the proportion correct in the upper group minus that in the lower group. A negative value means the lower group did better, which signals a faulty item: an ambiguous stem, a wrong key or a misleading option. Such items are revised or dropped.

*Why not (C): the discrimination index says nothing about the percentage answering correctly; that is the facility value.*

**Q36 — (D),** because Jacob Kounin found that effective classroom managers communicate, by their behaviour, that they know what is going on everywhere in the room; he called this 'withitness'. Overlapping is the ability to attend to two matters at the same time, and momentum and smoothness concern the flow of the lesson.

*Why not (A): overlapping is handling two events at once; the quality of being aware of everything in the room is withitness.*

**Q37 — (B),** because credit points so far are 40 × 6.0 = 240. For a CGPA of 6.5 over 60 credits, 60 × 6.5 = 390 credit points are needed. The next semester must supply 390 − 240 = 150 points over 20 credits, that is, an SGPA of 150 ÷ 20 = 7.5.

*Why not (A): 7.0 treats the CGPA as the simple average of 6.0 and 7.0, ignoring that the earlier credits are twice as many.*

**Q38 — (B),** because Stevenson's definition stresses a problem, completion and a natural setting. Kilpatrick defined a project as 'a whole-hearted purposeful activity proceeding in a social environment', and Ballard as 'a bit of real life that has been imported into the school'.

*Why not (A): Kilpatrick's definition speaks of 'whole-hearted purposeful activity'; the 'problematic act' wording is Stevenson's.*

**Q39 — (A),** because Bigge described understanding in two complementary ways: seeing relationships between particulars and generalisations, and seeing the 'tool use' of a fact, that is, what it can be used for in reaching a goal. A student understands a formula when he or she knows what it is for.

*Why not (B): remembering the exact wording is retention, a memory level outcome, and no part of understanding.*

**Q40 — (D),** because a demonstration can leave students as passive as a lecture. Asking them to predict what will happen, observe and then explain engages their thinking at every step and exposes misconceptions. Questioning during the demonstration is therefore a standard recommendation.

*Why not (A): announcing the conclusion first leaves nothing to observe for; students merely wait to see the stated result.*

**Q41 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. A computer logs every action, so response times and answer changes are available for analysis. The mode of delivery does nothing for the quality of the items: a poor item remains poor on a screen, and validity must still be established.

*Why not (A): technology changes how items are delivered and scored, not whether they measure what they should.*

**Q42 — (B),** because Gronlund expressed evaluation as measurement (quantitative description of pupil behaviour) and non-measurement techniques (qualitative description), together with value judgements about the desirability of what is described. The judgement of worth is what turns description into evaluation.

*Why not (A): statistical analysis belongs to the quantitative description; the element it leaves out is the judgement of worth.*

**Q43 — (C),** because in brainwriting, ideas are generated in writing before any talk, so quiet members contribute as much as vocal ones and nobody has to wait for a turn. It keeps the rule of deferred judgement. The cards are afterwards pooled, grouped and discussed.

*Why not (A): a buzz session is oral discussion in small groups, in which vocal members can still dominate.*

**Q44 — (D),** because guidelines 1 and 3 are sound: a clear single problem in the stem and plausible distractors are basic rules. Guideline 2 is wrong because correct answers should be placed at random to avoid patterns. Guideline 4 is wrong because such options give clues and are best avoided.

*Why not (C): 'all of the above' lets a candidate who recognises two correct options select it without knowing the rest.*

**Q45 — (C),** because students take part, ask questions and attempt difficult tasks only when they feel that mistakes will not bring humiliation. Such psychological safety is a feature of a supportive learning environment. It is different from laissez-faire, in which the teacher simply withdraws guidance.

*Why not (B): laissez-faire means absence of direction; a safe climate still has high expectations and active guidance.*

**Q46 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. The continuum rises from conditioning through training and instruction to teaching, according to the part played by the learner's understanding. Because conditioning by-passes understanding, as the Reason says, it stands at the bottom, not the top.

*Why not (A): the Reason, correctly understood, is the ground for placing conditioning lowest, so it contradicts the Assertion.*

**Q47 — (C),** because Analyse comprises differentiating, organising and attributing. Evaluate comprises checking and critiquing. Create comprises generating, planning and producing. Apply comprises executing and implementing. Each of the nineteen specific processes of the revised taxonomy belongs to one category only.

*Why not (A): critiquing is a judgement against external criteria and belongs to Evaluate; attributing, finding the point of view behind material, belongs to Analyse.*

**Q48 — (A),** because K. Patricia Cross grouped barriers into situational (arising from one's circumstances at a given time, such as job and family duties, cost and transport), institutional (inconvenient schedules, rigid rules, fees fixed by providers) and dispositional (attitudes and self-perceptions, such as feeling too old).

*Why not (B): an institutional barrier is created by the provider's practices; here the obstacle lies in the learner's own life situation.*

**Q49 — (B),** because operations 1, 2 and 3 belong to the post-active phase, which deals with judging the outcomes of teaching and revising strategy accordingly. Sizing up the class, or perception of its mood and size, is done on meeting the class and belongs to the interactive phase.

*Why not (A): changing strategies on the basis of evidence is also a post-active operation, so item 3 cannot be left out.*

**Q50 — (A),** because statements 1 and 2 are correct. Team teaching assigns topics according to each teacher's strength and depends on planning, teaching and evaluating together. Statement 3 is wrong: it demands far more coordination than individual teaching, which is its main practical difficulty.

*Why not (D): team teaching increases the need for coordination; that need is its best-known limitation.*

# **Set 15**

**Level:** Critical     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

**1.**	In the analysis of teaching in terms of variables, the content and the methods through which a teacher influences learners function as:

(A) independent variables	(B) dependent variables

(C) intervening variables	(D) extraneous variables

**2.**	Match the qualities of a test in List I with the evidence in List II:

| **List I (Quality)** | **List II (Evidence)** |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Content validity | i. Agreement between marks awarded by two independent examiners |
| 2. Predictive validity | ii. Correlation between scores on the same test given twice |
| 3. Test–retest reliability | iii. Correspondence between test items and the course blueprint |
| 4. Inter-rater reliability | iv. Correlation between entrance scores and later course grades |

Choose the correct matching:

(A) 1-iv, 2-iii, 3-ii, 4-i	(B) 1-iii, 2-iv, 3-i, 4-ii

(C) 1-iii, 2-iv, 3-ii, 4-i	(D) 1-iii, 2-ii, 3-iv, 4-i

**3.**	Arrange the following steps of Dewey's analysis of reflective thinking, on which the problem-solving method is based, in the correct order:

1\.	Suggesting possible solutions

2\.	Sensing a difficulty

3\.	Testing the chosen solution in action

4\.	Locating and defining the problem

5\.	Reasoning out the implications of each solution

Choose the correct order:

(A) 4, 2, 1, 5, 3	(B) 2, 4, 5, 1, 3

(C) 2, 1, 4, 5, 3	(D) 2, 4, 1, 5, 3

**4.**	Consider the following statements about the levels of teaching:

1\.	Understanding level teaching presupposes that learners already command the relevant facts.

2\.	Reflective level teaching is essentially problem-centred.

3\.	The teacher's role is more dominant at the reflective level than at the memory level.

4\.	Essay-type tests suit reflective level outcomes better than objective-type tests do.

Which of the statements given above are correct?

(A) 1, 2 and 3 only	(B) 2, 3 and 4 only

(C) 1, 2 and 4 only	(D) 1 and 4 only

**5.**	Which of the following is a NON-projected visual aid?

(A) Epidiascope	(B) Flannel board

(C) Filmstrip	(D) Overhead transparency

**6.**	A student whose measured intelligence is above average consistently scores far below what that ability predicts, and no specific disability has been identified. The student is best described as:

(A) a slow learner	(B) an underachiever

(C) a learner with dyscalculia	(D) a learner with low aptitude

**7.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:
