## **Answer Key: Set 15**

| 1  **C** | 2  **C** | 3  **D** | 4  **C** | 5  **B** | 6  **B** | 7  **C** | 8  **A** | 9  **D** | 10  **A** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 11  **C** | 12  **B** | 13  **C** | 14  **B** | 15  **C** | 16  **B** | 17  **C** | 18  **A** | 19  **D** | 20  **A** |
| 21  **A** | 22  **B** | 23  **C** | 24  **C** | 25  **D** | 26  **A** | 27  **A** | 28  **B** | 29  **D** | 30  **A** |
| 31  **D** | 32  **D** | 33  **C** | 34  **C** | 35  **B** | 36  **A** | 37  **A** | 38  **D** | 39  **B** | 40  **D** |
| 41  **C** | 42  **A** | 43  **D** | 44  **B** | 45  **A** | 46  **D** | 47  **D** | 48  **A** | 49  **B** | 50  **B** |

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 15**

**Q1 — (C),** because the teacher is the independent variable and the learner the dependent variable. Content, strategies and methods stand between the two and carry the teacher's influence to the learner, so they are intervening variables. They are neither the source of the change nor the place where the change is observed.

*Why not (A): methods are chosen and manipulated by the teacher, which tempts one to call them independent, but in this analysis the independent variable is the teacher.*

**Q2 — (C),** because content validity is judged by checking items against the syllabus or blueprint. Predictive validity is shown when scores forecast a later criterion. Test–retest reliability is the stability of scores across two administrations. Inter-rater reliability is the agreement between independent scorers, the property that essay tests most often lack.

*Why not (D): items ii and iv are both correlations, but one links a test with itself over time (reliability) and the other links a test with a later outcome (validity).*

**Q3 — (D),** because Dewey's five steps are: a felt difficulty; its location and definition; suggestion of possible solutions (hypotheses); reasoning out the bearings of each suggestion; and further observation or experiment leading to acceptance or rejection. The problem-solving method in the classroom follows this order.

*Why not (A): the problem cannot be defined before some difficulty is felt; the vague sense of perplexity comes first and is then sharpened into a definite problem.*

**Q4 — (C),** because statements 1, 2 and 4 are correct. Each level builds on the one below, reflective teaching is organised around a problem that learners investigate, and its outcomes (reasoning, originality, judgement) are best revealed by essay-type tests. Statement 3 is wrong: at the reflective level the teacher is a democratic guide and the learner takes the active role.

*Why not (A): raising a problem does not make the teacher dominant; dominance of the teacher is the mark of the memory level.*

**Q5 — (B),** because non-projected aids are seen directly without a projector: chalkboards, charts, models, flash cards and flannel boards. A filmstrip and an overhead transparency need a projector, and an epidiascope is itself a projector that throws enlarged images of opaque objects or of slides on a screen.

*Why not (A): the epidiascope can show a page of a book or a specimen, which looks like direct display, but it does so by projecting an image.*

**Q6 — (B),** because underachievement is a gap between potential and performance: ability is adequate or high but attainment is low, usually because of motivational, emotional or environmental factors. A slow learner has below-average ability and attains accordingly, so there is no such gap between ability and attainment.

*Why not (A): low marks are common to both, but the slow learner's marks match limited ability, whereas here ability is above average.*

**Q7 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. A lecture is economical: one teacher can present an organised overview to many students in a short time, which makes it useful for opening a topic. Its well-known weakness is that communication is largely one-way and learners remain comparatively passive.

*Why not (A): suitability for large classes rests on economy of time and effort, not on participation, which the lecture does least well.*

**Q8 — (A),** because ipsative or self-referenced assessment judges a learner against his or her own earlier performance, so the result reports personal progress. Norm-referenced assessment compares the learner with other learners, and criterion-referenced assessment compares the learner with a fixed external standard of mastery.

*Why not (B): no outside standard is applied here; the only yardstick is the student's own previous essay.*

**Q9 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Ausubel treated reception–discovery and rote–meaningful as two separate dimensions. Well-organised expository teaching can be fully meaningful, and discovery can be rote if the learner merely stumbles on an answer. What makes learning meaningful is its substantive link with existing cognitive structure.

*Why not (A): the Assertion reflects a popular belief about lectures, but it is precisely the belief Ausubel argued against.*

**Q10 — (A),** because Flanders' ten categories place four under indirect teacher talk: accepting feelings, praising or encouraging, accepting or using students' ideas, and asking questions. Lecturing, giving directions and criticising or justifying authority are direct influence. Two categories cover student talk and one covers silence or confusion.

*Why not (B): lecturing is the commonest teacher behaviour and feels neutral, but it restricts the student's freedom to respond and is coded as direct.*

**Q11 — (C),** because in the model used by Mitzel and by Dunkin and Biddle, presage variables are the characteristics teachers bring with them: formative experiences, training and personal traits. Context variables concern pupils, school and community; process variables are observable classroom behaviours; product variables are the outcomes in pupils.

*Why not (A): context covers the setting in which the teacher works (pupils, classroom, school), not what the teacher brings to it.*

**Q12 — (B),** because heutagogy, a term introduced by Hase and Kenyon, is self-determined learning: the learner sets the path and even the goals, and develops the capability to learn in unfamiliar situations. It goes a step beyond andragogy, in which the learner is self-directed but the teacher still largely designs the course and its objectives.

*Why not (A): andragogy stresses self-direction in how to learn within a planned course; choosing and revising what to learn marks heutagogy.*

**Q13 — (C),** because facility value is the proportion of examinees answering correctly, here 30 out of 100, or 0.30. The discrimination index compares the upper and lower groups; when both do equally well, it is close to zero. Such an item is fairly difficult and fails to separate able from weak candidates, so it needs revision.

*Why not (A): 0.70 is the proportion who failed the item; facility value counts those who succeeded.*

**Q14 — (B),** because Krathwohl's levels rise with internalisation. Attending is Receiving; active participation is Responding; attaching worth to a cause and showing commitment to it is Valuing; and a value system that consistently governs behaviour is Characterisation. Organisation, the level between Valuing and Characterisation, is not represented here.

*Why not (A): volunteering a comment shows participation only; commitment to the worth of a cause, as in item 3, is what marks Valuing.*

**Q15 — (C),** because Helen Parkhurst devised the Dalton Plan of individual assignments or contracts. H. E. Armstrong advocated the heuristic method, in which pupils find things out for themselves. Skinner developed linear programmed instruction from operant conditioning. Alex Osborn introduced brainstorming for the free production of ideas in groups.

*Why not (A): the Dalton Plan and the heuristic method both stress the pupil's own work, but the Dalton Plan is Parkhurst's and the heuristic method is Armstrong's.*

**Q16 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Reflection needs facts to reflect on and an understanding of their relationships, which is why the lower levels are prerequisites. That Hunt is the thinker associated with the reflective level is a separate fact and says nothing about prerequisites.

*Why not (A): a true Reason on the same topic is not automatically an explanation; ask whether it answers 'why' for the Assertion.*

**Q17 — (C),** because Mager required three parts in a well-written objective: the performance (what the learner will do, here 'mark five river systems'), the conditions under which it is done ('given an outline map') and the criterion of acceptable performance ('90 per cent accuracy'). The opening phrase specifies the circumstances, so it is the condition.

*Why not (B): the criterion is the standard of acceptable performance, which here is the accuracy level, not the material supplied.*

**Q18 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Teaching always involves an intention to bring about learning, but learning is wider: people also learn from unplanned experience and observation. Because learning may be incidental, it can and does occur where nobody is teaching.

*Why not (B): the Reason is not a separate fact; the incidental nature of learning is exactly why it can occur without teaching.*

**Q19 — (D),** because an analytic rubric breaks performance into separate criteria and describes levels for each, giving a profile of strengths and weaknesses as well as a total. A holistic rubric gives a single overall score for the work as a whole, and a checklist merely records whether a feature is present or absent.

*Why not (A): both are rubrics, but a holistic rubric gives one overall judgement and does not score the criteria separately.*

**Q20 — (A),** because Cattell distinguished fluid intelligence, the capacity to reason in novel situations, from crystallised intelligence, the store of knowledge and skills built up through experience. Fluid intelligence tends to decline from early adulthood, while crystallised intelligence is maintained or grows, which is the pattern described.

*Why not (B): the labels are easily reversed: 'fluid' refers to flexible reasoning on new problems, not to knowledge that flows from experience.*

**Q21 — (A),** because recall alone would be memory level, but the students also explain relationships and apply the idea to a familiar type of example, which is understanding level work. The reflective level requires a genuinely problematic situation that learners investigate critically and independently; a routine worked example does not provide one.

*Why not (C): application to a familiar example is still understanding; reflection begins only when the learner faces an open problem with no ready procedure.*

**Q22 — (B),** because from the base upward, Dale's cone runs: direct purposeful experiences, contrived experiences, dramatised experiences, demonstrations, study trips, exhibits, television, motion pictures, recordings and still pictures, visual symbols and, at the apex, verbal symbols. Words are the most abstract because they bear no resemblance to what they stand for.

*Why not (C): visual symbols such as charts and diagrams still show something to the eye, so they lie just below verbal symbols, not above them.*

**Q23 — (C),** because the Herbartian steps are preparation, presentation, comparison (association), generalisation and application. Morrison's steps are exploration, presentation, assimilation, organisation and recitation. Only presentation appears in both lists, though in Morrison's model it follows an exploration of what learners already know.

*Why not (A): recitation sounds like a memory level activity, but as a formal step it belongs to Morrison's model, where it closes the unit.*

**Q24 — (C),** because the 2021 Regulations allow up to 40 per cent of the total courses offered in a particular programme in a semester to be taken through online courses on SWAYAM, with the credits counted towards the degree. The earlier 2016 Regulations, which they replaced, had set the ceiling at 20 per cent.

*Why not (A): 20 per cent was the ceiling under the 2016 Regulations, which the 2021 Regulations superseded.*

**Q25 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Certifying mastery needs comparison with a fixed standard, which is the work of a criterion-referenced test. Norm-referenced tests are built to discriminate among examinees and yield a wide spread of scores for ranking; a high rank does not show that the competencies have been mastered.

*Why not (A): the true Reason actually argues against the Assertion: a test designed to spread candidates out tells us rank, not mastery.*

**Q26 — (A),** because the skill of stimulus variation consists of deliberate changes in the teacher's behaviour to secure and hold attention: movement, gestures, change in speech pattern, pausing, focusing, change in interaction style and switching between oral and visual channels. Every component listed in the question belongs to it.

*Why not (B): set induction also aims at attention, but only at the opening of a lesson, by linking the new topic with previous knowledge.*

**Q27 — (A),** because assimilation is fitting a new experience into an existing schema, as when the cow is first called a dog. Accommodation is changing the schema, or creating a new one, because the experience does not fit. Forming the category 'cow' alters the child's mental structure, so it is accommodation.

*Why not (B): assimilation describes the first half of the episode, the 'big dog' label, not the creation of the new category.*

**Q28 — (B),** because SWAYAM PRABHA telecasts curriculum-based educational programmes round the clock on DTH television channels, precisely so that learners without internet access are not left out. SWAYAM, e-PG Pathshala and Virtual Labs are all web-based and need an internet connection.

*Why not (A): the names are nearly identical, but SWAYAM is the online MOOC platform; only SWAYAM PRABHA reaches the learner through television.*

**Q29 — (D),** because reinforcement increases behaviour and punishment decreases it; 'positive' means something is added and 'negative' means something is taken away. Here an unpleasant event, the test, is removed when the desired behaviour occurs, and the behaviour increases. Removal plus increase is negative reinforcement.

*Why not (B): negative punishment removes something pleasant in order to reduce a behaviour; here something unpleasant is removed and the behaviour rises.*

**Q30 — (A),** because statements 1 and 2 are correct. In the interactive phase the teacher perceives the class, diagnoses learners and reacts; strategies are decided earlier, in the pre-active or planning phase. Statement 3 is wrong because fixing objectives is also a pre-active operation. The post-active phase deals with evaluating outcomes and reflecting on the lesson.

*Why not (D): objectives are consulted in the post-active phase when results are judged, but they are fixed before teaching begins.*

**Q31 — (D),** because statements 1 and 3 are correct. Formal operations bring abstract and hypothetical reasoning, and peers become a major reference group in adolescence. Statement 2 is wrong: Erikson assigns identity versus role confusion to adolescence; intimacy versus isolation is the crisis of young adulthood, which follows it.

*Why not (C): intimacy versus isolation is a genuine Erikson stage and applies to many postgraduate students, but it is not the adolescent stage.*

**Q32 — (D),** because in branching or intrinsic programming, developed by Norman Crowder, each frame ends with a multiple-choice question and the learner's choice decides the next frame; wrong choices lead to remedial frames. In Skinner's linear programming every learner follows the same small steps and errors are kept to a minimum.

*Why not (A): linear programmes also use frames and immediate confirmation, but they have a single fixed path and no remedial loops.*

**Q33 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. The Yerkes–Dodson law describes an inverted-U relationship: performance improves with arousal up to an optimum and then falls, and the optimum is lower for difficult tasks. A steady, unlimited rise is therefore wrong, although moderate arousal does help.

*Why not (A): the Reason looks like support for the Assertion, but it claims a straight-line rise, which would make very high anxiety best of all.*

**Q34 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. Measurement assigns numbers according to rules and stops there. Evaluation is the wider term: it draws on quantitative measurement and on qualitative description such as observation, and adds a judgement of worth against objectives.

*Why not (A): Statement II reverses the relationship; evaluation includes measurement and more, so it cannot be the narrower term.*

**Q35 — (B),** because in 1956 the order ended Synthesis, Evaluation. In the revision the two were interchanged: Evaluation became Evaluate at the fifth level, and Synthesis, renamed Create, moved to the sixth and highest level. All categories were also re-expressed as verbs, and Knowledge became Remember.

*Why not (A): Evaluation was not renamed Create; it survived as Evaluate and moved one step down.*

**Q36 — (A),** because the Hawthorne effect is an improvement in performance caused by the awareness of being studied or given special attention, not by the treatment itself. It threatens the validity of classroom experiments. Here the students themselves know they are in the experimental group, and that knowledge alone raises their performance.

*Why not (B): the Pygmalion effect works through the teacher's expectations; in this case the cause is the students' own awareness of being observed.*

**Q37 — (A),** because Dave's order is Imitation (copying a model), Manipulation (performing from instructions), Precision (performing accurately without help), Articulation (coordinating a series of acts smoothly) and Naturalisation (automatic, effortless performance). The sequence shows growing accuracy, coordination and independence from the model.

*Why not (B): precision cannot come before manipulation; the learner must first perform the act from instructions before refining it to accuracy.*

**Q38 — (D),** because the course builds one specific skill through repeated practice to a set standard of performance, with no concern for understanding; that is training. Teaching is wider and aims at understanding and judgement, while conditioning refers to establishing stimulus–response connections, not to the deliberate practice of a skill.

*Why not (A): instruction is the structured imparting of knowledge or information; here nothing is explained or imparted, only practised.*

**Q39 — (B),** because in a symposium several speakers present prepared statements on different aspects of one theme, one after another, and audience questions follow. The absence of conversation among the speakers is the distinguishing feature. In a panel discussion the members talk informally among themselves under a moderator.

*Why not (A): a panel also seats several experts before an audience, but its essence is free exchange among the panellists, which is absent here.*

**Q40 — (D),** because Statement I is false and Statement II is true. Subject mastery is necessary but never sufficient: communication skill, method, support material and learner characteristics also decide the outcome. Statement II states the accepted position that the same method works differently for learners who differ in readiness and prior knowledge.

*Why not (A): the word 'sufficient' is the trap; a statement that makes any single factor enough for effective teaching should be doubted.*

**Q41 — (C),** because statements 1, 3 and 4 are correct. Synchronous learning happens in real time and asynchronous learning at the learner's convenience; blended learning mixes classroom and online modes. Statement 2 describes cMOOCs. xMOOCs are the structured, instructor-led courses with video lectures and quizzes that most large platforms offer.

*Why not (A): connectivism is a genuine basis of MOOCs, but of the cMOOC type; the 'x' type follows a conventional course structure.*

**Q42 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Knowles's first assumption about adult learners concerns self-concept: adults see themselves as responsible for their own decisions. A classroom that treats them as dependent children conflicts with that self-concept, and this conflict is the source of the resistance.

*Why not (B): the Reason is not merely another feature of adults; the shift in self-concept is the stated cause of the resistance.*

**Q43 — (D),** because SGPA is the credit-weighted mean of grade points. Credit points earned are 6 × 9 = 54, 2 × 7 = 14 and 2 × 8 = 16, a total of 84. Total credits are 10. SGPA = 84 ÷ 10 = 8.4. Courses with more credits pull the average towards their grade.

*Why not (B): 8.0 is the simple average of 9, 7 and 8, which ignores the credits attached to each course.*

**Q44 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Formative evaluation monitors progress and gives feedback; when difficulties persist despite that feedback, diagnostic evaluation probes their underlying causes. The Reason correctly describes a different type, placement evaluation, and has no bearing on the relation between formative and diagnostic evaluation.

*Why not (A): the Reason is true and sounds related because it also names a type of evaluation, but it explains nothing about diagnosis.*

**Q45 — (A),** because Bandura showed that people learn by observing models. Köhler's chimpanzee studies demonstrated insight, a sudden grasp of the solution. Thorndike's law of effect states that responses followed by satisfaction are strengthened. Tolman showed that learning can occur without reinforcement and remain hidden until needed, which he called latent learning.

*Why not (B): insight and latent learning are both cognitive ideas, but insight belongs to Köhler's Gestalt work and latent learning to Tolman's maze studies.*

**Q46 — (D),** because statements 1 and 3 are correct. Induction moves from examples to the rule, so it suits discovery but takes time; deduction states the rule first and applies it, which is quicker. Statement 2 assigns discovery to the wrong method, and statement 4 is reversed: induction moves from the concrete to the abstract.

*Why not (C): statement 4 uses the right pair of terms in the wrong order; abstract to concrete describes deduction.*

**Q47 — (D),** because the cycle runs plan, teach, feedback, re-plan, re-teach and re-feedback. The trainee plans a short lesson on one skill, teaches it, receives feedback from supervisor and peers, revises the plan in the light of that feedback and teaches again, usually to a different group of pupils.

*Why not (A): re-planning before feedback would leave the trainee nothing to revise against; feedback must come first.*

**Q48 — (A),** because Spearman proposed a general factor and specific factors (two-factor theory). Thurstone rejected a single general factor in favour of seven primary mental abilities. Guilford's structure-of-intellect model classifies abilities by operations, contents and products. Sternberg's triarchic theory describes analytical, creative and practical intelligence.

*Why not (B): Thurstone and Guilford both proposed many abilities, but the three-dimensional structure-of-intellect model is Guilford's.*

**Q49 — (B),** because in computerised adaptive testing the programme estimates the candidate's ability after each response and selects the next item to match it, so different candidates see different items and fewer items are needed for a precise estimate. In a linear fixed-form test every candidate answers the same items.

*Why not (C): linear-on-the-fly testing also gives each candidate a different form, but the form is assembled before the test starts and does not adapt to responses.*

**Q50 — (B),** because the revised taxonomy crosses six cognitive processes with four kinds of knowledge: factual, conceptual, procedural and metacognitive. Metacognitive knowledge is knowledge about cognition in general and about one's own cognition, including strategies and self-knowledge. It was the category added in the revision.

*Why not (A): procedural knowledge is knowing how to carry out a subject's methods and techniques; knowing how one's own mind learns best is a step removed from that.*

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 16**

**Q1 — (A),** because the concept attainment model is based on Bruner's studies of thinking. The inquiry training model was developed by Richard Suchman. The advance organiser model comes from Ausubel's theory of meaningful verbal learning. The jurisprudential inquiry model, for analysing public issues, is the work of Oliver and Shaver.

*Why not (B): Bruner and Ausubel are easily exchanged; Bruner's name goes with concept attainment and Ausubel's with advance organisers.*

**Q2 — (A),** because both statements are true. A scholar who cannot communicate does not teach effectively, so content must be joined to pedagogical skill. And since teaching has to start from where learners are, the teacher must know their previous knowledge, abilities and interests as well as the subject.

*Why not (C): Statement II is a standard requirement; teaching is fitted to learners, not only to content.*

**Q3 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Synchronous means 'at the same time': teacher and learners are online together and interact in real time, as in a live webinar. Freedom from a common time is the defining feature of asynchronous learning.

*Why not (A): the Reason defines asynchronous learning; applied to synchronous learning it is false.*

**Q4 — (B),** because the Knowledge category includes knowledge of specifics, of ways and means of dealing with specifics (among them 'knowledge of criteria') and of universals. Recalling criteria is still recall. The objective would reach Evaluation only if the student had to use the criteria to judge a report.

*Why not (A): the word 'criteria' suggests Evaluation, but the verb is 'recall'; the level is set by what the student does.*

**Q5 — (B),** because the defining feature of a workshop is that participants do practical work and produce something, here a question bank. Expert input is kept short and serves the work. In a seminar or symposium the main activity is presenting and discussing papers.

*Why not (A): a seminar is built around papers and their discussion; a tangible product made by participants is the mark of a workshop.*

**Q6 — (D),** because Morrison's steps are exploration, presentation, assimilation, organisation and recitation. The Herbartian steps are preparation, presentation, comparison, generalisation and application. Organisation, in which pupils write an outline of the unit unaided, appears only in Morrison's list.

*Why not (A): presentation is the one step named in both lists, so it cannot be the answer.*

**Q7 — (A),** because in stimulus generalisation, a response conditioned to one stimulus is also evoked by similar stimuli. The fear learnt with one teacher spreads to others who resemble him in role. Discrimination would be the opposite: learning to fear only that teacher.

*Why not (C): in response generalisation one stimulus comes to evoke several similar responses; here several stimuli evoke the same response.*

**Q8 — (B),** because test–retest reliability shows how stable scores are over time, hence the coefficient of stability. Parallel forms give the coefficient of equivalence. Split-half and Kuder–Richardson methods give coefficients of internal consistency. Parallel forms given after an interval yield stability and equivalence together.

*Why not (A): equivalence comes from two parallel forms of a test; one test given twice shows stability.*

**Q9 — (A),** because administrative support and leadership are institution-related. Equipment such as projectors, laboratories and libraries are instructional facilities. Conditions surrounding the class, physical ones like noise and light and psychological ones like climate, make up the learning environment.

*Why not (C): the projector is equipment, an instructional facility; noise is a condition of the surroundings, the learning environment.*

**Q10 — (C),** because function depends on use. The comments guide further learning, which is a formative use. Counting the marks towards the final grade is a summative use. A single assessment can serve both, though the grading use may reduce students' willingness to treat it as feedback.

*Why not (A): the feedback makes it formative, but once its marks enter the final grade it also has a summative function.*

**Q11 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. A case describes a real or realistic situation that students analyse and on which they propose decisions. Role play is spontaneous: players are given a situation and a role and improvise; a memorised script would turn it into a drama.

*Why not (A): scripted performance is dramatisation; the teaching value of role play lies in spontaneous response.*

**Q12 — (B),** because Marcia's statuses depend on exploration and commitment. Active exploration without commitment is moratorium. Diffusion is absence of both. Foreclosure is commitment without exploration, and achievement is commitment reached after exploration. Moratorium is common, and healthy, among students in their first years of college.

*Why not (A): in diffusion there is no commitment and also no exploration; this student is exploring actively.*

**Q13 — (D),** because Statement I is false and Statement II is true. Concrete-to-abstract concerns the kind of experience offered, moving from things to ideas. Particular-to-general concerns the scope of statements, moving from single cases to a rule. A particular case may itself be abstract, so the two maxims are distinct.

*Why not (A): the two maxims often work together in a lesson, which makes them look identical, but one is about experience and the other about generality.*

**Q14 — (B),** because an episcope projects opaque material by reflected light. A slide projector (diascope) projects small mounted transparent slides. An overhead projector takes large transparencies laid on its stage, on which the teacher can write while facing the class. An LCD projector displays computer output.

*Why not (C): both use transparent material, but mounted slides go in the slide projector and large writable sheets on the overhead projector.*

**Q15 — (B),** because Alderfer condensed Maslow's hierarchy into existence, relatedness and growth needs (ERG). Herzberg separated hygiene factors from motivators. Vroom's expectancy theory uses valence, instrumentality and expectancy. McClelland described learned needs for achievement, affiliation and power.

*Why not (A): both Alderfer and McClelland list three needs; 'existence, relatedness, growth' is Alderfer's set and 'achievement, affiliation, power' is McClelland's.*

**Q16 — (D),** because Statement I is false and Statement II is true. Self-assessment is primarily reflection on one's work against criteria; it need not produce a grade. Peer assessment likewise is often confined to comments (peer feedback or review) and used purely to improve drafts.

*Why not (A): assessment by students is commonly confused with grading; its main use is as feedback, with or without marks.*

**Q17 — (C),** because statements 1, 3 and 4 are correct. Statement 2 is wrong: comparing and explaining are among the seven processes of Understand, along with interpreting, exemplifying, classifying, summarising and inferring. The processes of Analyse are differentiating, organising and attributing.

*Why not (D): comparing sounds analytical, but the revised taxonomy lists it under Understand, so statement 2 cannot be included.*

**Q18 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Cronbach and Snow called this aptitude–treatment interaction: a highly structured method may help anxious or less able learners, while able learners may do better with freedom. No method is best for all, which is the case for varying instruction.

*Why not (B): the interaction between method and learner is the cause of the varying results, so the Reason is the explanation.*

**Q19 — (C),** because at both levels learners deal with relationships and generalisations. At the understanding level the teacher explains them and learners grasp and apply them. At the reflective level learners face a problem and arrive at generalisations through their own inquiry, testing them against evidence.

*Why not (A): explanation by the teacher is the mark of the understanding level; it is what the reflective level leaves behind.*

**Q20 — (B),** because the components of probing are prompting (giving hints after no answer or a wrong answer), seeking further information, refocusing (relating the answer to another situation), redirection (putting the same question to other students) and increasing critical awareness (asking for justification).

*Why not (A): prompting is used when a student cannot answer and needs a hint; here the answer was correct and the question was passed on.*

**Q21 — (A),** because diagnosis asks, in order: who is having trouble, where exactly the errors occur and why they occur. Only when the causes are known can remedial instruction be planned. Remedy without diagnosis of causes amounts to repeating the teaching that has already failed.

*Why not (B): the cause of a difficulty cannot be sought until the specific area of difficulty has been located.*

**Q22 — (C),** because Martin Seligman showed that organisms exposed to uncontrollable failure learn that outcomes do not depend on their actions, and then fail to act even when control becomes possible. In students it shows as passivity and giving up. Graded success experiences and effort attributions help to reverse it.

*Why not (A): test anxiety is fear that interferes during testing; here the student has stopped trying altogether, in all tasks.*

**Q23 — (C),** because Guilford distinguished convergent production, which seeks the single correct answer, from divergent production, which generates many different answers and is the basis of creativity. Unusual-uses tests are scored for fluency, flexibility and originality. Conventional intelligence tests mostly measure convergent thinking.

*Why not (A): convergent production homes in on one right answer; a task that invites many answers measures divergent production.*

**Q24 — (B),** because credit points are 20 × 8.0 = 160, 20 × 7.0 = 140 and 10 × 6.0 = 60, a total of 360. Total credits are 50. CGPA = 360 ÷ 50 = 7.2. The simple average of the three SGPAs is 7.0, which gives the 10-credit semester too much weight.

*Why not (A): 7.0 is the unweighted mean of 8.0, 7.0 and 6.0; the semesters do not carry equal credits.*

**Q25 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Facts are needed as raw material for understanding and reflection, so the memory level is necessary. It is insufficient precisely because it does not develop critical thought; that capacity is the product of reflective level teaching.

*Why not (A): if memory level teaching developed critical thought, as the Reason claims, it would not be 'insufficient'.*

**Q26 — (A),** because analysing a lesson already taught is evaluation and reflection, which is post-active. Planning a lesson yet to be taught is pre-active. The same sitting therefore contains the end of one cycle and the beginning of the next, which shows how the phases feed into each other.

*Why not (D): the order matters: reflection on the lesson taught comes first (post-active), and planning of the next comes second (pre-active).*

**Q27 — (D),** because the National Academic Depository is a round-the-clock online store of academic awards lodged by institutions and boards, accessible through DigiLocker, which allows employers and institutions to verify them. The Academic Bank of Credits records the credits a student earns, for transfer and redemption.

*Why not (A): the Academic Bank of Credits holds credits earned course by course; the awards themselves are held in the National Academic Depository.*

**Q28 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. If knowledge is constructed by revising one's present ideas, then an error shows what those ideas are and where they break down. It is information for the teacher and an opportunity for the learner to restructure understanding.

*Why not (B): errors matter in constructivism precisely because learning is the revision of existing ideas; the Reason supplies that link.*

**Q29 — (A),** because Responding has three sub-levels: acquiescence in responding (compliance), willingness to respond and satisfaction in response. Doing what is required, though without enthusiasm, is acquiescence, so it already counts as Responding. Receiving would be mere attention, with no action taken.

*Why not (B): the student does more than attend; carrying out the task, even reluctantly, is the lowest form of Responding.*

**Q30 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Recitation is indeed the fifth and last step, but in Morrison's usage it means an oral presentation of the mastered unit in the pupil's own words before the class. The everyday meaning of 'recite' misleads here.

*Why not (A): the name of the step invites the rote meaning, but Morrison used it for an organised oral presentation.*

**Q31 — (C),** because orientation to learning concerns time perspective and the organisation of content: adults seek immediate application and are therefore problem-centred. Readiness to learn concerns when adults become ready, namely when the tasks of their social roles demand it.

*Why not (A): readiness is about the timing of learning in relation to life roles; the problem-centred organisation of content is orientation.*

**Q32 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. A library goes unused when teaching does not require independent reading, when hours are inconvenient or when students lack library skills. Its classification as an instructional facility is correct but explains none of this.

*Why not (A): naming the category to which a library belongs does not explain why availability and use differ.*

**Q33 — (D),** because a percentile rank locates a student among other students, so it is norm-referenced. The percentage of objectives mastered describes what the student can do against a defined domain, with no reference to others, so it is criterion-referenced. The two similar-sounding indices belong to opposite systems.

*Why not (B): 'percentage' and 'percentile' sound alike, but a percentage of objectives mastered makes no comparison with other students.*

**Q34 — (B),** because statements 1 and 2 are correct. A demonstration lets learners see the process, and a lecture conveys much information quickly. Statement 3 is wrong because in a demonstration the teacher performs and learners watch; practice needs laboratory work. Statement 4 is wrong for the same reason.

*Why not (D): watching a demonstration is not hands-on practice; statement 3 confuses demonstration with laboratory work.*

**Q35 — (D),** because content validity concerns how well the items sample the course. Concurrent and predictive validity are both criterion-related: the first compares the test with a criterion available now, the second with one obtained later. Construct validity concerns the theoretical trait behind the scores.

*Why not (A): concurrent and predictive validity differ only in timing: 'now' is concurrent and 'later' is predictive.*

**Q36 — (C),** because Crowder's frames are relatively large, each ending in a multiple-choice question; the option chosen decides the next frame, and a wrong choice leads to an explanation. Skinner's linear style is the reverse on each count: small steps, constructed (written) responses and near-errorless learning.

*Why not (A): small frames, constructed responses and minimal errors together describe Skinner's linear programme.*

**Q37 — (D),** because the Stanford model used real pupils and videotape feedback. The Indian model, developed for conditions of limited resources, uses fellow trainees as pupils (simulated conditions) and feedback from supervisors and peer observers, with no costly equipment. The cycle and the one-skill principle are retained.

*Why not (C): the Indian model keeps the full cycle, including re-teach and re-feedback; only the setting and the feedback source differ.*

**Q38 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Teaching is triadic because the concept logically needs three terms: a teacher, a content and a learner. That teaching is also organised as a profession is a fact about its social standing, not about its logical structure.

*Why not (A): professional status has no bearing on the three-term structure of the concept of teaching.*

**Q39 — (D),** because the analytic method proceeds from the unknown, the conclusion to be established, back to the known data. It shows learners why each step is taken. The synthetic method presents the finished proof from data to conclusion; it is shorter but hides how the proof was found.

*Why not (C): deduction applies a general rule to cases; working backwards from the conclusion to the data is analysis.*

**Q40 — (C),** because a test is an instrument, a set of tasks presented to the learner. Measurement is the process of assigning numbers to the performance obtained with that instrument. Evaluation interprets the numbers, together with other evidence, and judges their worth against objectives.

*Why not (A): measurement is not an instrument; it is the quantifying process that uses the instrument, so the test must come first.*

**Q41 — (B),** because Dave's psychomotor taxonomy begins with Imitation, Simpson's with Perception and Harrow's with Reflex movements. Krathwohl's taxonomy belongs to the affective domain and begins with Receiving. The three psychomotor schemes differ because each author approached motor behaviour from a different angle.

*Why not (A): Dave and Simpson are the pair most often exchanged: imitation opens Dave's scheme and perception opens Simpson's.*

**Q42 — (D),** because relative grading awards grades according to a student's position in the group, which is the norm-referenced logic. Absolute grading awards grades according to fixed, pre-set standards, which is the criterion-referenced logic. The analogy must keep this order.

*Why not (A): the pair is right but reversed; relative grading corresponds to norm-referencing, not to criterion-referencing.*

**Q43 — (B),** because a diorama represents a scene, such as a village, a battlefield or a habitat, in three dimensions on a small scale. A mock-up is a simplified working replica of a device, used for training in its operation. A cut-away model shows the inside of an object.

*Why not (A): a mock-up imitates the working of an apparatus for practice; a static miniature scene is a diorama.*

**Q44 — (A),** because in integration, the learner with a disability is placed in the regular setting and is expected to fit into a system that stays as it is. Inclusion requires the system itself to change, by removing barriers and providing accommodation, so that the learner can participate fully.

*Why not (B): inclusion means the institution adapts to the learner; mere physical placement in a regular class is integration.*

**Q45 — (C),** because the note names ten National Coordinators: NPTEL, UGC, AICTE, CEC, IGNOU, IIM Bangalore, NITTTR, INI, NCERT and NIOS. Older material lists nine, which was the number when the platform was launched. Each coordinator is responsible for courses in an assigned sector.

*Why not (B): nine was the number of coordinators at the launch of the platform; the 2026 note lists ten.*

**Q46 — (A),** because the full order is: gaining attention, informing learners of the objective, stimulating recall, presenting the stimulus, providing guidance, eliciting performance, providing feedback, assessing performance and enhancing retention and transfer. Feedback can only follow a performance, and the objective is announced before the material.

*Why not (B): the objective is stated before the stimulus material is presented, so that learners know what to look for.*

**Q47 — (D),** because statements 1 and 3 are correct. SWAYAM PRABHA is one-way television broadcast through DTH, available free on DD Free Dish, so no internet is needed. Statement 2 describes SWAYAM, the interactive MOOC platform with assessments and credit transfer, not the television channels.

*Why not (C): quizzes and credit transfer are features of SWAYAM; a television broadcast cannot assess viewers.*

**Q48 — (A),** because Vygotsky identified two levels: actual development, shown by independent problem-solving, and potential development, shown by problem-solving under guidance. The zone of proximal development is the distance between these two levels, not either of the levels themselves.

*Why not (C): the zone is the gap between the two levels; performance with help marks its upper boundary, the level of potential development.*

**Q49 — (D),** because F (Fail) and Ab (Absent) both carry 0 grade points. P (Pass) is a passing grade with 4 points and C (Average) carries 5. A course with F or Ab earns no credit points, but its credits still count in the calculation of the SGPA.

*Why not (A): P is the lowest passing grade and carries 4 points, not zero.*

**Q50 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Conservation is the chief achievement of the concrete operational stage; it is the pre-operational child who fails conservation tasks. The child conserves once he or she understands that an action, such as pouring, can be mentally reversed.

*Why not (A): the failure described belongs to the pre-operational stage; the stage names are easily confused.*

# **Set 17**

**Level:** Critical     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

**1.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:

**Assertion:** In Dave's taxonomy, articulation comes after precision.

**Reason:** Articulation means copying the action of a model exactly as it is shown.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

**2.**	Which statement correctly relates the flipped classroom to blended learning?

(A)	Every blended course is flipped, but not every flipped classroom is blended

(B)	The two terms refer to exactly the same arrangement of teaching

(C)	Every flipped classroom is blended, but not every blended course is flipped

(D)	The two terms refer to arrangements that exclude each other

**3.**	An assessment instrument lists the criteria for a presentation and provides a five-point numerical scale against each, but gives no description of what each point on the scale means. The instrument is a:

(A) analytic rubric	(B) checklist

(C) holistic rubric	(D) rating scale

**4.**	Since the validity coefficient of a test cannot exceed the square root of its reliability coefficient, a test with a reliability of 0.64 can have a validity of at most:

(A) 0.41	(B) 0.64

(C) 0.80	(D) 0.90

**5.**	Which of the following is support material, as distinct from an instructional facility?

(A)	A worksheet prepared for a particular lesson

(B)	A seminar hall with a public address system

(C)	A computer laboratory with thirty terminals

(D)	A campus-wide network with wireless access

**6.**	In outcome-based education, a course-exit survey that asks students how well they think they have achieved the course outcomes is:

(A) an indirect tool of assessment	(B) a direct tool of assessment

(C) a formative classroom test	(D) an analytic scoring rubric

**7.**	In Dale's Cone of Experience, the band lying immediately above 'contrived experiences' is:

(A) demonstrations	(B) dramatised experiences

(C) study trips	(D) direct purposeful experiences

**8.**	A student refuses to cheat, saying, 'Rules are rules; if everyone broke them, the system would collapse.' In Kohlberg's scheme this reasoning belongs to the:

(A) conventional level	(B) pre-conventional level

(C) post-conventional level	(D) heteronomous level

**9.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:

**Assertion:** A cooperative classroom climate tends to favour learning.

**Reason:** Competition among students is always harmful and has no place in any classroom.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

**10.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:

**Assertion:** Reflective level teaching cannot be carried on in an authoritarian classroom.

**Reason:** Reflection requires freedom to question accepted beliefs and to propose alternatives.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

(A)	disregard the prescribed curriculum and evaluation norms

(B)	present controversial views supported by evidence

(C)	choose the methods by which a topic is taught

(D)	criticise established theories in his or her field

(A) student portfolio	(B) anecdotal record

(B)	essay-type items of an open kind only

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

**32.**	A student of normal intelligence reads well, but her handwriting is illegible, unevenly spaced and painfully slow. The specific learning difficulty indicated is:

(A) dyslexia	(B) dysgraphia

(C) dyspraxia	(D) dyscalculia

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 11  **A** | 12  **C** | 13  **D** | 14  **B** | 15  **A** | 16  **D** | 17  **D** | 18  **C** | 19  **A** | 20  **B** |
| 21  **D** | 22  **B** | 23  **C** | 24  **A** | 25  **A** | 26  **D** | 27  **D** | 28  **B** | 29  **C** | 30  **B** |
| 31  **D** | 32  **B** | 33  **A** | 34  **B** | 35  **D** | 36  **C** | 37  **D** | 38  **A** | 39  **B** | 40  **D** |
| 41  **A** | 42  **B** | 43  **D** | 44  **C** | 45  **A** | 46  **C** | 47  **B** | 48  **C** | 49  **B** | 50  **D** |

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 17**

**Q1 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Dave's order is imitation, manipulation, precision, articulation and naturalisation. Articulation is the coordination of a series of actions into a harmonious sequence. Copying a model is imitation, the first level.

*Why not (A): the Reason defines imitation; articulation is the smooth combining of several skilled acts.*

**Q2 — (C),** because blended learning is the general idea of combining face-to-face and online learning. The flipped classroom is one model of it, in which first exposure to content is online and class time is used for application. Other blended models, such as rotation or flex, are not flipped.

*Why not (A): that option reverses the relation; flipped is the special case and blended the wider class.*

**Q3 — (D),** because a checklist records only whether a feature is present. A rating scale records the degree of each feature on a scale of numbers or words. A rubric goes further by describing, for each criterion, what performance at each level looks like. Without descriptors the instrument remains a rating scale.

*Why not (A): an analytic rubric also scores criteria separately, but its defining feature is the descriptor for every level, which is missing here.*

**Q4 — (C),** because the upper limit of validity is the index of reliability, the square root of the reliability coefficient. The square root of 0.64 is 0.80. So unreliable tests cannot be highly valid. The limit is a ceiling only; actual validity is usually much lower.

*Why not (B): 0.64 is the reliability itself; the ceiling on validity is its square root, 0.80.*

**Q5 — (A),** because support material consists of the aids and resources used in teaching a particular content: charts, models, handouts, worksheets, slides and films. Instructional facilities are the physical infrastructure the institution provides for all teaching, such as halls, laboratories, libraries and networks.

*Why not (C): a computer laboratory is infrastructure provided by the institution; material designed for one lesson is support material.*

**Q6 — (A),** because direct assessment examines actual student performance through tests, assignments and projects. Indirect assessment gathers opinions about learning from students, alumni or employers through surveys. Attainment of outcomes is usually computed mainly from direct tools, with indirect tools as supporting evidence.

*Why not (B): a survey records what students believe they have learnt; it does not examine their performance directly.*

**Q7 — (B),** because the three lowest bands of the cone are direct purposeful experiences, contrived experiences and dramatised experiences, all of which involve doing. Demonstrations come next, followed by study trips and exhibits. Direct purposeful experiences lie below contrived experiences, at the base.

*Why not (A): demonstrations are the fourth band; dramatised experiences come between contrived experiences and demonstrations.*

**Q8 — (A),** because upholding rules for the sake of social order is the 'law and order' orientation, stage four, within the conventional level. Pre-conventional reasoning would refer to punishment or personal gain. Post-conventional reasoning would appeal to principles of justice that may even override particular rules.

*Why not (C): the argument sounds principled, but it defends existing rules as such; post-conventional reasoning judges rules by higher principles.*

**Q9 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Research generally favours cooperative over competitive structures for achievement and relationships. But the Reason overstates the case: moderate, friendly competition, especially between groups or against one's own record, can motivate without harm.

*Why not (A): the absolute words 'always' and 'no place' make the Reason false, though it points in the same direction as the Assertion.*

**Q10 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. An authoritarian teacher fixes what is to be believed and discourages doubt. Since reflective thinking consists in doubting, proposing and testing, it needs an open classroom, and the freedom it needs is exactly what authoritarian control removes.

*Why not (B): the freedom mentioned in the Reason is what an authoritarian classroom denies; the connection is direct.*

**Q11 — (A),** because academic freedom protects inquiry, expression and the choice of methods within the teacher's field. It is exercised within institutional obligations: the teacher must still teach the approved curriculum, meet classes and follow evaluation rules. Freedom and accountability go together in the professional code.

*Why not (B): presenting controversial views with evidence is the core of academic freedom, so it is included, not excluded.*

**Q12 — (C),** because a cumulative record is a comprehensive, year-by-year account of a student kept by the institution for guidance. A portfolio is compiled chiefly by the student and holds selected work with reflections. An anecdotal record describes single incidents, and a sociogram charts choices within a group.

*Why not (A): a portfolio is the student's own selection of work; a record kept by the institution on many aspects is a cumulative record.*

**Q13 — (D),** because the three levels call for different evaluation. Memory level outcomes are tested by oral and written recall. Understanding level outcomes are tested by both objective-type and essay-type items that require explanation and application. Reflective level outcomes are tested mainly by essay-type tests.

*Why not (B): reliance on essay-type tests alone is characteristic of the reflective level, not of the understanding level.*

**Q14 — (B),** because statements 2, 3 and 4 are correct. Learner-centred teaching relies on continuous feedback; a clear exposition by the teacher is often the best way to open an unfamiliar topic; and no approach is best in all cases. Statement 1 is wrong: the teacher's role changes to that of designer and guide but remains essential.

*Why not (C): statement 3 is also true; teacher-centred methods have legitimate uses and must be included.*

**Q15 — (A),** because statements 1, 2 and 4 are correct. Herbart's formal steps arrange content systematically, on the principle that new ideas are assimilated to old ones, and retention is secured by recitation and drill. Statement 3 is wrong: the approach is teacher-centred.

*Why not (B): recitation and drill are standard devices of this approach, so statement 4 must be included.*

**Q16 — (D),** because the correction formula is S = R − W ÷ (n − 1), where n is the number of options. Here S = 40 − 12 ÷ (4 − 1) = 40 − 4 = 36. Omitted items are neither rewarded nor penalised. The formula assumes that wrong answers come from blind guessing.

*Why not (A): 28 subtracts all 12 wrong answers; the formula subtracts only one-third of them.*

**Q17 — (D),** because the law of readiness states that when a learner is ready to act, acting is satisfying and not acting is annoying; when not ready, being made to act is annoying. The law of effect concerns the consequences that follow a response already made.

*Why not (A): satisfaction and annoyance figure in both laws, but the law of effect concerns the aftermath of a response, not preparedness to respond.*

**Q18 — (C),** because Kelley showed that the upper and lower 27 per cent of examinees, ranked by total score, give the most efficient comparison. Twenty-seven per cent of 200 is 0.27 × 200 = 54, so each extreme group has 54 scripts and the middle 92 are set aside.

*Why not (B): 50 would be the upper and lower quarter (25 per cent); the method specifies 27 per cent.*

**Q19 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. A score of 35 tells us nothing until we know the maximum, how others performed or what standard was set. Norm-referenced and criterion-referenced interpretation are the two frames that turn a raw score into information.

*Why not (B): the need for a frame of reference is the very reason a raw score is meaningless alone.*

**Q20 — (B),** because Bandura named four sources of self-efficacy: mastery experiences (one's own past successes), vicarious experiences (seeing similar others succeed), verbal persuasion and physiological or emotional states. Actual success is the strongest because it gives direct evidence of capability.

*Why not (A): seeing others succeed does raise efficacy, but less than succeeding oneself.*

**Q21 — (D),** because moving from known to unknown follows Herbart's doctrine of apperception, that new ideas are learnt through old ones. Whole to part follows the Gestalt finding that wholes are perceived before parts. Simple to complex is gradation of difficulty. Particular to general is the path of induction.

*Why not (A): apperception is about linking new ideas to known ones; reasoning from particular cases to a rule is induction.*

**Q22 — (B),** because tutorials are of three kinds. In the supervision tutorial a tutor works with one or two students on their written work. In the group tutorial a tutor meets a small group to clear common difficulties after lectures. In the practical tutorial skills are practised in the laboratory or workshop.

*Why not (A): a group tutorial involves a larger group and common difficulties; one or two students discussing their own essay is supervision.*

**Q23 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. The four features are those Bruner set out in Toward a Theory of Instruction. Far from excluding young children, he held that children at any stage can discover the basic ideas of a subject if these are presented in a suitable mode.

*Why not (A): Statement II contradicts Bruner's best-known claim, that any subject can be taught honestly to any child.*

**Q24 — (A),** because justifying a choice with reasons means judging alternatives against criteria, which is the cognitive process Evaluate. The words 'choice' and 'justify' may suggest values, but the objective concerns a reasoned judgement about technique, not an attitude or commitment.

*Why not (C): Valuing concerns the worth a person attaches to something as a matter of attitude; here an intellectual judgement is defended with reasons.*

**Q25 — (A),** because Receiving has three sub-levels: awareness, willingness to receive and controlled or selected attention. Picking out particular stimuli from what is heard is selected attention, the highest sub-level of Receiving. Responding begins only when the learner does something in reaction.

*Why not (B): her attention is active and selective, but she has not reacted; without a response it remains Receiving.*

**Q26 — (D),** because statements 1 and 2 are correct. Statement 3 is wrong: asking questions is category 4 and belongs to indirect influence, since it invites student participation. Statement 4 is wrong: the system codes only verbal behaviour, which is one of its accepted limitations.

*Why not (C): questions come from the teacher, which tempts one to call them direct, but Flanders classed them as indirect influence.*

**Q27 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Guidance is given so that it can eventually be withdrawn; the aim of good teaching is a learner who can learn independently. The teacher's help is a means to that independence, which is why it is adjusted to changing needs.

*Why not (A): guidance and dependence are not the same; good guidance works towards making itself unnecessary.*

**Q28 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Each is one of Knowles's separate assumptions. Readiness arises when a role, as worker, parent or citizen, demands new learning. The reservoir of experience concerns the resources adults bring, not what makes them ready.

*Why not (A): two assumptions from the same list are both true, but neither is the cause of the other.*

**Q29 — (C),** because in the incident method, devised by Paul Pigors, the group receives a short statement of an incident and has to dig out the relevant facts by asking questions, then define the issue and decide. It trains fact-finding as well as analysis. In an ordinary case study the facts are supplied in full.

*Why not (A): a standard case study presents the full facts at the outset; having to elicit them by questioning marks the incident method.*

**Q30 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Evaluation is integral to teaching because the teacher must know whether objectives are being achieved in order to adjust instruction. That teaching is carried on through communication is a separate characteristic.

*Why not (A): communication is how teaching proceeds; it is not the reason why teaching must include evaluation.*

**Q31 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. The two methods share learner activity and purpose, but they differ: problem-solving is chiefly mental and ends in a solution or conclusion, while a project is carried out in a real-life setting and ends in a tangible product.

*Why not (A): a shared feature does not make two methods identical; the Reason shows similarity, not sameness.*

**Q32 — (B),** because dysgraphia is a specific difficulty with writing: letter formation, spacing, spelling and written expression. Dyslexia affects reading, which this student does well. Dyscalculia affects number work, and dyspraxia affects the planning and coordination of movement generally.

*Why not (A): dyslexia is the commonest of these difficulties and often assumed, but its core problem is reading, which is intact here.*

**Q33 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. cMOOCs were designed on connectivist lines by Siemens and Downes. If learning is the building of networks, then learners must find, create, remix and share material themselves, with the course acting as a hub, not as a fixed body of content.

*Why not (B): the learner-generated content of cMOOCs is a direct application of the connectivist view stated in the Reason.*

**Q34 — (B),** because under negative interdependence, a student can reach the goal only if others fail to reach it, which is the structure of competition. Under positive interdependence, the structure of cooperation, each succeeds only if the others succeed. With no interdependence, the individualistic structure, each person's result is unaffected by others.

*Why not (C): students' results here do depend on one another, since one student's 'A' reduces another's chance; that is negative interdependence.*

**Q35 — (D),** because Statement I is false and Statement II is true. Many classrooms with projectors continue with unchanged lecturing. A facility must be available before it can be used, but its use depends further on the teacher's skill, willingness, time and the support the institution gives.

*Why not (A): Statement I assumes that equipment produces change automatically, which Statement II correctly denies.*

**Q36 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. In the general analysis of teaching the teacher is the independent and the learner the dependent variable. In the experiment described, the method is what the investigator manipulates, so the method is the independent variable; achievement, the measured outcome, is the dependent variable.

*Why not (A): achievement is what is measured as the outcome, which makes it the dependent, not the independent, variable.*

**Q37 — (D),** because Statement I is false and Statement II is true. Knowles treated experience as the adult's richest resource. He also noted its negative side: experience produces fixed habits of mind, biases and assumptions that can close the mind to new ideas, so adults sometimes have to unlearn.

*Why not (A): the word 'never' is the trap; experience can harden into habits that resist new learning.*

**Q38 — (A),** because ePathshala, developed by NCERT's Central Institute of Educational Technology, offers textbooks, audio, video and other resources for school classes. e-PG Pathshala, despite the similar name, is a UGC initiative that provides e-content for postgraduate courses.

*Why not (B): the names differ by two letters: e-PG Pathshala is for postgraduate content, ePathshala for school textbooks.*

**Q39 — (B),** because Scriven (1967) used 'formative' for evaluation carried out while a curriculum or programme is being developed, so as to improve it. Bloom, Hastings and Madaus (1971) applied the idea to the evaluation of student learning, particularly within mastery learning.

*Why not (A): the order is the reverse: Scriven wrote about curricula, and Bloom carried the idea over to student learning.*

**Q40 — (D),** because ability enhancement courses are of two kinds. Ability Enhancement Compulsory Courses are Environmental Science and English or MIL Communication, taken by all. Skill Enhancement Courses are chosen from a pool and provide value-based or skill-based knowledge, as this course does.

*Why not (A): both are 'enhancement' courses, but the compulsory ones are fixed for all students; a skill course chosen from a pool is a Skill Enhancement Course.*

**Q41 — (A),** because achievement goal theory contrasts mastery (learning) goals, where the aim is to understand and improve, with performance goals, where the aim is to demonstrate ability relative to others. Mastery goals are linked with deeper strategies and persistence after failure.

*Why not (B): she works hard, as mastery-oriented students do, but her aim is to look able compared with others, not to understand.*

**Q42 — (B),** because instruction is the organised imparting of knowledge and can be carried out by a book, a programme or a machine. Teaching, in the strict sense, involves a person who responds to the learner as a person, with judgement, values and human interaction. All teaching includes instruction, but not all instruction is teaching.

*Why not (A): teaching in the strict sense requires an interpersonal relationship, which a program does not supply.*

**Q43 — (D),** because in a pure speed test the items are easy and scores depend on how many are reached. Odd and even halves are then almost identical for each examinee, so their correlation is close to perfect whatever the true reliability. Test–retest or parallel forms should be used for speeded tests.

*Why not (A): in a power test all examinees attempt all items, so the two halves can properly be compared; the problem arises with speed tests.*

**Q44 — (C),** because the diverger combines concrete experience with reflective observation, and the assimilator abstract conceptualisation with reflective observation. The converger combines abstract conceptualisation with active experimentation, and the accommodator concrete experience with active experimentation. The two dimensions are how experience is grasped and how it is transformed.

*Why not (A): diverger and assimilator both use reflective observation; the diverger starts from concrete experience and the assimilator from abstract ideas.*

**Q45 — (A),** because both statements are true. Memorising isolated facts has little appeal of its own, so teachers rely on marks, praise and reproof. At the reflective level the learner is absorbed in a problem felt to be his or her own, and the wish to resolve it supplies the motivation.

*Why not (C): a problem that the learner has accepted as a real difficulty is its own motive; that is why reflective motivation is intrinsic.*

**Q46 — (C),** because a terminal objective states what the learner will be able to do at the end of instruction. Enabling objectives are the component skills and knowledge needed to reach it, found by task analysis. Expressive objectives, a term of Elliot Eisner, describe an educational encounter without specifying its outcome.

*Why not (A): expressive objectives deliberately leave the outcome open; they are not stepping stones to a specified terminal behaviour.*

**Q47 — (B),** because progressive differentiation means presenting the most general and inclusive ideas first and then differentiating them in detail and specificity. Integrative reconciliation is the complementary principle of explicitly relating new ideas to earlier ones and resolving apparent contradictions between them.

*Why not (A): integrative reconciliation links and reconciles ideas across topics; moving from the general to the detailed is progressive differentiation.*

**Q48 — (C),** because the halo effect occurs when a favourable impression on one characteristic spreads to the rating of other, unrelated characteristics. A leniency error is the tendency to rate everyone high, and the error of central tendency is the tendency to place everyone near the middle.

*Why not (A): leniency would raise the marks of all students alike; here one student gains because of one impressive feature.*

**Q49 — (B),** because BY means attribution: the creator must be credited. SA means share-alike: adaptations must be released under the same licence. A bar on changes is expressed by ND (no derivatives) and a bar on commercial use by NC (non-commercial). Open licences remove the need for individual permission.

*Why not (C): a non-commercial condition would be shown by NC in the licence; BY-SA contains no such restriction.*

**Q50 — (D),** because a forum is an open discussion in which the whole audience may take part by asking questions and making comments, usually after a lecture, film, panel or symposium. In a panel discussion and a symposium the main talking is done by a few selected persons on the platform.

*Why not (A): in a panel the discussion takes place among the experts on the platform; free participation by the audience is a forum.*

# **Set 18**

**Level:** Advanced     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

**1.**	Consider the following statements:

1\.	Andragogy involves learners in diagnosing their own learning needs.

2\.	Heutagogy emphasises the development of capability and double-loop learning.

3\.	Pedagogy, andragogy and heutagogy can be seen as a continuum of increasing learner autonomy.

4\.	In heutagogy, the teacher fully controls the path of learning.

Which of the statements given above are correct?

(A) 1 and 2 only	(B) 1, 2 and 3 only

(C) 2, 3 and 4 only	(D) 1, 3 and 4 only

**2.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:

**Assertion:** Credits earned through SWAYAM courses can be counted towards a degree at the student's own university.

**Reason:** UGC regulations permit institutions to allow a specified proportion of the courses in a semester to be taken through SWAYAM.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

**3.**	An examination declares as qualified those candidates who (a) score at least 40 per cent and (b) are among the top 6 per cent of those satisfying (a). Conditions (a) and (b) are, respectively:

(A)	norm-referenced and criterion-referenced

(B)	criterion-referenced and norm-referenced

(C)	criterion-referenced in both cases

(D)	norm-referenced in both cases

**4.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:

**Assertion:** Many undergraduates can handle propositional logic and hypothetical syllogisms.

**Reason:** According to Piaget, formal operational thought is confined to concrete objects that are physically present.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

**5.**	To remember a new term, a student links it with a personal example and with related concepts she already knows. In information-processing theory this is:

(A) elaborative rehearsal	(B) maintenance rehearsal

(C) sensory registration	(D) proactive interference

**6.**	To help students remember a twelve-digit number, a teacher groups it into four sets of three digits. The device works by overcoming:

(A)	the gradual decay of long-term memory

(B)	the limited capacity of working memory

(C)	the interference of earlier learning

(D)	the brief duration of the sensory register

**7.**	Given below are two statements about the design of slides as support material:

**Statement I:** Adding interesting but irrelevant pictures to a slide improves learning.

**Statement II:** Learners understand a diagram better with printed text on screen than with spoken narration.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Statement I and Statement II are true

(B)	Both Statement I and Statement II are false

(C)	Statement I is true, but Statement II is false

(D)	Statement I is false, but Statement II is true

**8.**	Match Gagné's events of instruction in List I with the internal learning processes they support in List II:

| **List I (Event)** | **List II (Internal process)** |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Gaining attention | i. Reinforcement |
| 2. Informing learners of the objective | ii. Semantic encoding |
| 3. Providing learning guidance | iii. Reception |
| 4. Providing feedback | iv. Expectancy |

Choose the correct matching:

(A) 1-iv, 2-iii, 3-ii, 4-i	(B) 1-iii, 2-iv, 3-i, 4-ii

(C) 1-iii, 2-iv, 3-ii, 4-i	(D) 1-iii, 2-ii, 3-iv, 4-i

**9.**	A computer program presents a new concept in short units, asks a question after each and sends the learner to remedial material when the answer is wrong. This mode of computer-assisted instruction, and the style of programming it resembles, are:

(A)	tutorial mode, resembling branching programming

(B)	tutorial mode, resembling linear programming
