## **Answer Key: Set 18**

| 1  **B** | 2  **A** | 3  **B** | 4  **C** | 5  **A** | 6  **B** | 7  **B** | 8  **C** | 9  **A** | 10  **C** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 11  **A** | 12  **C** | 13  **C** | 14  **D** | 15  **B** | 16  **C** | 17  **D** | 18  **B** | 19  **A** | 20  **A** |
| 21  **D** | 22  **C** | 23  **B** | 24  **A** | 25  **B** | 26  **D** | 27  **A** | 28  **D** | 29  **D** | 30  **C** |
| 31  **D** | 32  **A** | 33  **A** | 34  **C** | 35  **A** | 36  **B** | 37  **D** | 38  **D** | 39  **B** | 40  **C** |
| 41  **D** | 42  **D** | 43  **B** | 44  **C** | 45  **C** | 46  **B** | 47  **D** | 48  **B** | 49  **A** | 50  **C** |

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 18**

**Q1 — (B),** because statements 1, 2 and 3 are correct. Knowles's process model has adults share in diagnosing needs; heutagogy aims at capability and at questioning one's own assumptions (double-loop learning); and the three are often arranged as a continuum. Statement 4 is wrong: in heutagogy the learner determines the path.

*Why not (A): the continuum described in statement 3 is the standard way of relating the three terms and must be included.*

**Q2 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. The UGC's credit framework regulations for SWAYAM enable a parent institution to accept such courses in place of its own up to a stated ceiling and to give equivalent credit. Credit transfer is thus a consequence of this regulatory permission.

*Why not (B): the regulation is the legal basis of the credit transfer, so the Reason explains the Assertion.*

**Q3 — (B),** because condition (a) is a fixed standard that any number of candidates may satisfy, so it is criterion-referenced. Condition (b) depends on rank among other candidates, so it is norm-referenced. Many selection tests combine a minimum standard with a merit-based limit in this way.

*Why not (A): the order is reversed in that option; the fixed percentage is the criterion and the top 6 per cent is the norm.*

**Q4 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Formal operations free thought from the concrete: the adolescent can reason about propositions, possibilities and hypotheses. Dependence on concrete objects is the limitation of the preceding, concrete operational stage.

*Why not (A): the Reason describes concrete operations; if it were true of formal operations, the Assertion could not hold.*

**Q5 — (A),** because maintenance rehearsal merely repeats an item to keep it in short-term memory. Elaborative rehearsal relates the item to existing knowledge, giving it meaning and many retrieval routes, and is far more effective for transfer to long-term memory. It is the 'deep processing' of Craik and Lockhart.

*Why not (B): maintenance rehearsal is plain repetition; connecting the term to examples and concepts is elaboration.*

**Q6 — (B),** because George Miller showed that short-term or working memory holds about seven items, plus or minus two. Chunking combines items into larger meaningful units, so twelve digits become four chunks, well within capacity. It is a standard aid in memory level teaching.

*Why not (C): interference explains forgetting caused by other learning; chunking deals with how much can be held at one time.*

**Q7 — (B),** because both statements are false. Mayer's coherence principle holds that extraneous material, however attractive, competes for attention and reduces learning. His modality principle holds that graphics are better accompanied by narration than by on-screen text, since eye and ear then share the load.

*Why not (C): decorative material is a form of noise; the coherence principle says it harms learning, so Statement I is not true.*

**Q8 — (C),** because Gagné paired each external event with an internal process. Gaining attention secures reception of the stimulus. Stating the objective creates expectancy. Learning guidance helps semantic encoding into long-term memory. Feedback provides reinforcement by confirming correct performance.

*Why not (A): attention concerns receiving the stimulus; knowing the objective is what sets up an expectancy of the outcome.*

**Q9 — (A),** because the tutorial mode teaches new material through dialogue with the learner. Routing the learner to remedial units according to the response is the principle of Crowder's branching programming. Drill and practice only rehearses material already taught, and simulation models a real system.

*Why not (B): the mode is right, but remedial routing after errors is branching; a linear programme has one fixed path.*

**Q10 — (C),** because Piaget regarded such talk as egocentric speech, a sign that the child cannot yet take another's viewpoint, which fades with development. Vygotsky regarded it as private speech, a tool by which the child plans and regulates action, which later turns inward to become verbal thought.

*Why not (A): the attributions are reversed in that option; the 'tool of thought' interpretation is Vygotsky's.*

**Q11 — (A),** because observing real teaching gives direct evidence of the skill, so validity rises. But a single lesson is a small sample, observers differ, and observation takes time, so reliability and practicability fall. Rubrics, trained observers and several observations are used to limit the loss.

*Why not (B): the trade-off runs the other way: performance tasks are stronger on validity and weaker on reliability.*

**Q12 — (C),** because statements 1, 2 and 4 are correct. Reflection on outcomes identifies problems that a teacher may investigate through action research; learners' feedback steers interactive decisions; and evaluation results feed the next plan, making teaching cyclic. Statement 3 is wrong: experience shortens planning but does not remove it.

*Why not (A): statement 4 is also true; it expresses the cyclic link between the post-active and pre-active phases.*

**Q13 — (C),** because the verb 'judge' gives the process Evaluate. The object of the judgement is the student's own study strategies, that is, knowledge about one's own cognition, which is metacognitive knowledge. The cell is found by reading the verb for the process and the noun phrase for the knowledge.

*Why not (A): strategies are procedures, but knowledge of one's own strategies and their suitability is self-knowledge, hence metacognitive.*

**Q14 — (D),** because the phases described, namely presentation of exemplars, testing of attainment and analysis of thinking strategies, are the syntax of Bruner's concept attainment model. Since it deals with how learners process information to form concepts, it is placed in the information-processing family.

*Why not (A): inquiry training belongs to the same family, but it starts from a puzzling event that students explain by questioning, not from examples and non-examples.*

**Q15 — (B),** because test construction begins with planning: objectives, content and blueprint. Items are then written and tried out on a sample. The try-out responses are subjected to item analysis, which shows which items to keep, revise or drop, and the final form is then assembled.

*Why not (A): item analysis needs response data, so it cannot come before the try-out.*

**Q16 — (C),** because ARCS stands for Attention, Relevance, Confidence and Satisfaction. The puzzling case gains attention and the career link establishes relevance. Tasks arranged so that learners succeed early build the expectation of success, which is Confidence. Satisfaction comes from rewards and from using what has been learnt.

*Why not (D): satisfaction concerns the feeling after achievement; arranging tasks so that success is expected builds confidence.*

**Q17 — (D),** because statements 1, 3 and 4 are correct. In problem-based learning the problem comes first and drives the learning; problems are open, with incomplete information; and the tutor guides the group's process. Statement 2 is wrong: a physical product is characteristic of the project method.

*Why not (A): the facilitating role of the tutor in statement 4 is a defining feature and must be included.*

**Q18 — (B),** because first find z: (67 − 55) ÷ 12 = 12 ÷ 12 = 1. Then T = 50 + 10 × 1 = 60. T-scores have a mean of 50 and a standard deviation of 10, which removes the negative signs and decimals of z-scores. They are used in relative grading and in combining marks.

*Why not (D): 67 is the raw score; the T-score re-expresses it on a scale with mean 50 and standard deviation 10.*

**Q19 — (A),** because the mastery test stands at the end of assimilation. Morrison's rule is that a pupil does not go on to organisation and recitation until mastery is shown; a pupil who falls short is re-taught and re-tested. Sixty per cent against a standard of eighty means more assimilation.

*Why not (B): organisation presupposes mastery; allowing the student to proceed would defeat the 'teach and test again' principle.*

**Q20 — (A),** because a lecture has a sender, an encoded message, a channel and receivers. What is largely missing is feedback, so the lecturer cannot tell whether the message has been decoded as intended. Questions, pauses and short tasks improve a lecture by restoring the feedback loop.

*Why not (B): the channel, the lecturer's voice and visuals, is present; what is missing is the return flow from the audience.*

**Q21 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Charts, models and the chalkboard remain useful because they are cheap, always available and under the teacher's direct control, and a model can be handled as no screen image can. Good teaching combines traditional and digital support.

*Why not (A): the advantages stated in the Reason are grounds for the continued relevance of traditional aids, not for their irrelevance.*

**Q22 — (C),** because the method is manipulated, so it is the independent variable; achievement is the measured outcome, the dependent variable. Motivation changes the strength of the relation between the two, which is the definition of a moderator. An intervening variable would lie on the causal path between method and achievement.

*Why not (A): an intervening variable transmits the effect of the method; motivation here only alters how strong the effect is.*

**Q23 — (B),** because the grade points are O = 10, A+ = 9, B+ = 7 and P = 4. With equal credits the SGPA is their simple mean: (10 + 9 + 7 + 4) ÷ 4 = 30 ÷ 4 = 7.5. Taking P as 5 or B+ as 8 gives a wrong total.

*Why not (C): 8.0 results from wrong grade points; with O = 10, A+ = 9, B+ = 7 and P = 4 the total is 30.*

**Q24 — (A),** because one cycle takes 6 + 6 + 12 + 6 + 6 = 36 minutes. Three hours are 180 minutes, and 180 ÷ 36 = 5 exactly. Five complete cycles can therefore be run. Taking the cycle as 30 minutes, by leaving out a feedback session, gives the wrong answer 6.

*Why not (B): 6 results from taking the cycle as 30 minutes; the full cycle, including re-feedback, takes 36.*

**Q25 — (B),** because credit points so far are 80 × 7.0 = 560. The fifth semester adds 20 × 8.5 = 170. Total credit points = 730 over 100 credits, so CGPA = 7.3. A good semester moves the CGPA only a little, because the earlier 80 credits weigh four times as much.

*Why not (D): 7.75 is the simple average of 7.0 and 8.5, which ignores the fact that 80 credits stand behind the first figure and 20 behind the second.*

**Q26 — (D),** because Das, Naglieri and Kirby, drawing on Luria's work on the brain, described intelligence as four interdependent processes: planning, attention (arousal), simultaneous processing and successive processing. The theory treats intelligence as cognitive processing and underlies the Cognitive Assessment System.

*Why not (B): that option keeps 'Planning' but replaces the two forms of processing, simultaneous and successive, which give the theory its last two letters.*

**Q27 — (A),** because both statements are true. Bloom's 1956 categories were presented as a strict cumulative hierarchy. Anderson and Krathwohl retained the order of complexity but conceded that categories overlap; for instance, some forms of Understand (explaining) are more complex than simple forms of Apply (executing).

*Why not (C): the revision kept the ordering but dropped the claim of a strict cumulative hierarchy, so Statement II is true.*

**Q28 — (D),** because synchronous components require everyone to be present at the same moment. Only the live lecture does. The forum and the recorded lecture are used at each learner's convenience, and a quiz open through the day is also asynchronous, since learners take it at different times.

*Why not (B): a quiz with a day-long window has a deadline but no common moment, so it is asynchronous.*

**Q29 — (D),** because prepared statements on different aspects, delivered one after another, make a symposium. Spontaneous exchange among experts under a moderator makes a panel discussion. The same four people can serve both purposes; the format differs according to whether they present or converse.

*Why not (A): the formats are reversed in that option; set presentations are a symposium and free exchange is a panel.*

**Q30 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. Teaching fits the communication model: the teacher encodes, the learner decodes and feedback returns. Noise is anything that distorts the message, including semantic noise (unfamiliar terms), psychological noise (anxiety, prejudice) and physical noise.

*Why not (A): the everyday meaning of 'noise' misleads; in communication theory it covers every source of distortion.*

**Q31 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Dewey's five steps move from a felt difficulty to suggested solutions (an inductive leap), then to reasoning out their implications (deduction) and to testing by observation. Reflection thus combines induction and deduction and starts from doubt, not from fixed premises.

*Why not (A): hypothesis formation, mentioned in the Reason, is an inductive step, which shows that the process is not purely deductive.*

**Q32 — (A),** because a SWAYAM course and Virtual Labs are web-based and interactive, so the learner needs an internet connection. SWAYAM PRABHA received through a DTH dish is a television broadcast and needs none, which is the reason it was created for learners without connectivity.

*Why not (D): the DTH broadcast of SWAYAM PRABHA does not use the internet at the learner's end.*

**Q33 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. A generalisation drawn from some instances claims more than those instances contain, so a further case may always contradict it. This 'inductive leap' is the logical reason why induction yields probability, whereas valid deduction yields certainty.

*Why not (B): going beyond the observed instances is exactly what makes the conclusion uncertain, so the Reason is the explanation.*

**Q34 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Vygotsky's central claim is the opposite of the Reason: every higher mental function appears first between people and only afterwards within the individual. That is why talk with teachers and peers is treated as the medium of learning.

*Why not (A): the Reason denies the social origin of thinking, which is the very basis of the Assertion.*

**Q35 — (A),** because the mean shows the general level and the standard deviation shows the spread. A standard deviation of 15 means that Class B contains both very weak and very strong students, so one lesson pitched at the average will miss many of them. Class A is relatively homogeneous.

*Why not (D): equal means hide unequal spread; the need for differentiation depends on variability, not on the average.*

**Q36 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. The policy expects every teacher to take part in professional development each year, and separately sets the four-year integrated B.Ed. as the entry qualification. One concerns in-service growth, the other pre-service preparation.

*Why not (A): pre-service qualification and in-service development are distinct provisions; one is not the ground of the other.*

**Q37 — (D),** because attainment is the proportion of students who reach the set level: 52 ÷ 80 = 0.65, or 65 per cent. Since this exceeds the target of 60 per cent, the outcome is attained. The two percentages, the pass level of 50 and the target of 60, must not be confused with the result.

*Why not (B): 52 is the number of students, not a percentage; it must be divided by the class strength of 80.*

**Q38 — (D),** because 85 and 115 lie one standard deviation below and above the mean. In a normal distribution about 34 per cent of cases fall between the mean and one standard deviation on each side, so about 68 per cent lie within one standard deviation either way. About 95 per cent lie within two.

*Why not (B): 34 per cent is the share between the mean and one standard deviation on one side only.*

**Q39 — (B),** because the Trump Plan divided student time roughly into forty per cent large-group instruction by the most competent teacher, twenty per cent small-group discussion and forty per cent independent study. Teachers work as a team and are deployed according to their strengths.

*Why not (D): small-group discussion has the smallest share, about a fifth; the two larger shares are large-group instruction and independent study.*

**Q40 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. Kolb built his cycle on Dewey, Lewin and Piaget, with reflection on experience at its centre. Honey and Mumford's reflector corresponds to reflective observation; the pragmatist corresponds to active experimentation, the testing of ideas in practice.

*Why not (A): the pragmatist tries ideas out, which matches active experimentation; reflective observation matches the reflector.*

**Q41 — (D),** because facility value = (24 + 9) ÷ (30 + 30) = 33 ÷ 60 = 0.55. Discrimination index = (24 − 9) ÷ 30 = 15 ÷ 30 = 0.50. The item is of moderate difficulty and discriminates well, so it would be retained.

*Why not (B): the facility value is right, but the discrimination index divides the difference of 15 by 30, the size of one group, not by 60.*

**Q42 — (D),** because statements 2, 3 and 4 are correct. Spacing practice over time, continuing practice beyond the first perfect recall, and early revision to counter the steep initial drop of the forgetting curve are well-established findings. Statement 1 is wrong: meaningful material is retained much better than nonsense material.

*Why not (B): overlearning is a well-supported aid to retention, so statement 3 must be included.*

**Q43 — (B),** because projected aids are those whose image is thrown on a screen by a projector: slides, filmstrips, transparencies, films and opaque projection. Flash cards, charts, flannel boards, models and bulletin boards are seen directly and are non-projected aids.

*Why not (A): flash cards are shown by hand, without a projector, so that set is mixed.*

**Q44 — (C),** because a divergent question has many acceptable answers and calls for imagination and reasoning; this one asks students to predict and justify consequences, a higher-order task. The other three are convergent: each has one correct answer, whether recalled or computed.

*Why not (D): the SGPA–CGPA question needs calculation, but it converges on a single correct answer.*

**Q45 — (C),** because the policy states that assessment should move from being summative and testing mainly rote memorisation to being regular and formative, competency-based, promoting learning and testing higher-order skills such as analysis, critical thinking and conceptual clarity. It also proposes holistic, multidimensional progress reporting.

*Why not (A): that option states the same two poles in the wrong direction; the policy moves towards formative assessment.*

**Q46 — (B),** because automatic, effortless performance of a manual skill is naturalisation in the psychomotor domain. Holding to confidentiality under pressure shows commitment to a value, which is Valuing. Designing an original questionnaire is Create. Stating the steps of a procedure is recall, that is, Remember.

*Why not (A): protecting confidentiality is a matter of commitment, not of producing something new; designing the questionnaire is the creative act.*

**Q47 — (D),** because in an interpretive exercise, introductory material is followed by several objective items that require the examinee to interpret, infer and judge. Because the facts are supplied, the items test thinking and not recall. The comprehension and data interpretation sets of this examination are of this kind.

*Why not (A): a cloze test also uses a passage, but with words deleted at intervals to measure reading comprehension, not a set of interpretive questions.*

**Q48 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Many items per sub-skill are needed so that a pattern of errors can be identified reliably for each sub-skill. That diagnostic results are not used for grading is a separate characteristic.

*Why not (A): the absence of grading is a feature of diagnostic tests, but the reason for many items is the need for reliable sub-scores.*

**Q49 — (A),** because a percentile shows standing within one's own shift. Treating the same percentile in two shifts as equal is fair only if the shifts are comparable in ability, which is secured by allotting candidates to shifts at random. Papers differ between shifts; that is why raw scores cannot be compared.

*Why not (B): if the papers were identical there would be no need to normalise; normalisation exists because the papers differ.*

**Q50 — (C),** because NAAC advises every accredited institution to establish an Internal Quality Assurance Cell as a post-accreditation measure for sustaining quality. It develops benchmarks, collects feedback from students and other stakeholders and prepares the Annual Quality Assurance Report. It is an institution-related factor in teaching quality.

*Why not (A): the Academic Council approves courses and academic regulations; continuous monitoring of quality is the task of the IQAC.*

# **Set 19**

**Level:** Advanced     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

**1.**	Under the multiple entry and exit scheme of the National Education Policy 2020, a student who leaves a four-year undergraduate programme after successfully completing two years is awarded:

(A)	an undergraduate diploma

(B)	an undergraduate certificate

(B)	Both Statement I and Statement II are false

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 19**

**Q1 — (A),** because the scheme provides a certificate after one year, a diploma after two years, a bachelor's degree after three years and a bachelor's degree with honours or research after four years. Credits earned are held in the Academic Bank of Credits, so that the student may re-enter later.

*Why not (B): the certificate is the award for exit after one year; two years lead to the diploma.*

**Q2 — (B),** because completion rate on enrolments = 1,500 ÷ 20,000 = 0.075, or 7.5 per cent. Calculated on those who actually began, it is 1,500 ÷ 6,000 = 25 per cent. The choice of base changes the figure greatly, which is why reported MOOC completion rates must be read with care.

*Why not (C): 25 per cent is the rate among those who started the course; the question asks for the rate on enrolments.*

**Q3 — (A),** because both statements are true. Understanding works on facts that must first be known, and reflection in turn works on understood relationships and principles. The understanding level thus stands between the other two, depending on the one and making the other possible.

*Why not (C): the levels form a ladder; the middle level is both dependent on the lower and preparatory to the higher.*

**Q4 — (C),** because the Act requires all government and government-aided institutions of higher education to reserve not less than five per cent of seats for persons with benchmark disabilities, and allows an upper age relaxation of five years. The figure of four per cent in the Act applies to government employment.

*Why not (B): four per cent is the reservation in government employment under the same Act, not in admissions.*

**Q5 — (C),** because the two examiners differ in severity (means) and in spread (standard deviations). If raw marks are added, the examiner with the larger spread dominates the ranking. Conversion to standard scores, such as z or T scores, puts both sets on a common scale. This is the principle of scaling or moderation.

*Why not (A): adding raw marks lets the examiner whose marks vary more decide the order of merit.*

**Q6 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Bandura showed that behaviour is acquired simply by observing a model, without any reinforcement to the observer. Reinforcement, direct or vicarious, mainly affects whether the acquired behaviour is performed, which is the distinction the Reason states.

*Why not (A): the acquisition–performance distinction is what allows learning without reinforcement, so the Reason contradicts the Assertion.*

**Q7 — (A),** because only the one item at Analyse falls in the higher-order group; Remember, Understand and Apply are lower-order. One item out of ten is 10 per cent. Counting the two Apply items as higher-order gives 30 per cent, which is the usual error.

*Why not (B): 30 per cent counts the Apply items as higher-order; Apply belongs to the lower-order group.*

**Q8 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Unrelated facts have no internal links by which one can lead to another. An acronym, rhyme or image creates such links artificially, giving the learner a cue from which the items can be recovered.

*Why not (B): the artificial organisation described in the Reason is the mechanism by which mnemonics aid retention.*

**Q9 — (B),** because John Biggs's principle of constructive alignment requires that teaching activities and assessment tasks call for the same performance as the intended outcome. Here the outcome is 'design', yet students neither practise designing nor are assessed on it, so all three components are out of line.

*Why not (D): predictive validity concerns forecasting later performance; the fault here is the mismatch among outcome, teaching and assessment.*

**Q10 — (D),** because the Academic Bank of Credits is a digital store in which credits earned from recognised institutions are deposited in a student's account, transferred and later redeemed towards a certificate, diploma or degree. It supports multiple entry and exit. Storage of awards is the function of the National Academic Depository.

*Why not (A): storing and verifying certificates is done by the National Academic Depository; the Bank deals in credits.*

**Q11 — (D),** because repeated practice of a specific skill to a standard is training. Explaining with reasons and responding to objections engages understanding, which is teaching. Presenting beliefs as beyond question is indoctrination. The three differ in how far the learner's reason is engaged.

*Why not (B): the first term is right, but the lecturer who invites objections is teaching and the preacher who forbids questions is indoctrinating.*

**Q12 — (B),** because the product shows what the group achieved, and peer ratings against agreed criteria show how the work was shared. Adjusting the group mark by a contribution factor rewards those who worked and discourages free riding. Identical marks for all ignore unequal effort.

*Why not (A): identical marks are simple, but they let non-contributors benefit from the work of others.*

**Q13 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. A conference is a large gathering on a broad theme, usually with plenary lectures and many parallel sessions in which papers are presented. Paper presentation is thus a normal part of conferences as well as of seminars.

*Why not (A): conferences are built around paper sessions; the Reason is contrary to practice.*

**Q14 — (A),** because statements 1, 3 and 4 are correct. The model begins with a problem that unsettles existing beliefs; its evaluation covers attitudes, participation and critical ability; and it works only with motivated learners. Statement 2 is wrong: all sources, including the textbook, are open to question.

*Why not (B): a reasonably high level of aspiration is a recognised condition of this level, so statement 4 must be included.*

**Q15 — (D),** because statements 1, 2 and 4 are correct, and follow from the research of Deci and Ryan. Statement 3 is wrong: extrinsic incentives are useful for tasks that are not interesting in themselves, and extrinsic reasons that the learner has accepted as his or her own work well.

*Why not (A): autonomy support is central to self-determination theory, so statement 4 must be included.*

**Q16 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. The taxonomy is arranged from simple to complex, Knowledge first and Evaluation last, and each category builds on those before it. Comprehension is a prerequisite for Application precisely because of this simple-to-complex order.

*Why not (A): the Reason reverses the direction of the hierarchy; the order runs from simple to complex.*

**Q17 — (B),** because both statements are false. Simulation is used above all for skills, decisions and attitudes that are costly or risky to practise in reality, such as flying, surgery or classroom teaching. Debriefing is the stage at which the experience is analysed and turned into learning.

*Why not (C): simulation is least needed for facts and most valuable for skills and decisions, so Statement I is not true.*

**Q18 — (A),** because statements 2, 3 and 4 are correct. Admission policy fixes class size, assessment rules shape what teachers dare to try, and the institution decides how far it supports the growth of its teachers. Statement 1 is wrong: these factors operate in every institution, public or private.

*Why not (C): assessment policy is a powerful institutional influence on teaching, so statement 3 must be included.*

**Q19 — (B),** because the formula is: reliability of full test = 2r ÷ (1 + r), where r is the half-test correlation. Here 2 × 0.60 ÷ (1 + 0.60) = 1.20 ÷ 1.60 = 0.75. The correction is needed because each half is only half as long as the test, and shorter tests are less reliable.

*Why not (A): 0.60 is the reliability of a half-length test; the full test is more reliable than either half.*

**Q20 — (B),** because Slavin found that group work improves achievement when teams work for a common goal or reward and when the team's success depends on the learning of every member, as shown by individual assessment. Without individual accountability some members leave the work to others.

*Why not (A): assessing only the group product allows free riding; individual assessment is the necessary second condition.*

**Q21 — (D),** because the focus is the central aim or frame of reference of a model. The syntax is its sequence of phases. The social system describes roles, relationships and norms. The support system lists the additional requirements, such as special materials, beyond the usual skills of a teacher.

*Why not (B): 'system' occurs in both names; the social system concerns people's roles, the support system concerns materials and facilities.*

**Q22 — (C),** because skill training with a product calls for a workshop. Successive expert presentations on aspects of a theme before a large audience make a symposium. In-depth discussion of a single paper is the function of a seminar.

*Why not (A): that option exchanges the first and the third: item-writing practice needs a workshop, and critique of a paper needs a seminar.*

**Q23 — (C),** because Shodhganga is the repository of Indian theses. ShodhShuddhi gives universities access to plagiarism-detection software. Vidwan is a database of profiles of scientists, researchers and faculty. IRINS is a web-based research information management system that institutions use to showcase their faculty's research.

*Why not (A): Vidwan and IRINS both deal with researcher profiles; Vidwan is the national expert database and IRINS the institution-level system.*

**Q24 — (B),** because equal overall means conceal opposite effects in the two groups. This is an aptitude–treatment interaction: the effect of a method depends on a characteristic of the learner. The practical lesson is to match or vary methods, not to look for one best method.

*Why not (D): equal overall means do not show absence of effect; each method helped one group and hindered the other.*

**Q25 — (A),** because statements 1 and 2 are correct. Standard-setting methods such as Angoff's rely on expert judgement of minimal competence, and a quota fixes the pass rate beforehand. Statement 3 is wrong: after effective teaching, scores on a criterion-referenced test are typically bunched at the high end.

*Why not (D): a normal spread is what norm-referenced tests aim at; mastery tests are expected to be skewed towards high scores.*

**Q26 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. In communication terms, physical noise interferes with the channel between sender and receiver, so parts of the message are lost and attention is diverted. Learning suffers because the message does not reach the learner intact.

*Why not (B): the barrier effect described in the Reason is how noise comes to reduce learning.*

**Q27 — (B),** because reflective teaching moves from a felt problem through hypotheses and the testing of them to a conclusion, which is the pattern of scientific inquiry as analysed by Dewey. The Herbartian steps order the presentation of content by the teacher and belong to the memory level.

*Why not (A): the Herbartian steps also end in generalisation and application, but they organise the teacher's presentation, not the learners' inquiry.*

**Q28 — (C),** because the argument is a syllogism: a general premise is applied to a particular case. If the premises are true and the form valid, the conclusion follows necessarily. Had the class heated several metals and then generalised, the reasoning would have been inductive and its conclusion probable.

*Why not (A): induction would run from observed cases to the general law; here the general law is the starting point.*

**Q29 — (C),** because handling concrete objects is the enactive mode, working with pictures and diagrams is the iconic mode, and using numerals and algebraic notation is the symbolic mode. The sequence applies Bruner's view that learners of any age grasp a new idea best in this order.

*Why not (A): 'concrete' means handling objects, which is action (enactive); pictures come second, as the iconic mode.*

**Q30 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Transmission is one function among several; university teaching must also develop inquiry, judgement, research orientation and values. The Reason states this wider expectation and thereby contradicts the word 'sole' in the Assertion.

*Why not (A): the Reason widens the functions of teaching; it cannot support a claim that restricts them to one.*

**Q31 — (C),** because Harrow's levels are reflex movements, basic fundamental movements, perceptual abilities, physical abilities, skilled movements and non-discursive communication. The first two are largely inborn or early developed, the next two are abilities on which skills are built, and the last two are learned.

*Why not (A): perceptual abilities come before physical abilities in Harrow's order; both precede skilled movements.*

**Q32 — (D),** because pass percentage is a product measure, and student outcomes depend on entry level, motivation, class size and facilities as well as on teaching. Comparing teachers by raw results is therefore unfair unless such factors are taken into account, or gains are measured instead of final levels.

*Why not (C): pass percentage is a product measure; the flaw is that the product has many causes besides the teacher.*

**Q33 — (D),** because Ausubel crossed two dimensions: reception versus discovery (how the content becomes available) and rote versus meaningful (how it is incorporated). The student found the solution unaided, so it is discovery, but cannot relate it to any principle, so it is rote.

*Why not (A): discovery does not guarantee meaning; a solution stumbled upon without understanding is rote.*

**Q34 — (A),** because in each sum the student has subtracted the smaller digit from the larger in every column (8 − 2, 5 − 3; 9 − 1, 7 − 4; 7 − 0, 6 − 2), whichever number it belongs to. A consistent pattern shows a misconception about borrowing, not carelessness. The remedy is to re-teach regrouping.

*Why not (B): careless errors are irregular; the same wrong procedure in all three sums shows a systematic misconception.*

**Q35 — (D),** because Messick named two main threats to validity. Construct under-representation occurs when a test is too narrow and leaves out important parts of what it should measure, as here. Construct-irrelevant variance occurs when scores are affected by something outside the construct, such as reading speed.

*Why not (A): irrelevant variance means that something extra is being measured; here something essential is missing from the test.*

**Q36 — (A),** because addition and averaging are meaningful only when the units are equal, which is the property of an interval scale. Letter grades are strictly ordinal, since they only rank performance. Treating their grade points as equal steps is a convention, and the SGPA rests on that assumption.

*Why not (C): an ordinal scale gives order only; averaging also requires that the steps between grades be equal.*

**Q37 — (D),** because self-learning material is designed to be self-explanatory, self-contained, self-directed, self-motivating and self-evaluating, since the teacher is not present. Check-your-progress questions with model answers let the distance learner assess his or her own understanding, which is the self-evaluating feature.

*Why not (A): self-contained means that the unit needs no outside sources; checking one's own progress is the self-evaluating feature.*

**Q38 — (C),** because alternative text is read aloud by screen-reader software, so a learner who cannot see the image learns what it shows. Captions put spoken content into text for learners who cannot hear it. Both are basic requirements of accessible digital content.

*Why not (A): the order is reversed in that option: alternative text helps those who cannot see, and captions those who cannot hear.*

**Q39 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Learning time is extended because online activities can be done at any hour, outside the timetable. That the flipped classroom is one model of blended learning is a correct classification, not the cause.

*Why not (A): naming a type of blended learning does not explain why blended learning extends learning time.*

**Q40 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Identity formation is the subject of Erikson's and Marcia's work and extends into the early twenties. Kohlberg's stages concern moral judgement, a different aspect of development.

*Why not (A): a true statement about moral development does not explain a fact about identity formation.*

**Q41 — (A),** because points given at once after a correct response are immediate positive reinforcement, as Skinner recommended for programmed learning. A bonus that arrives after an unpredictable number of responses is a variable-ratio schedule, which produces persistent responding. The design combines the two.

*Why not (B): nothing unpleasant is removed and no fixed time interval is involved; the rewards follow responses, some of them unpredictably.*

**Q42 — (D),** because teacher talk is categories 1 to 7: 150 + 300 = 450 tallies. The total of all categories is 150 + 300 + 120 + 30 = 600. Teacher talk = 450 ÷ 600 = 75 per cent. Student talk is 20 per cent and silence 5 per cent.

*Why not (C): 50 per cent counts only the direct categories 5 to 7; indirect talk is also teacher talk.*

**Q43 — (B),** because the policy calls for pedagogy that is experiential, holistic, integrated, inquiry-driven, discovery-oriented, learner-centred, discussion-based and flexible, with less emphasis on rote learning. It links this with formative assessment and with freedom for faculty to design their own approaches within the approved framework.

*Why not (A): that option describes the pattern the policy seeks to move away from.*

**Q44 — (B),** because Dale's cone was a visual model of how abstract different experiences are; it carried no numbers, and Dale warned against taking it too literally. The neat percentages were added by others and have no known experimental source. The cone itself remains a useful classification.

*Why not (A): Dale reported no such experiments; the figures were attached to his diagram by later writers.*

**Q45 — (A),** because the policy proposes the National Educational Technology Forum to advise institutions and governments on the induction, deployment and use of technology for learning, assessment, planning and administration. The National Research Foundation is meant to fund and promote research.

*Why not (B): the National Research Foundation concerns research funding; technology in education is the remit of the Forum.*

**Q46 — (C),** because the seven principles are: student–faculty contact, cooperation among students, active learning, prompt feedback, time on task, high expectations and respect for diverse talents and ways of learning. Public ranking runs against the principle of cooperation among students.

*Why not (D): high expectations are one of the seven principles; the question asks for the practice that is not on the list.*

**Q47 — (A),** because habitual conduct governed by a value in all situations is Characterisation, the highest affective level. Because it concerns consistent behaviour over time, it can be judged only from conduct observed over a long period. A questionnaire or a written test shows what a person says, not what the person does.

*Why not (B): the level is right, but one questionnaire records professed attitudes at one moment and can be answered to please.*

**Q48 — (D),** because Statement I is false and Statement II is true. Mental age levels off in the late teens while chronological age keeps increasing, so the ratio IQ would fall absurdly in adults. Wechsler therefore introduced the deviation IQ, with a mean of 100 and a standard deviation of 15 for each age group.

*Why not (A): the ratio IQ breaks down for adults, which is the very reason the deviation IQ replaced it.*

**Q49 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. Induction builds a rule from cases. Deduction, applying an established rule to cases, is equally necessary: it is used for practice, verification and problem-solving. Good teaching usually combines the two, induction first and deduction after.

*Why not (A): deduction is the method by which rules, once established, are applied and tested; it is a standard classroom procedure.*

**Q50 — (B),** because gift (or honorary) authorship is the listing of a person who has made no substantial contribution. Ghost authorship is the opposite fault, omitting a person who did contribute. Both breach publication ethics. Authorship should reflect actual intellectual contribution and carries responsibility for the work.

*Why not (A): in ghost authorship a real contributor is left out; here a non-contributor has been put in.*

# **Set 20**

**Level:** Advanced     |     **Questions:** 50     |     **Suggested time:** 60 minutes

**1.**	Students usually remember best the points made at the beginning and at the end of a lecture, and worst those in the middle. This is the:

(A) von Restorff effect	(B) Zeigarnik effect

(C) Hawthorne effect	(D) serial position effect

**2.**	Two students both solve, unaided, problems at the eight-year level. With help, the first solves problems up to the nine-year level and the second up to the twelve-year level. Following Vygotsky, the teacher should conclude that:

(A)	the two students are at the same level and need the same instruction

(B)	the first student has the wider zone and needs more advanced instruction

(C)	assisted performance is irrelevant to the planning of instruction

(D)	the second student has the wider zone and needs more advanced instruction

**3.**	Match the teaching skills in List I with their main purposes in List II:

| **List I (Skill)** | **List II (Purpose)** |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Probing | i. Consolidating what has been learnt |
| 2. Illustrating with examples | ii. Sustaining the attention of the class |
| 3. Stimulus variation | iii. Deepening a student's response |
| 4. Closure | iv. Clarifying an abstract idea |

Choose the correct matching:

(A) 1-iii, 2-iv, 3-ii, 4-i	(B) 1-iv, 2-iii, 3-ii, 4-i

(C) 1-iii, 2-iv, 3-i, 4-ii	(D) 1-ii, 2-iv, 3-iii, 4-i

**4.**	The multiple-choice format of a high-stakes test leads teachers to drill answering techniques and to neglect extended writing. This influence of testing on teaching is called:

(A) positive washback	(B) negative washback

(C) practice effect	(D) halo effect

**5.**	Given below are two statements about educational measurement:

**Statement I:** A score on an achievement test is an indirect measure, from which learning is inferred.

**Statement II:** Because educational measurement is indirect, it always contains some error.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Statement I and Statement II are true

(B)	Both Statement I and Statement II are false

(C)	Statement I is true, but Statement II is false

(D)	Statement I is false, but Statement II is true

**6.**	In a semester of four courses of equal credit under the CBCS, a student has grades A (8), B (6) and C (5) in three courses. For an SGPA of 7.0, the grade needed in the fourth course is:

(A) B+ (7 points)	(B) A (8 points)

(C) A+ (9 points)	(D) O (10 points)

1\.	Only Indian citizens below twenty-five years of age may enrol.

4\.	It is counted among the pillars of the PM e-Vidya initiative.

Which of the statements given above are correct?

(A) 1, 2 and 3 only	(B) 2, 3 and 4 only

(C) 2 and 3 only	(D) 1 and 4 only

(A) action research	(B) historical research

(C) ex post facto research	(D) pure experimental research

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

Choose the correct matching:

(A) 1-i, 2-iv, 3-ii, 4-iii	(B) 1-iii, 2-ii, 3-iv, 4-i

(C) 1-iii, 2-iv, 3-ii, 4-i	(D) 1-iv, 2-iii, 3-ii, 4-i

**36.**	Research on feedback for formative work suggests that learning improves most when students are given:

(A) grades together with comments	(B) grades without comments

(C) ranks within the class	(D) comments without grades

**37.**	Students can repeat 'opportunity cost is the next best alternative forgone', but fall silent when asked the opportunity cost of attending today's class. To bring them to the understanding level, the teacher should next:

(A)	have them repeat the definition until it is fluent

(B)	assign an open research problem on theories of cost

(C)	have them identify opportunity costs in familiar choices

(D)	give a written test on the wording of the definition

**38.**	The online tool that most nearly reproduces the extended, reasoned discussion of a seminar without requiring participants to be present at the same time is:

(A) a live webinar	(B) a podcast series

(C) a threaded discussion forum	(D) a broadcast e-mail

**39.**	A four-option item was answered by 50 examinees: 20 chose the key A, 2 chose B, 25 chose C and 3 chose D. Taking a distractor to be non-functional if fewer than 5 per cent choose it, the non-functional distractors are:

(A) two, namely B and D	(B) one, namely B

(C) one, namely C	(D) none of the three

**40.**	Consider the following statements, which reflect later evaluations of Piaget's theory:

1\.	Piaget underestimated the abilities of young children.

2\.	Piaget gave less weight to social and cultural influences than Vygotsky did.

3\.	The ages at which the stages are reached are fixed and universal.

4\.	Not all adults use formal operations in all fields.

Which of the statements given above are correct?

(A) 1 and 2 only	(B) 2, 3 and 4 only

(C) 1, 3 and 4 only	(D) 1, 2 and 4 only

**41.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:

**Assertion:** A flannel board allows a display to be built up step by step.

**Reason:** A flannel board is a non-projected visual aid.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

**42.**	The term 'emerging adulthood', for the period from about eighteen to twenty-five marked by identity exploration, instability and a sense of being 'in between', was proposed by:

(A) Jeffrey Arnett	(B) Erik Erikson

(C) James Marcia	(D) Daniel Levinson

**43.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:

**Assertion:** Kolb's learning styles are fixed traits that never change with experience.

**Reason:** Kolb derived the four styles from preferences for particular stages of the experiential learning cycle.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

**44.**	The same test shows a reliability coefficient of 0.90 in a group of mixed ability and 0.60 in a group consisting only of top students. The most likely reason is that:

(A)	restriction of range lowers the variance of scores

(B)	the test becomes harder for the top students

(C)	top students guess more often than the others

(D)	the scoring key differs for the two groups

**45.**	In a class, the correlation between attendance and examination marks is found to be +0.60. The sound interpretation is that:

(A)	the two are positively related, but causation is not established

(B)	attendance is the cause of sixty per cent of the marks

(C)	sixty per cent of the students attend classes regularly

(D)	attendance and marks are unrelated to each other

**46.**	A teacher has to show 100 students how the valves of the heart open and close in sequence. The most suitable support is:

(A)	a preserved specimen of a heart

(B)	a set of flash cards of the heart

(C)	a wall chart of the heart in section

(D)	an animation shown through a projector

**47.**	Given below are an Assertion and a Reason:

**Assertion:** Evaluation at the understanding level uses essay-type as well as objective-type tests.

**Reason:** Teaching at the understanding level is more thoughtful than teaching at the memory level.

Choose the correct option:

(A)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, and Reason is the correct explanation of Assertion

(B)	Both Assertion and Reason are true, but Reason is not the correct explanation of Assertion

(C)	Assertion is true, but Reason is false

(D)	Assertion is false, but Reason is true

**48.**	Objective X is that 180 students should gain an overview of the structure of a policy document in one period. Objective Y is that 20 students should acquire the skill of titration. The suitable methods are, respectively:

(A)	lecture, and demonstration followed by laboratory practice

(B)	lecture, and demonstration without any practice

(C)	laboratory practice, and lecture with slides

(D)	project work, and lecture with slides

**49.**	A teacher (i) prepares a set of diagnostic questions at home, (ii) puts them to the class at the start of the lesson to gauge its level, and (iii) later analyses the unit-test scripts to revise her notes. These activities belong, respectively, to the:

(A)	interactive, interactive and post-active phases

(B)	pre-active, post-active and interactive phases

(C)	pre-active, interactive and interactive phases

(D)	pre-active, interactive and post-active phases

**50.**	The highest levels in Dave's and in Simpson's taxonomies of the psychomotor domain are, respectively:

(A) origination and naturalisation	(B) naturalisation and origination

(C) articulation and adaptation	(D) naturalisation and adaptation

## **Answer Key: Set 20**

| 1  **D** | 2  **D** | 3  **A** | 4  **B** | 5  **A** | 6  **C** | 7  **B** | 8  **A** | 9  **C** | 10  **D** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 11  **D** | 12  **B** | 13  **A** | 14  **D** | 15  **B** | 16  **B** | 17  **C** | 18  **C** | 19  **A** | 20  **D** |
| 21  **B** | 22  **C** | 23  **C** | 24  **A** | 25  **B** | 26  **A** | 27  **C** | 28  **C** | 29  **D** | 30  **C** |
| 31  **B** | 32  **D** | 33  **C** | 34  **A** | 35  **C** | 36  **D** | 37  **C** | 38  **C** | 39  **B** | 40  **D** |
| 41  **B** | 42  **A** | 43  **D** | 44  **A** | 45  **A** | 46  **D** | 47  **B** | 48  **A** | 49  **D** | 50  **B** |

## **Answers and Explanations: Set 20**

**Q1 — (D),** because the serial position effect combines primacy (better recall of first items) and recency (better recall of last items). Its lesson for teaching is to place key points at the start and end and to break up the middle. The von Restorff effect is better recall of an item that stands out.

*Why not (A): the von Restorff effect concerns an item that is distinctive, whatever its position; here position alone decides recall.*

**Q2 — (D),** because this is Vygotsky's own illustration. The level of actual development is the same, but the zones of proximal development are one year and four years. Since instruction should be aimed at the zone, the two need different teaching, and unaided tests alone would have hidden the difference.

*Why not (A): equal unaided performance conceals unequal potential; Vygotsky's point is that the zones, not the present levels, guide instruction.*

**Q3 — (A),** because probing leads a student from a partial or shallow answer to a fuller one. Examples make an abstract idea clear by connecting it with the familiar. Stimulus variation keeps attention alive. Closure draws the main points together at the end so that learning is consolidated.

*Why not (B): probing works on a student's answer; clarifying the idea itself, for the whole class, is the work of examples.*

**Q4 — (B),** because washback, or backwash, is the effect of a test on teaching and learning. It is negative when the test narrows the curriculum and positive when it encourages desirable learning. It is treated as part of the consequences of testing that a full account of validity must consider.

*Why not (D): the practice effect is a gain in score from having taken a test before; it does not describe changes in what teachers teach.*

**Q5 — (A),** because both statements are true. Learning cannot be observed directly; it is inferred from performance on a sample of tasks. Sampling of content, the examinee's condition and scoring all introduce error, so every obtained score is an estimate. Hence the importance of reliability and of the standard error of measurement.

*Why not (C): no educational measurement is free of error; that is why obtained and true scores are distinguished.*

**Q6 — (C),** because with equal credits the SGPA is the mean of the four grade points. An SGPA of 7.0 needs a total of 4 × 7 = 28 points. The three known grades give 8 + 6 + 5 = 19, so the fourth must supply 28 − 19 = 9 points, which is A+.

*Why not (A): a grade equal to the target average is not enough, because the three earlier grades average below 7.*

**Q7 — (B),** because statements 2, 3 and 4 are correct. SWAYAM covers school, undergraduate, postgraduate and other learners; certificates require a proctored examination; and the Ministry of Education lists SWAYAM among the components of PM e-Vidya. Statement 1 is wrong: enrolment is open, without an age bar.

*Why not (C): its place within PM e-Vidya is correctly stated, so statement 4 must be included.*

**Q8 — (A),** because the spiral of plan, act, observe and reflect is the cycle of action research, associated with Kurt Lewin. A project of this kind unites the project method with a research approach aimed at improving practice in one's own setting. Ex post facto research studies effects after the event, without intervention.

*Why not (D): an experiment also intervenes, but it requires control groups and aims at generalisation, not at improving a local situation through repeated cycles.*

**Q9 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Because behavioural objectives specify observable performance by the learner, a test item can be written directly from each one. They describe what the learner will do; statements of the teacher's activities are not objectives at all.

*Why not (A): it is the learner's observable behaviour, not the teacher's activity, that makes such objectives easy to evaluate.*

**Q10 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. The system takes over routine tasks, which frees the teacher for designing learning, giving qualitative feedback, guiding discussion and supporting individuals. These functions need professional judgement and cannot be automated by the platform.

*Why not (A): automation of routine tasks changes the teacher's work; it does not replace the teacher.*

**Q11 — (D),** because with four options, the chance of guessing an item correctly is 1 in 4. Over 50 items the expected number correct is 50 × 1/4 = 12.5. Scores near this level therefore show no knowledge at all, which is why the chance score must be kept in mind in interpreting low scores.

*Why not (C): 10 would be the chance score with five options per item.*

**Q12 — (B),** because connectivism, proposed by George Siemens and Stephen Downes as a learning theory for the digital age, sees learning as the creation and navigation of networks of people, information sources and devices. Knowing where to find knowledge matters more than holding it. It underlies the first MOOCs.

*Why not (A): constructivism places knowledge in the learner's own constructions; the idea that learning resides in networks and devices is connectivist.*

**Q13 — (A),** because statements 1, 3 and 4 are correct. Teaching is intentional, is directed at someone and, as Scheffler argued, submits its reasons to the learner's own judgement, which separates it from indoctrination. Statement 2 is wrong: only in the achievement sense does teaching imply learning.

*Why not (B): the normative point in statement 4 is what distinguishes teaching from indoctrination, so it must be included.*

**Q14 — (D),** because weighted score = 0.40 × 4 + 0.30 × 2 + 0.30 × 2 = 1.6 + 0.6 + 0.6 = 2.8 out of 4. As a percentage, 2.8 ÷ 4 = 70 per cent. The unweighted mean of 4, 2 and 2 is 2.67, or about 67 per cent, which ignores the heavier weight of the first criterion.

*Why not (C): 67 per cent is the unweighted average of the three scores; the first criterion carries a heavier weight.*

**Q15 — (B),** because Vroom's formula is force = expectancy × instrumentality × valence. Here 0.9 × 0.5 × 0 = 0. Since the terms are multiplied, a zero anywhere reduces motivation to zero: however sure the path, nobody strives for an outcome that has no value for them.

*Why not (C): a zero term cannot be dropped from a product; it makes the whole product zero.*

**Q16 — (B),** because Gagné held that a rule is a relation among concepts and can be learnt only if those concepts, here length, breadth and multiplication, are already mastered. Task analysis works downward from the target skill to its prerequisites. Novel problems lie above the rule in the hierarchy, not below it.

*Why not (A): problem solving is the level above rule learning; failure at a rule sends the teacher down to its prerequisites.*

**Q17 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. A hypothesis is a provisional answer adopted for testing. When evidence refutes it, learners have eliminated one possibility, sharpened the problem and practised reasoning from evidence, all of which are aims of reflective teaching.

*Why not (A): a refuted hypothesis narrows the field and trains respect for evidence; it is far from valueless.*

**Q18 — (C),** because the Assertion is true and the Reason is false. Self-pacing is one of the basic principles of programmed instruction: each learner works through the frames alone, quickly or slowly as needed. A pace fixed for the whole class is the feature of conventional group teaching.

*Why not (A): the Reason contradicts the principle of self-pacing stated in the Assertion.*

**Q19 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Achievement depends on intelligence, motivation and background as well as on method. Randomisation spreads such characteristics evenly, on average, across the groups, so that a difference in outcome can be attributed to the method.

*Why not (B): equalising extraneous variables is the purpose of random assignment, so the Reason explains why it is desirable.*

**Q20 — (D),** because immediacy, a concept from Mehrabian, refers to verbal and non-verbal behaviours that reduce the psychological distance between people. Teacher immediacy is consistently associated with students' liking for the course and motivation. Clarity concerns how understandable the explanation is, and credibility how competent and trustworthy the teacher appears.

*Why not (A): clarity is about the intelligibility of explanations; behaviours that signal warmth and closeness are immediacy.*

**Q21 — (B),** because feedback changes behaviour when it is specific, descriptive and tied to the skill practised, and when it points to a concrete next step. Counting the kinds of questions and suggesting an addition does all of this. General praise or blame gives the trainee nothing to act upon.

*Why not (A): praise is pleasant, but 'quite good' tells the trainee nothing about the questioning skill or how to improve it.*

**Q22 — (C),** because Statement I is true and Statement II is false. A student who teaches another has to organise and explain the material, which deepens his or her own understanding. Tutorials supplement lectures by dealing with individual difficulties; they are not designed to carry the whole course.

*Why not (A): tutorials are a supplement to large-group teaching, not a substitute for it.*

**Q23 — (C),** because before: credit points = 4 × (9 + 8 + 8 + 6 + 0) = 124, and 124 ÷ 20 = 6.2. After: 4 × (9 + 8 + 8 + 6 + 5) = 144, and 144 ÷ 20 = 7.2. The rise is 7.2 − 6.2 = 1.0. Equivalently, 20 extra credit points over 20 credits add 1.0.

*Why not (D): 1.25 divides the 5 grade points by four courses; the semester has five.*

**Q24 — (A),** because both statements are true. Maslow spoke of relative satisfaction: a person may be partly satisfied at several levels at once. McClelland's theory is called the acquired-needs theory because needs for achievement, affiliation and power are learnt, which implies that achievement motivation can be developed through training.

*Why not (C): McClelland's needs are learned, not inborn; that is the basis of his achievement-motivation training.*

**Q25 — (B),** because content mastery, knowledge of learners and communication skill are basic requirements of teaching. A belief that ability is fixed is not; it lowers expectations and works against the learner, as the research on teacher expectations and on mindsets has shown.

*Why not (A): communication skill is as basic as the other two; teaching is carried on through communication.*

**Q26 — (A),** because the framework, issued by the UGC, sets levels from 4.5 to 8 for higher education, each described in terms of learning outcomes: 4.5 for the undergraduate certificate, 5 for the diploma, 5.5 for the bachelor's degree, 6 for the honours degree, 6.5 for the master's degree and 8 for the doctorate.

*Why not (C): 10 is the top level of frameworks that use a ten-level scale; the higher education framework ends at level 8.*

**Q27 — (C),** because Michael Moore held that the distance that matters is psychological and depends on three variables: dialogue, structure and learner autonomy. Rigid structure with little interaction gives the greatest transactional distance and demands the most autonomy from the learner. More dialogue reduces the distance.

*Why not (A): low structure with much dialogue is the condition of least transactional distance, the opposite case.*

**Q28 — (C),** because Daniel Stufflebeam's model evaluates a programme at four points: context (needs and goals), input (resources and plans), process (implementation) and product (outcomes). It is decision-oriented and is widely used for evaluating educational programmes and institutions.

*Why not (D): that option keeps Input and Product but replaces Context and Process, which are the other two parts of the name.*

**Q29 — (D),** because a television broadcast reaches a large, scattered and anonymous audience through a mass medium, and the viewers cannot respond through the same channel. That makes it one-way mass communication. Interaction has to be provided separately, for example through the web portal or SWAYAM courses.

*Why not (B): group communication involves members who interact with one another; television viewers are a dispersed mass audience.*

**Q30 — (C),** because portfolios are strong in authenticity and in showing growth, but different assessors can judge the same portfolio very differently. Clear rubrics, training of raters, double marking and moderation meetings are used to raise inter-rater reliability.

*Why not (A): authenticity is the strength of portfolios, not their weakness; the weakness is consistency of scoring.*

**Q31 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. The many-sided view comes from having several speakers, each dealing with a different aspect of the theme. The question session is a customary feature, but it is not what produces the breadth of treatment.

*Why not (A): breadth comes from the plurality of speakers and aspects, not from the question session.*

**Q32 — (D),** because Statement I is false and Statement II is true. What marks indoctrination is the manner and aim: beliefs are implanted so that they will be held regardless of evidence. Any content, religious, political or even scientific, can be taught with reasons or indoctrinated without them.

*Why not (A): if content alone decided the matter, a scientific truth could never be indoctrinated; in fact it can be, when reasons are withheld.*

**Q33 — (C),** because 'define' asks for recall (Remember). 'Explain why' asks for a causal account (Understand). 'Compute' uses a known procedure on given data (Apply). 'Design' requires producing something new (Create). The ascending order is therefore define, explain, compute, design.

*Why not (A): computing looks simpler than explaining, but carrying out a procedure is Apply, one level above Understand.*

**Q34 — (A),** because both are true and the Reason explains the Assertion. Cognitive load theory holds that working memory can handle only a few elements at once. Each aid makes demands on it; when aids multiply, the load exceeds capacity and the point of the lesson is lost among the displays.

*Why not (B): overload of working memory is the mechanism by which too many aids harm learning.*

**Q35 — (C),** because Knowles set out the assumptions of andragogy about the self-directing adult. Mezirow described perspective transformation through critical reflection. Paulo Freire criticised 'banking' education, in which knowledge is deposited in passive learners, and proposed dialogue. Hase and Kenyon introduced heutagogy, or self-determined learning.

*Why not (A): self-direction (Knowles) and self-determination (Hase and Kenyon) are easily exchanged; heutagogy goes a step beyond andragogy.*

**Q36 — (D),** because studies such as Ruth Butler's found that students who received only comments improved more than those given grades, and that adding a grade to the comments cancelled the benefit, because students attended to the grade and ignored the advice. Black and Wiliam drew on this in recommending comment-only marking.

*Why not (A): two kinds of information seem better than one, but the grade draws attention away from the comments.*

**Q37 — (C),** because the students hold the words without the meaning. Understanding comes from seeing the concept at work in cases they know and expressing it in their own terms. More repetition keeps them at the memory level, and an open research problem demands reflection before understanding exists.

*Why not (B): a research problem belongs to the reflective level, which presupposes the understanding these students still lack.*

**Q38 — (C),** because in a threaded forum, a paper or prompt can be posted and participants reply to it and to one another over several days, so arguments are developed at length. A webinar allows discussion but only synchronously. Podcasts and broadcast e-mail are one-way.

*Why not (A): a webinar is the online seminar in real time; the question asks for a tool that does not need simultaneous presence.*

**Q39 — (B),** because five per cent of 50 is 2.5 examinees. B, chosen by 2, falls below this and is non-functional. D, chosen by 3, is just above it. C, chosen by 25, more than the key, is over-attractive and needs checking for ambiguity, but it is certainly functioning.

*Why not (A): D was chosen by 3 examinees, or 6 per cent, which is above the 5 per cent threshold.*

**Q40 — (D),** because statements 1, 2 and 4 are correct. Later studies with simpler tasks showed competence earlier than Piaget reported; his theory centres on the individual child; and formal reasoning is often limited to familiar domains. Statement 3 is wrong: ages vary with culture, schooling and experience.

*Why not (A): the limited reach of formal operations in adults is a well-known finding, so statement 4 must be included.*

**Q41 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. The display can be built up because cut-outs backed with rough material stick to the flannel and can be added, moved or removed at will. Being non-projected is a classification of the aid, not the source of this advantage.

*Why not (A): many non-projected aids, such as printed charts, cannot be built up; the advantage comes from the adhesive cut-outs.*

**Q42 — (A),** because Arnett argued in 2000 that in societies where marriage and settled work come late, the years from eighteen to twenty-five form a distinct stage, neither adolescence nor full adulthood. Most university students fall in this period. Erikson and Marcia described identity formation, and Levinson the 'seasons' of adult life.

*Why not (B): Erikson described the identity crisis of adolescence, but the separate stage of 'emerging adulthood' is Arnett's proposal.*

**Q43 — (D),** because the Assertion is false and the Reason is true. Kolb described styles as relatively stable preferences, shaped by personality, education and career, which can alter over time; he held that mature learners integrate all four modes. The styles do arise from the two dimensions of his cycle.

*Why not (A): that the styles come from the cycle does not make them unchangeable; Kolb expected them to develop.*

**Q44 — (A),** because reliability coefficients depend on the spread of scores in the group tested. In a homogeneous group, true differences among examinees are small, so error forms a larger share of the variance and the coefficient falls. The test has not changed; the group has.

*Why not (B): the items are the same for both groups; what differs is how widely the scores are spread.*

**Q45 — (A),** because a coefficient of +0.60 shows a moderately strong tendency for higher attendance to go with higher marks. It does not show that one causes the other: motivated students may both attend more and study more. The shared variance is the square of the coefficient, 36 per cent, not 60.

*Why not (B): a correlation coefficient is not a percentage of causation; even the shared variance is 0.36, not 0.60.*

**Q46 — (D),** because the objective concerns movement over time, which only a moving image can show, and the class is large, which calls for projection. A specimen is real but static and too small for a hundred students; a chart and flash cards are also static.

*Why not (A): a specimen is authentic, but it cannot show motion and cannot be seen by a hundred students at once.*

**Q47 — (B),** because both statements are true, but the Reason does not explain the Assertion. Both kinds of test are used because understanding has to be shown through explanation and application as well as recognition. The comparison with the memory level is correct but gives no account of the choice of tests.

*Why not (A): a general comparison of the two levels does not explain why two forms of test are used at one of them.*

**Q48 — (A),** because an overview for a large group in limited time is the classic use of the lecture. A manipulative skill has first to be shown and then practised by each learner with feedback, so demonstration must be followed by laboratory work. Method follows objective, class size and time.

*Why not (B): watching a demonstration shows what to do, but a skill is acquired only by doing it oneself.*

**Q49 — (D),** because preparing questions before the class is planning (pre-active). Asking them in class to size up the learners is diagnosis within the interactive phase. Analysing test scripts and revising material afterwards is evaluation and reflection (post-active). The phase is decided by when and why an act is done.

*Why not (A): the questions are diagnostic in both (i) and (ii), but preparing them at home is a planning act, not classroom interaction.*

**Q50 — (B),** because Dave's five levels end with naturalisation, in which the skill has become automatic. Simpson's seven levels end with origination, in which the performer creates new movement patterns. Articulation and adaptation are the levels just below the top in the two schemes.

*Why not (A): the two terms are right but attached to the wrong authors; naturalisation is Dave's and origination is Simpson's.*
